01 — Part 0 · The Operating System
read this first · the ideas everything else stands on

Before the reps: install Dan's operating system.

Every course, video, and repo in this dojo runs on a small set of invariants. Learn them once, and the other twenty-seven chapters become variations on a theme.

the core fourthe lawsthe KPIsthe map
In this chapter 1The Evolution 2The Laws 3How You Keep Score 4The Map of the Dojo
1

The Evolution: from prompt to fleet

Dan's whole body of work is one idea, compounding.

It starts with the Big Three — context, model, prompt — the levers of AI coding. Agentic coding adds the fourth: tools, and reliably long chains of tool calls. That is the Core Four, and it is his anti-hype instrument: every feature, framework, skill, MCP server, and sub-agent you will ever meet is "just" context, model, prompt, and tools with ergonomics.

Then the unit of work climbs. First "the prompt is the fundamental unit of engineering." Then, as models learn to prompt-engineer their own sub-agents: "if you master the agent, you master knowledge work." The endgame is agents managing agents — and you, out of the loop, orchestrating.

The compositional unit keeps climbing. Master each rung before the next; the rungs never go away.

Focus on principles, not tools, not models. Principles never change.

— IndyDevDan, the line under everything
2

The Laws: twelve invariants of the house style

You will meet each of these a dozen times across the volumes. Treat them as laws of physics for agentic work.

L1

A focused agent is a performant agent

The context window is precious, ephemeral, temporal. Protect it above all else.

L2

R&D: Reduce & Delegate

The only two ways to manage a context window. Everything else is commentary.

L3

The plan is the prompt

Great planning is great engineering. Never outsource your thinking — template it.

L4

Template your engineering

Encode how you build into committable prompts, plans, and workflows — then fix systems, never one-off issues.

L5

One agent, one prompt, one purpose

Specialization all the way down. Avoid context pollution like production debt.

L6

Always add feedback loops

Request → validate → resolve. Agents that test their own work win, full stop.

L7

Agents + code beats either alone

Code is deterministic, light-speed, zero tokens. Never give an agent a step code can do.

L8

Scale your compute to scale your impact

Best-of-N, parallel fleets, sandboxes. More compute should equal more trust — or you're doing it wrong.

L9

Three times marks a pattern

The third repetition is your signal: codify it into a reusable prompt, skill, or workflow.

L10

Measure or you're vibe coding

Context meters, observability, KPIs, evals. "If you don't measure it, you can't improve it."

L11

Own your harness

"Whoever controls the agent harness controls your results." CLIs over MCP; the system prompt is the product.

L12

Build the system that builds the system

Spend 50%+ of your time on the agentic layer. Implementation is commoditized; orchestration is not.

◴ vibe coding
  • Not knowing what will happen — and not looking.
  • Prompting one agent back and forth, forever in the loop.
  • Ad-hoc prompt strings that die with the session.
◵ agentic engineering
  • Knowing your system so well you don't need to look.
  • Showing up only at the two ends: planning and review.
  • Every prompt a committed, improvable, versioned asset.

The distinction that defines the era.

Planning and reviewing are the two constraints of agentic coding. Everything Dan builds — meta prompts, ADWs, verifier agents, orchestrators — attacks one of those two bottlenecks. When you're deciding what to build next in your own agentic layer, ask which constraint it relieves.

3

How You Keep Score

Size ↑
bigger units of work per prompt
Attempts ↓
one-shot success, not iteration
Streak ↑
consecutive wins without correction
Presence ↓
less of your time per shipped change
2026 Trust is the metric of the year: "How long can you let your agents go before making a mistake you have to correct?" Trust = speed = iteration = impact.
The trajectory of every lesson. Each tactic exists to move you one box to the right for one more problem class.
4

The Map of the Dojo

Theory feeds practice. The volumes and the vault give you the ideas; the gym and the program make them muscle.

How to use this book.

Every lesson chapter ends in a workout module — a hands-on replication of exactly what Dan did, with done-when criteria. The Repo Gym (chapters 26–27) gives you his real repositories and commit-by-commit rebuild paths. The 12-Week Program (chapter 28) sequences all of it into a progressive training plan.

Do not just read. Dan's own rule applies to his material: don't outsource your learning. Read a chapter, then do its reps on a real codebase — yours, not a toy.

DAILY The one question to ask every morning: "Am I working on the agentic layer, or the application layer?"

02 — Vol I · Tactic 1
tac-1 · hello agentic coding

Your hands are no longer the best tool for the job. Stop coding.

AI coding was phase one. Phase two is agentic coding — "where real engineers strike back" and turn codebases into "increasingly self-operating machines." Throughout TAC, not a single line of code is typed by hand.

Tactic 1 · Stop CodingThe Core FourCommander of ComputeProgrammable Agents
In this chapter 1The Idea 2The Nuggets 3What Dan Did 4The Workout
1

The Idea — Systems That Build Systems

"Engineering was never about writing code. It's about building systems of leverage that produce valuable outcomes for your users and your customers."

Phase one was AI coding — the birth of the VibeCoders and "the complete commoditization of code." Phase two is agentic coding. Tactic #1 is blunt: "Your hands and your mind are no longer the best tool for the job of writing code. Full stop."

AI coding runs on the Big Three: context, model, prompt. Agentic coding adds a fourth dimension — tools — to form the Core Four. "Agentic Coding is a superset of AI coding": the new capability is "prompts that can reliably execute long chains of tools."

The Core Four. "With the right context, model, prompt, and now tools, these prompts are now more capable than ever before."

We move up the stack once again. We become a commander of compute.

— the phase-two role

The agentic prompt is a prompt containing a sequence of tool calls. As a commander of compute, "you take this compute and you use it to build systems that build systems." Your engineering cycles move to "planning, reviewing, and creating closed loop structures."

The mission of TAC: "transform you into an engineer they can't replace. You know who they are. It's your boss. It's the company. It's the industry. It's AI." And a tease of where it ends — ADWs: "long running end-to-end AI developer workflows that run for minutes to hours with and without your oversight."

01

Claude Code's three essentials

"A powerful language model that can reason when needed, the ability to consistently call long chains of tools, and the right agent architecture."

02

Programmable agentic coding tool

Claude Code runs from any language with terminal access — shell, TypeScript/Bun, Python/uv. The key differentiator enabling embedded agents across the SDLC.

03

The Irreplaceable Engineer

The mission of the whole course — and its contradicting truth: to become irreplaceable, you will replace yourself.

2

The Nuggets

01

Engineer, not coder

"You're an engineer, not a coder. The difference is massive."

02

Vastly superior coders

"Powerful language models called the Agentic Loops running on powerful supercomputers are now vastly superior coders than you and I are."

03

No chance of keeping up

"If you're still typing code, you have no chance of keeping up."

04

Let go of the past

"Don't be the engineer that can't let go of the past."

05

The 10x opportunity

"Not by spending 10x the time or effort, but by achieving 10x the leverage… it's 2x, it's 10x, it's 100x — it all depends on the leverage points you can find."

06

A new floor

"Reliable Agentic Coding at scale was not possible before Claude Code."

On engineering

  • "AI coding is not enough. Engineering is about much more than just writing code."
  • "Engineering was never about writing code. It's about building systems of leverage that produce valuable outcomes for your users and your customers."
  • "80% of life is noise, boring repetition and wasted time — but every once in a while an opportunity presents itself that can change everything if you focus."
  • Phase-two engineering cycles go to "planning, reviewing, and creating closed loop structures."
  • "It's not a question of new. It's a question of scale and performance." — on tool calling.

On the game ahead

  • "It's not just about the agentic prompt. It's about where we can use the agentic prompt." The terminal means everywhere.
  • "Tools will change and models will improve. Don't get stuck on any one tool."
  • "In the tech industry, this is where all the alpha lies. It's in the future… your asymmetric engineering advantage."
  • "Big things are just two or more small things put together" — the primitives-first framing seeded here.
PAY TO PLAY "We're going to be using millions and millions of tokens… we have to pay to play."
HABIT "Always check out the settings so you know what Claude Code has access to."
3

What Dan Did

  1. Stacked the toolbelt

    Listed the essential stack: Claude Code, git, gh CLI, Python + Astral uv, Node/npm/Bun — links in the loot box.

  2. Cloned and booted

    git clone https://github.com/disler/tac-1.git, opened VS Code, opened the terminal, ran claude.

  3. Inspected the settings

    Read .claude/settings permissions — "always check what Claude Code has access to."

  4. Ran the AI coding prompt

    From the README: Claude just creates a main file with a print. "This is phase one."

  5. Ran the agentic coding prompt

    ~35 lines: checked out a demo-agentic-coding branch, wrote main_tac, ran both files, git add / commit, maintained a to-do list, reported the work. "This is engineering work, not just writing code."

  6. Opened programmable/

    Three scripts — shell, Bun/TypeScript, Python/uv — each firing Claude Code programmatically with the same prompt.

  7. Deleted the branch, re-ran headlessly

    git branch -D …, then the Python/uv version: the whole workflow re-ran with zero interaction and a full report of outputs at the end.

terminal · getting in
git clone https://github.com/disler/tac-1.git
cd tac-1
code .        # Cmd+J for the terminal
claude
tac-1/ .claude/ # settings — know what your agent can touch programmable/ run.sh # shell run.ts # Bun / TypeScript run.py # Python / uv — the one Dan re-ran README.md # both demo prompts live here
Run it from anywhere. Any language with terminal access can command the agent — that is what makes it programmable.
4

The Workout

GOAL Feel the difference between an AI coding prompt and an agentic prompt, then run an agent programmatically.
PREREQUISITES Claude Code installed and authenticated · git · Python + uv (or Bun) · any empty repo.

To become an irreplaceable engineer, you will replace yourself.

— the contradicting truth · quote of the lesson

  1. Set the AI coding baseline

    In a fresh repo, run claude and prompt: "Create hello.py that prints 'goodbye AI coding'. Do nothing else."

  2. Now go agentic

    Prompt: "Check out a new branch demo-agentic. Create main_tac.py printing 'hello agentic coding world' and a one-line description of AI agents. Run hello.py and main_tac.py and show outputs. git add and commit with a clear message. Report all work done as a checklist."

  3. Inspect the evidence

    Look at the branch, the commit, and the files the agent created.

  4. Make it programmable

    Write run_agent.py (uv single-file script) that shells out claude -p "<the same agentic prompt>" --output-format stream-json --verbose and writes output to a file.

  5. Delete and replay

    Delete the branch and run uv run run_agent.py — verify the agent reproduces everything with zero interaction.

DONE WHEN The branch, files, commit, and final report are recreated entirely by the programmatic run — and you never typed a line of code.
LEVEL UP Write the same runner in a second language (shell or Bun) and have the agent's prompt also open a PR via gh.
Next · The 12 Leverage Points  →
03 — Vol I · Tactic 2
tac-2 · adopt your agent's perspective

Your agent is brilliant, but blind. Hand it the 12 leverage points.

"With every new session, it starts as a blank instance… no context, no memories, and no awareness outside of what you give it." Success is giving it your perspective — and betting on what never changes.

Tactic 2 · Adopt Your Agent's PerspectiveCore Four + 84 KPIsIDKs
In this chapter 1The Idea 2The Nuggets 3What Dan Did 4The Workout
1

The Idea — Twelve Levers, One Blind Genius

Bet on what never changes: the software development lifecycle, compressed to five steps.

The Phase-Two SDLC — Plan, Code, Test, Review, Document — is "our framework for success," the aspect of engineering that never changes while tools and models churn underneath it.

Every leverage point you control either rides inside the agent or flows through it. The Core Four (context, model, prompt, tools) are "always with your agent." The other eight are "external levers… that typically flow through the context window."

The framework for success. Plan, Code, Test, Review, Document — the loop the whole course automates.
The 12 Leverage Points of Agentic Coding. Four ride in-agent; eight flow through the context window. "Your agent can only see what you let it see."

If you don't measure it, you can't improve it.

The Agentic Coding KPIs: Size is the amount of work handed off per prompt. Attempts is the number of prompts needed to finish — target the one-shot. Streak counts back-to-back one-shot successes. Presence is "how much you the engineer have to sit, guide and correct your AI agent" — target zero.

Two more named ideas power this lesson: IDKs — Information Dense Keywords ("Types are IDKs. They point to exact locations in your codebase and represent the flow of a specific type of information") and the Agent Navigation Problem — every new agent must re-explore your codebase, so architecture and consistency stack up "for you or against you." When a workflow repeats, remember: "three time makes a pattern" — template it into a slash command.

Size ↑
work handed off per prompt
Attempts ↓
prompts to finish · target one-shot
Streak ↑
back-to-back one-shots
Presence ↓
babysitting time · target zero
CLOSED LOOPS "We let our agent write the code itself by getting a concrete feedback loop" — self-validating loops, formalized in TAC-5.

Build your codebase for agents — the architecture checklist

  • Clear entry points; constants files; types, classes, structures
  • Meaningful, verbose names — everything you name can be an IDK
  • Files under ~1000 lines; one responsibility per file; separated services
  • Agent docs (README / CLAUDE.md) spread through the tree
  • Test tree mirroring the source tree

"Plans are just prompts… great planning is great prompting." And at the top of the stack sit Templates (reusable prompts — custom slash commands) and ADWs: "one or more agentic prompts wrapped in arbitrary code, kicked off autonomously with a trigger" — the highest level of abstraction.

app/ client/ # one simple split cuts all possibilities in half server/ constants.py # IDKs live here data_models.py # types tell a story tests/ # mirrors the source tree .claude/commands/ # templates: /prime /start /tools specs/ # plans — 750 lines Dan never typed CLAUDE.md # agent docs spread through the tree
2

The Nuggets

01

Teach, don't do

"It's not about what you can do anymore. It's about what you can teach your agents to do."

02

Autonomy to 11

"We're here to dial up the autonomy knob all the way to 11."

03

Sight is a lever

"Your agent can only see what you let it see" — standard out is a top leverage point.

04

No babysitters

"Counterintuitively, iterating is not good… We're not aiming to become a babysitter for AI agents. We're looking for one-shot solutions."

05

Protect the streak

"Once you start one-shotting, don't break the streak. And when you break it, figure out exactly which leverage point you were missing."

06

English, ahead of schedule

"English is the new programming language and we're taking it where it's going before it gets there."

On leverage

  • "Agentic coding is easy to start, hard to master. All the low hanging fruit is getting chewed up."
  • "Do the smart work, not the hard work, to get asymmetric return on your engineering."
  • "Types tell a story about how information travels throughout your codebase."
  • "One simple decision (client/server split) cuts all possibilities in half for your agent."
  • "If it's hard for you, you should assume it'll be hard for your agent."
  • "Consistency is a powerful actor against complexity for both humans and for agents."

On the craft

  • "We want our codebases to be token efficient."
  • "If you're not writing tests, you're probably vibe coding."
  • "Most engineers don't spend enough time communicating what they want done."
  • "Plans are how you communicate massive amounts of work to your agent."
  • "This is not the end game. This is the early game." — on back-and-forth prompting.
  • "Master the primitives and you'll master the compositions."
◴ bad agentic code
  • "Not only do we have bad code, we have bad agentic code" — code that hides errors from agents.
  • Endpoints return raw responses, exceptions swallowed, nothing on stdout.
◵ good agentic code
  • Every exception logs all details; every successful response logs before returning.
  • Errors trace to standard out — the agent diagnoses and fixes without you.
HAND OFF ERRORS "Stop looking at error messages… give them to your agent. Only when your agent fails do you take your precious time and dig in."
MISTAKES ARE FINE "Your agent will make mistakes, just like you. The only question is: does it have the leverage it needs to correct them?"
MEASURE IT "If you don't measure it, you can't improve it." Track Size, Attempts, Streak, and Presence on every session.
3

What Dan Did

  1. Cloned the NL→SQL app

    git clone https://github.com/disler/tac-2.git — Vue-ish frontend, Python backend, in-memory SQLite.

  2. Ran /install — prompts inside prompts

    A 7-line reusable prompt that reads and executes /prime, then installs frontend + backend deps — "no different than passing a function into a function."

  3. Booted the app

    Set up .env (OpenAI + Anthropic keys), ran scripts/start.sh, uploaded sample CSV data, ran natural-language queries.

  4. Dumped the toolbox with /tools

    A baked-in prompt template listing every tool definition Claude Code has — surfacing hidden capabilities like the bash tool's timeout parameter.

  5. Standard out demo — exposed bad agentic code

    A CSV upload errored, but endpoints returned raw responses with no logging. Prompted with @app/server/server.py to log every exception and every success. Errors now trace to stdout.

  6. Let the agent watch stdout live

    Fresh agent, /prime, then ran the start script inside the agent (300s bash timeout). Reproduced the error, asked "any issues with the server?" — it diagnosed unescaped special characters — then "resolve" in YOLO mode.

the stdout fix · one prompt
@app/server/server.py
make sure every exception logs all exception details,
and every successful response before returning
the type-flow trace · fresh instance
# paste the DatabaseSchemaResponse data model, then:
show me how this type flows throughout the application;
detail functions, files, and purpose
RESULT The agent fired a search sub-agent (Task tool) and produced a top-to-bottom information-flow trace — 26K tokens, 7 tool uses.
three time makes a pattern
create .claude/commands/start.md
# then, in a fresh session:
/start
# and the bridge to ADWs — programmable mode:
claude -p "/tools"

He then walked the architecture itself — client/server split, mirrored test tree, agent-facing docs, and a specs/ directory holding a 750-line initialize-codebase plan ("no way I typed that — we plan with our agent") — ran uv run pytest from within the agent so all test output landed in its context, turned the repeated start workflow into /start, and closed by walking the 4 KPIs in a markdown file, mapping each failure mode to the leverage point that fixes it.

4

The Workout

GOAL Make one real codebase legible to agents via stdout, types, and a /prime command — and verify with a one-shot fix.
PREREQUISITES Claude Code · a small client/server project (or clone disler/tac-2).

It's not about you anymore. It's about what you can do for your agent so they can work for you.

— quote of the lesson

  1. Build /prime

    Create .claude/commands/prime.md: run git ls-files, read README, summarize the codebase. Boot claude, run /prime.

  2. Build /tools

    A command that asks the agent to list all its tool definitions; read the output and note 2 capabilities you didn't know.

  3. Audit one endpoint

    If it swallows errors, prompt the agent (with @file references) to log every exception and success to stdout.

  4. Close the loop on a real bug

    Run your app's start script inside the agent with a long bash timeout; trigger a known error in the UI; ask "any issues with the server?" then "resolve".

  5. Trace a type

    Copy one core type into a fresh agent and ask for the full flow trace — file → function → purpose format.

  6. Score yourself

    Track KPIs for the session: size of task, attempts, streak, presence — write them down.

DONE WHEN The agent detects and fixes a runtime bug in one attempt purely from stdout, and your type-trace covers every layer of the app.
LEVEL UP Refactor one confusing directory using the architecture checklist (entry points, verbose names, <1000-line files, mirrored tests) and re-measure attempts on a similar bug.
Next · Success Is Planned  →
04 — Vol I · Tactic 3
tac-3 · template your engineering

The plan is the prompt. Success is planned.

"What was once a complete joke is now the most valuable skill any engineer can have. The prompt is everything" — and a plan is just a prompt scaled up for high impact.

Tactic 3 · Template Your EngineeringMeta PromptsHigher Order Promptsspecs/
In this chapter 1The Idea 2The Nuggets 3What Dan Did 4The Workout
1

The Idea — Encode How You Solve Problems

"Plans are prompts scaled up for high impact… great planning is great prompting."

A scaled prompt is "a plan, a specification, a PRD." Tactic #3 — Template Your Engineering — means "you encode your problem solving into reusable units that you, your team, and your agents can see, use, and importantly, improve."

Two prompt species make it work. A meta prompt is "a prompt that builds a prompt" — /chore generates a plan from a template. A Higher Order Prompt (HOP) takes another prompt as an argument — /implement <path-to-plan>, "plans getting passed into plans," like passing functions into functions. A high-level prompt is "a prompt void of detail" that templates expand into detailed plans, landing in specs/ — "staging environments for code that will be generated."

The pipeline. This chore "has been completed agentically with two prompts — one for the planning phase and one for the coding/building phase."
Anatomy of a template. Purpose, Instructions, Relevant Files, Plan Format, and an $ARGUMENTS slot.

Always run fresh agents. Three reasons.

One — free the context window: "one agent, one task, one mission." Two — it forces prompts, plans, and templates to be "isolated, reusable, improvable assets with zero dependencies." Three — "prepare for true off-device agentic coding": "we need to decouple our performance from the conversational context window."

Inside the templates, Anthropic's encoded IDK "think hard" activates the reasoning model — "throw more compute at the problem." And together, the first three tactics — Stop Coding, Adopt Your Agent's Perspective, Template Your Engineering — form the Pareto 80/20 of agentic engineering.

2

The Nuggets

01

The prompt is everything

"What was once a complete joke is now the most valuable skill any engineer can have. The prompt is everything."

02

Vibe Coders, not Vibe Engineers

"There's a reason they're called Vibe Coders, not Vibe Engineers. They're just coding. We're doing it all."

03

Plan the future

"Those who plan the future tend to create it. Success is planned."

04

Template everything

"Chores, bugs, features, front end, back end, research notebooks, data analytics, code optimization, net new codebases — all of it can be templated. Don't limit yourself."

05

Custom is the moat

"The more custom your templates are, the more unique your engineering gets."

06

The system that builds the system

"You want to be building the system that builds the system."

On planning

  • "Planning alone is expensive… there's always something to miss."
  • Templates are "one of many solutions to the 'my codebase is too big' problem."
  • Out-of-the-box tools (Codex, Copilot, Devin, Jules, Gemini CLI) "don't understand your codebase like you do… they're limited by nature."
  • "It doesn't take a lot to do a lot, but you need the right information."
  • "Not only should we stop coding, we should also lean into and leverage our agent to write plans with us and for us."

On conviction

  • "Don't make the mistake in thinking that Gen AI can't solve your problem… tools will change, models will improve. We know this for a fact."
  • "We need to decouple our performance from the conversational context window."
  • "When I boot up this agent, I know 100% what the agent's perspective is."
  • "The more code, the more complexity in your codebase, the more you'll want to dial in your reusable templates to solve specific problems really well."
  • This chore "has been completed agentically with two prompts — one for the planning phase and one for the coding/building phase."
PLAN FOR FAILURE "Having agents run loose on your codebase without structure, without knowing how you do things, is a plan for failure."
RUN ONE NOW "You want to get to that first aha moment as fast as possible… run one of these. Don't wait to complete this course."
LIMITING BELIEF "Be very careful thinking that these tactics can't work for your codebase. That is a limiting belief."
3

What Dan Did

  1. Cloned tac-3 and installed agentically

    /install now also runs scripts/copy.env to copy the .env from tac-2 — agentic resource reuse — then scripts/start boots the NL→SQL app.

  2. Ran the chore flow

    Fresh Claude → /chore "replace all server print statements proper python logging log levels make sure all output is written to standard out". The meta prompt researched the codebase in thinking mode and generated a plan in specs/.

  3. Dissected chore.md

    Purpose → Instructions → Relevant Files → Plan Format → Chore: $ARGUMENTS.

  4. Implemented in a fresh instance

    New terminal, fresh Claude → /implement specs/<plan>.md. The 12-line HOP: read the plan, think hard, implement, report. Watched it execute, run pytest for regressions, and report.

  5. Validated by hand

    Restarted the server, ran queries, confirmed the new logging format, regex-searched to confirm zero print( remained.

  6. Ran the bug flow

    /bug "resolve major issue our SQL statements not being escaped, user input into SQL" → plan with Problem Statement, Solution Statement, Steps to Reproduce, Root Cause Analysis, Relevant Files, New Files (sql_security), Step-by-Step Tasks, Validation, Notes. Then /implement in a fresh instance — self-validation caught and fixed 2 issues, generating 18 new SQL-injection tests (52 total).

  7. Explained the fresh-agents rationale

    The three reasons above — context, reusable assets, off-device readiness.

  8. Previewed out-of-loop with /feature

    Wrote /feature "create a new query history side panel…" but ran it via programmable mode — watching the JSONL stream append live while the agent produced a comprehensive feature plan: user story, problem/solution statement, implementation phases, testing, acceptance criteria.

.claude/commands/chore.md · the skeleton
# Purpose
Create a plan in specs/ to resolve the chore.

# Instructions
Research the codebase. Think hard.

# Relevant Files
# Plan Format
Chore / Description / Relevant Files /
Step-by-Step Tasks / Validation Commands / Notes

Chore: $ARGUMENTS
.claude/commands/implement.md · 12 lines
Read the plan at $ARGUMENTS. Think hard.
Implement every step in order.
Then report: summarize work + files changed.
the out-of-loop preview
claude -p "/feature '…'" \
  --output-format stream-json \
  --dangerously-skip-permissions \
  --model <best> --verbose > output.jsonl
specs/ chore-python-logging.md # prompt 1 wrote this bug-sql-escaping.md # 18 new injection tests feature-query-history.md # streamed in headlessly
4

The Workout

GOAL Build a chore → plan → implement pipeline from templates and ship one chore with exactly two prompts.
PREREQUISITES Claude Code · a repo with at least one test command (or disler/tac-3).

The plan is the prompt — and that means great planning is great prompting.

— quote of the lesson

  1. Write the meta prompt

    Create .claude/commands/chore.md: Purpose ("create a plan in specs/ to resolve the chore"), Instructions, Relevant Files, a Plan Format (Chore / Description / Relevant Files / Step-by-Step Tasks / Validation Commands / Notes), ending with Chore: $ARGUMENTS.

  2. Write the HOP

    Create .claude/commands/implement.md: "Read the plan at $ARGUMENTS. Think hard. Implement every step in order. Run the validation commands. Report: summary + files changed."

  3. Generate a real plan

    Run /chore "<one real small task in your repo>" and read the generated plan in specs/.

  4. Implement fresh

    Open a NEW terminal + fresh Claude instance; run /implement specs/<plan>.md.

  5. Verify and commit

    Run the plan's validation commands yourself; commit specs + code.

  6. Repeat headlessly

    claude -p "/chore '<another chore>'" --output-format stream-json --verbose > run.jsonl — and watch the stream.

DONE WHEN One chore ships end-to-end with two prompts, the plan file exists in specs/, and validation commands pass on rerun.
LEVEL UP Add /bug (Steps to Reproduce + Root Cause Analysis) and /feature (user story + phased implementation) templates; specialize one template to a specific subsystem of your codebase.
Next · AFK Agents  →
05 — Vol I · Tactic 4
tac-4 · stay out the loop

AFK agents: from GitHub issue to pull request while you're away from the keyboard.

In-loop is "sitting on their device, prompting back and forth." Out-loop is "off-device agentic coding… you write high-to-low-level prompts that you pass off to your agentic pipeline, then you go AFK."

Tactic 4 · Stay Out The LoopPITERADWsThe Agentic Layer
In this chapter 1The Idea 2The Nuggets 3What Dan Did 4The Workout
1

The Idea — PITER, and Owning Your Pipeline

"Your agentic pipeline is too valuable to outsource to a third party tool… You want to own your agentic pipelines."

AFK Agents are "agents that run while we're away from the keyboard." They need four elements — PITER ("Peter"): Prompt input, Trigger, Environment, Review. Dan's defaults: GitHub issues, GitHub webhooks via a Cloudflared tunnel, a dedicated Mac mini the agent fully controls, and GitHub pull requests.

Why not Codex, Devin, or Jules? Cloud tools "are literally designed for everyone's codebases, not yours." Your templated engineering — /chore, /bug, /feature, /implement — chains into workflows that run your templates on your problems.

PITER. Prompt input, Trigger, Environment, Review — Dan's defaults shown; every slot is swappable.

"A reusable agentic workflow that combines code, agentic prompts, and agents to deliver results autonomously… the synthesis of previous-generation deterministic code and new-generation non-deterministic language models, prompt chains, and now agents."

— the ADW, fully defined. "In the future these will just be known as scripts."

The AI Developer Workflow is the highest leverage point and the highest composition level. And this lesson names something new: the agentic layer — "a net new agentic layer around your codebase" formed by two directories: .claude/commands (prompts) plus adws/ (workflows).

Two supporting patterns: issue classification — an agent classifies each issue as chore/bug/feature and routes to the right template, because "you need to be able to target specific classes of problems" — and the health check, a pre-flight ADW validating env vars, repo access, and Claude Code before any workflow runs.

Issue in, PR out. Fetch → classify → branch → plan → build → PR, "with issue comments as a live feed throughout."
2

The Nuggets

01

Leverage the future

"Stay out the loop… models will improve and tools will change. When you stay out the loop, you leverage this fact."

02

You will be wrong

"If you are not wrong now, you will be. It's only a matter of time."

03

Tomorrow's game

"We're not playing today's game, we're playing tomorrow's."

04

Chores first, no debate

"Chores are the simplest unit of engineering work that your agent should be taking off your plate with no debate at all."

05

Most work isn't special

"In-loop agentic coding is great for experimentation, exploring new territory, and very hard specific problems. But let's be real — most of engineering is not that."

06

Not your expertise

"There are increasingly more chores, bugs, and features that don't require your expertise at all."

On ownership

  • "Your agentic pipeline is too valuable to outsource to a third party tool… You want to own your agentic pipelines."
  • Cloud tools "are literally designed for everyone's codebases, not yours."
  • "It doesn't matter if our agent can solve every problem if we don't know that it's solved. We need to observe, we need to report, we need to log and monitor."
  • "No ad hoc prompts as strings inside of the codebase. Everything's isolated so that we can improve it."
  • "The details are everything. There's a reason why a lot of agentic coding tools don't work — they don't understand your codebase."

On method

  • "Success is absolutely planned. You can plan success into your codebase by templating your engineering."
  • "Start with low hanging fruit… template the chore, then move up to bugs, and eventually the feature level."
  • "These four elements can be anything… Don't read into how this is configured too much. Focus on the composable units."
  • "We're focusing on the what, not the how. The how is already encoded in the codebase."
MODEL CHOICE "For your build and implement steps, use the most powerful model you can."
THE AHA MOMENT "The ROI here is insane. I want you to get to the aha lightbulb moment you'll get when you run one of these end to end."
EASY THERE "Your ADWs can operate on your ADWs. So, you know, don't get too meta."
3

What Dan Did

  1. Ran the end-to-end demo first

    Created GitHub issue "ADW documentation — /chore document the ADW directory, read everything in adws, update README" → webhook fired → the Mac mini agent environment picked it up (screen-shared live) → classified as chore → new branch → planner agent wrote the spec → implementer agent built it → PR posted back. Total: 5 minutes, with live GitHub issue comments the whole way.

  2. Cloned tac-4 locally

    Disabled the webhook, then /install — unsets remote origin, walks you through creating your own repo, copies .env files, installs deps, reports "action required" items.

  3. Set the root .env

    ANTHROPIC_API_KEY and CLAUDE_CODE_PATH via which claude — "the agent SDK currently only runs with the API key."

  4. Filed issue #33: JSONL support

    A mid-level prompt with constraints: stdlib only, concat nested fields, handle arrays, update the UI.

  5. Ran the health check

    uv run adws/health_check.py 33 — verified env, repo, Claude Code, and posted a health comment to the issue.

  6. Ran the ADW with a local trigger

    uv run adws/adw_plan_build.py 33 — unique ADW ID assigned, jsonl-support branch created, classified as feature, /feature planner ran, /implement builder ran, PR created (~17 min).

  7. Walked the ADW code

    classify_issue maps to slash commands; build_plan requests agent name, slash command, args, model; execute fires Claude Code in programmable mode with --dangerously-skip-permissions (safe in its own environment); per-session log files via hooks; per-ADW agent directories — planner, implementer, branch generator, issue classifier, PR creator, committers.

  8. Reviewed and manually tested the PR

    JSONL upload option in the UI, sample data files, comprehensive tests; regression-tested existing queries.

terminal · the whole tactic in two commands
# pre-flight: env vars, repo access, Claude Code
uv run adws/health_check.py 33

# issue #33 → classify → branch → plan → build → PR
uv run adws/adw_plan_build.py 33

WHY IT WINS It outperforms generic cloud agents because it runs your templates on your problems — the how is already encoded in the codebase.
4

The Workout

GOAL Ship one GitHub issue to PR with zero keyboard time between prompt and review.
PREREQUISITES TAC-3-style templates (/chore, /feature, /implement) · a GitHub repo you own · gh CLI · ANTHROPIC_API_KEY.

You don't fix the issue — you fix the system that caused the issue.

— quote of the lesson. Fix your templates, fix your ADW.

  1. Write the ADW

    adws/adw_plan_build.py (uv single-file script): parse issue number → gh issue view for title/body → agent 1 classifies chore/bug/feature (its own prompt file) → create branch → agent 2 runs the matching template via claude -p "/feature '<issue body>'" --output-format stream-json → agent 3 runs claude -p "/implement <plan path>" → commit → gh pr create. Post gh issue comment progress updates between every step.

  2. Write the health check

    adws/health_check.py — validate env vars, repo remote, and a trivial claude -p "respond OK" call.

  3. File a real chore

    As a GitHub issue; run the health check, then the ADW with the issue number.

  4. Review only the PR

    Do not touch the branch.

  5. Upgrade the trigger

    Add a webhook: expose a tiny listener via cloudflared tunnel or ngrok that runs the ADW on issues.opened.

DONE WHEN An issue you wrote from the GitHub UI (or your phone) results in a merged-quality PR with live issue comments — and you never opened the working branch locally.
LEVEL UP Move the environment off your machine — a spare box, VM, or container that listens for webhooks 24/7 — and run a feature-class issue through it.
Next · Close the Loops  →
06 — Vol I · Tactic 5
tac-5 · always add feedback loops

Close the loops, and the code writes itself.

Your work is useless unless it's tested — and testing is now your agents' job. Encode every way you'd validate production-ready work into commands and tools, structure prompts as request → validate → resolve, and your agents self-correct until the work is provably done.

Tactic 5feedback loopsPlaywright MCPadw_test
In this chapter 1The Idea 2The Nuggets 3What Dan Did 4The Workout
1

The Idea

Tactic #5: Always Add Feedback Loops. "Your work, my work, any engineer's work is useless unless it's tested."

Closing the loop means "letting your agent operate on work, calling a command or tool to get feedback on the success of the work… your agent continues to build until the feedback is positive. When you do this, you let the code write itself."

Every closed-loop prompt has three parts: Request → Validation → Resolution. "This prompt instructs the agent to validate itself." Linters, unit tests, builds, Playwright browser tests — anything you would run by hand becomes a loop your agent runs for you.

Anatomy of a closed-loop prompt. Request → Validation → Resolution — the agent cycles until every validator passes, with no human diagnosis in between.

"Given a unit of valuable work that's production ready, how would you, the engineer, test and validate this work? If you can answer this for every class of work and encode the answers into a command or tool call, you will fly while other engineers run."

— the core validation question
01

Three loops, one vocabulary

In-loop = you prompting back and forth. Out-loop = a high-level prompt through the PITER system on an isolated machine. Closing the loop = the agent's own execute–validate–reflect cycle.

02

The hierarchy of trust

"The ultimate test will always be your users. The next best test used to be you. Now the next best test is an army of agents validating your entire codebase with regression tests and, most importantly, end-to-end tests."

03

Tests as rule of law

"What's more important, the code or your tests? The answer should be your tests. Your tests should be the rule of law in your codebase."

04

Playwright MCP

Browser control as a validation tool: open the browser, type, click, screenshot, read the images back, verify results — your agent clicks through the feature so you never have to.

05

The e2e test file

A markdown prompt: test name, test steps, verify IDK statements, success criteria — run through a HOP with preconditions (reset DB, ports, screenshot directory) and a JSON output format.

06

Validation, templated

The chore / bug / feature plan formats include a Validation Commands section by default — plus "if this bug affects UI or user interactions, create an end-to-end test."

2

The Nuggets

"Now engineers that test with their agents win. Full stop, zero exceptions."

— quote of the lesson
"We're stacking the confidence." Each loop — ruff → pytest → compile → tsc → build → e2e — adds certainty, so you stop second-guessing.
01

The waste of clicking

"You probably opened the browser and clicked through your new feature. What a waste of time."

02

Tests × executions

"The value of tests is multiplied by the number of agent executions that occur in your codebase."

03

One tool call away

"Everything is one tool call away. Tools are just functions and functions can do anything."

04

Hand off more

"Hand off more responsibility to your agents, not less." Your agent has more agency with self-validating loops.

05

Fresh agents, always

"Always run fresh agents… no context pollution." One agent per loop, every time.

06

Resolve, then re-verify

"It's not good enough to resolve once if that resolution causes something else to break — rerun every validation step top to bottom."

On scale and mindset

  • "Our most valuable contribution is the experience we create for our users."
  • "Have your agents test on your behalf like you never could, at scales you never will achieve. This is the gift of generative AI."
  • "With every passing set of tests, you free your context window and you stop second-guessing."
  • "Test, let your agents scale your success. Same game, different day, new tools, exponential impact."
  • "Testing at its core is simple: did it do what you designed it to do, and did it break anything else along the way?"
  • "We will be the ones prompting the errors" — as models improve, your validators catch your mistakes.
  • "Lean into the future. Don't lean into the present, don't lean into the past."

On moving out of the loop

  • "We want end-to-end agents, not in-loop agents… we don't want to be babysitting."
  • "Everything starts with a single prompt. Everything starts in the loop, and then slowly move it out the loop."
  • "One agent running here, one agent running there" — parallel issue-fixing is normal once loops are closed.
  • The in-loop limitation: "You're pretty much limited to one agent unless you're conscious about everything that you're changing" — no worktrees yet.
  • "When you're setting up your ADW to test, you might point this at staging or even a production test account."
  • "Engineers that test with their agents win" is a scaling law, not a slogan — the loop count compounds.
3

What Dan Did

  1. Clone and install

    git clone disler/tac-5, then /install — which now resets the SQLite DB and copies both app and tac-4 .env files.

  2. Fired the big one first

    GitHub issue "adw_plan_build_test feature — create a new button (generate random NL query)" → webhook → the dedicated agent box ran the full plan-build-test pipeline in the background for the whole lesson.

  3. Closed loop v1 — linter

    Prompt: update @sql_processor to comment out validation + "validate your work with: ruff check." Ruff flagged the unused import via stdout; the agent fixed and re-ran until clean.

  4. Closed loop v2 — three validators

    Remove the insights endpoint + move load_dotenv; validate with ruff, uv run pytest, and python -m py_compile. Ruff caught import-order, pytest caught the previously commented-out validation — fixed without any memory of the prior session. All three loops closed.

  5. Closed loop v3 — full-stack + browser

    A reusable validation prompt: backend checks, frontend checks (tsc --noEmit, bun run build), then Playwright MCP — open the local URL, screenshot before, type a query, screenshot after, read both images, verify the NL query returned exactly 7 results. Dan verified the screenshots himself by re-running the query manually.

  6. Caught a side effect

    Via git status: tests had written junk tables to the "production" SQLite DB — bookmarked as the next bug.

  7. Templated the feedback loops

    The /bug meta prompt now bakes Validation Commands into every plan, plus the conditional e2e-test instruction. Ran /bug 111 (run tests in memory) → plan with sqlite3 checks, targeted pytest, before/after table diffs → /implement in a fresh agent. Ran /bug 222 in parallel (disable input while running + debounce) — its plan included a new e2e test file with test steps, verify statements, and success criteria.

  8. Walked adw_test.py, then traced a failure

    /test returns standardized JSON so failures fan out to isolated resolver agents with retry parameters; /test_e2e is a HOP looping every e2e file with preconditions and screenshot output. Traced a failed run on the agent box by ADW ID through agents/ + logs/ — root cause was an Anthropic API 500, not the workflow.

  9. Reviewed the finished PR

    The new "generate random query" button worked in the agent's environment — with one noted UI nit: the motivation for the review step in TAC-6.

closed loop v1 — the three-part prompt
# Request
Update @sql_processor: comment out the validation logic.

# Validation
Validate your work with: ruff check

# Resolution
If there are any issues, resolve them and re-run
the validation until it passes clean.
closed loop v2 — run in order, top to bottom
1. ruff check
2. uv run pytest
3. python -m py_compile

# If any step fails: stop, resolve immediately,
# then rerun EVERY validation step.
CAUGHT A single git status exposed the side effect the loops missed — junk tables in the live DB. The fix wasn't manual: it became /bug 111, "tests are writing junk data to the live database — run tests in memory."

Every loop above ran in YOLO mode with a fresh agent — no context carried between sessions.

adw_test, the testing ADW. Fetch issue → run the full suite via /test returning standardized JSON → per-failure isolated resolver agents in a retry loop → e2e HOP → report → commit → push/PR.
4

The Workout

GOAL Convert your manual verification ritual into closed loops an agent runs, including one browser-level e2e test.
PREREQS TAC-3/4 setup; a linter, a test runner, and a build command in your repo; Playwright MCP configured in .mcp.json.

  1. Take your validation inventory

    Write down every step you personally take to verify a change ships — lint, tests, build, click-through. This is your validation inventory.

  2. Write one closed-loop prompt

    Request (one small refactor) + Validation (ruff check or equivalent) + Resolution ("if any issues, fix and re-run until clean"). Run it; watch the loop close.

  3. Scale to three validators

    Linter + unit tests + compile/build, with the instruction: "run in order top to bottom; if any step fails, stop, resolve immediately, then rerun EVERY validation step."

  4. Add the browser

    A validation prompt that starts your app, opens it via Playwright MCP, screenshots before/after a scripted interaction, and verifies a precise expected result.

  5. Bake validation into your templates

    Add a "Validation Commands" section to your /chore, /bug, /feature plan formats, plus "if this affects UI, create an e2e test file" with a test-steps / verify / success-criteria format.

  6. Prove the template carries its own validators

    Run one /bug through plan → implement and confirm the generated plan validates itself.

DONE WHEN An intentionally broken change gets fully repaired by the agent with zero human diagnosis, and one e2e screenshot pair proves a UI behavior.
LEVEL UP Build adw_test.py — run all tests, emit JSON results, spawn one fresh resolver agent per failure with a retry cap, then commit and PR the fixes.
Next · Let Your Agents Focus  →
07 — Vol I · Tactic 6
tac-6 · one agent, one prompt, one purpose

A focused agent is a productive agent.

Complete the SDLC with dedicated review agents — is what we built what we planned, with screenshot proof — and document agents whose conditional docs feed future agents, all governed by the tactic that sidesteps context pollution: one agent, one prompt, one purpose.

Tactic 6review agentsconditional docspresence = 2
In this chapter 1The Idea 2The Nuggets 3What Dan Did 4The Workout
1

The Idea

Tactic #6: One Agent, One Prompt, One Purpose. "Use specialized agents with focused prompts to achieve a single purpose. No agent runs more than a single prompt."

The enemy is context pollution — context overloading, toxic context. "Massive context windows often lead to a distracted, confused agent." The antidote is the minimum context principle: "You want to context engineer as little as possible — the minimum context in your prompt required to solve the problem."

Four advantages follow: you free up the context window; you let your agent focus; every prompt is committed — reproducible and improvable; and you "effectively create evals for the agentic layer of your codebase."

Why focus wins. "Every piece of context you add increases the number of variables your agent has to reason about" — specialized agents each get one prompt and one purpose.
The SDLC as questions and answers. "Testing answers does it work, but review answers is what we built what we asked for." Conditional documentation closes "a complete software developer lifecycle feedback loop" from documenting back into planning.
3
constraints: context window, codebase complexity, your abilities
2/3
bypassed by specialized agents — "model intelligence is not a constraint"
3
issue severities: skippable · tech_debt · blocker
2
optimal presence: your prompt in, your review + merge out
01

adw_review

Find the spec from the branch → /review against it (git diff + Playwright screenshots) → upload to R2 → if blockers, loop create_and_implement_patch (max retries) → post results + update PR.

02

adw_patch — the surgical template

"Create a focused patch plan to resolve a specific issue with minimal targeted changes" — a template meta prompt distinct from chore / bug / feature.

03

adw_document + two doc trees

/document generates concise markdown into app_docs/ (your app) from git diff vs main + the spec. ai_docs/ holds third-party docs.

04

Conditional documentation

conditional_docs.md: "if your task matches any conditions listed, include those documentation files" — referenced inside /bug, /chore, /feature, /patch, and /prime.

05

The state object

state.json carries ADW ID, branch name, plan file, issue class — meta info that travels along every workflow so composed ADWs can chain.

06

Composable ADWs

adw_plan_build_review, adw_plan_build_document, adw_plan_build_test_review ("probably the winning workflow") — each a standalone uv script chained by ADW ID.

SEVERITY "If you ask them to find something, they'll find something." Give review agents the tools to delineate what matters: skippable, tech_debt, blocker — only blockers stop the pipeline.
2

The Nuggets

"One agent, one prompt, one purpose."

— quote of the lesson

"Isn't more context better for your agent? This is not true."

"Big tech is chasing the all-in-one god model, the super agent that can do it all. That's not what we're doing here. Real engineering requires real developer workflows, not some pie-in-the-sky god model." Every step of engineering requires "a different set of information, a different approach, a different perspective, a different set of tools and context."

01

Focus is throughput

"A focused engineer working on a single task is a productive engineer. Agents are the same." And: "When your agent's performance drops, your performance drops."

02

Compaction is a band-aid

"If your agent is running compact, it is losing information." Every added piece of context increases the variables your agent must reason about.

03

Proof over promises

"You want your agents communicating proof — a hard asset that proves what was built is what you asked for. Screenshots, reports, ideally video."

04

Fast yes/no

"Even when your agent is wrong, when it presents you with proof that it thinks it's right, you can quickly say yes or no."

05

Embed your own review

"Whatever you would do to review that the thing is what you asked for — that's what you embed into your review step."

06

Blame the system

"I'm not blaming the model for this. I'm blaming my skills. I'm blaming the ADWs."

On the prompt level

  • "Everything comes down to the prompt level. All the ADW surrounding deterministic code is just support for the agents doing the real work."
  • "While other companies run big context windows blown away at the end, you'll run single focused prompts you can pinpoint and improve."
  • "The value in your ADWs isn't in a single workflow — it's the power of combining workflows: isolated, reviewable, improvable."
  • "It's a good practice to encode the agent names as constants right at the top of the file."
  • "Reduce the number of human-in-the-loop points in your workflow."

On documentation and identity

  • "There's no excuse for having poor documentation" — every ADW script self-documents its workflow at the top.
  • Documentation timing: "how you document and when to include your documentation is the tricky part."
  • "Why didn't you just do that by hand? You're missing the point… This isn't about you anymore. It's about teaching your agents to build on your behalf, so that it can be about you again."
  • "Do you want to be an agentic engineer, or an engineer of the past?"
3

What Dan Did

  1. Clone, install, tour

    git clone disler/tac-6; /install in Opus + YOLO mode (adds Cloudflare R2 env vars for image upload). Toured the new ADWs: adw_review, adw_patch, adw_document, plus the composed pipelines — standalone uv scripts chained by ADW ID.

  2. Filed issue #14

    One-click table export + result export as CSV — two new endpoints, download icon placement "directly to the left of the hide button."

  3. Ran the composed pipeline

    uv run adws/adw_plan_build_review.py 14 on his own machine, to inspect in real time. Agents own the repo — "we shouldn't be doing anything alongside them."

  4. Walked the review internals live

    state.json, branch validation, R2 uploader init, run_review → the /review prompt (collapse-first reading: review work against spec, use git diff, capture screenshots, report issues or success), prompt-level variables as on-the-fly IDKs, JSON ReviewResult/ReviewIssue Pydantic types, the three severity classes, and the patch-resolution loop with max retries.

  5. Reviewed the screenshots

    The feature worked — but the export button was misplaced (the agent missed "left of the hide button"): a blocker the review agent failed to flag.

  6. Filed a patch

    Issue comment: "make sure download button for query results is just to the left of the hide button," then adw_patch.py <issue> --adw-id <same> — after first passing the wrong issue number, which the state file caught. Patch plan into specs/patch/, implemented, UI verified fixed.

  7. Post-mortemed the miss

    Three root causes, all fixable in the system — see the callout.

  8. Ran the document agent

    uv run adws/adw_document.py <issue> <same ADW ID>: generated an app_docs/ feature doc (screenshots, what changed, files touched, how to use) from git diff + spec — and agentically updated conditional_docs.md.

  9. Closed the planning↔documentation loop

    Showed conditional_docs referenced in /bug, /chore, /feature, /patch, /prime. Scored the run against the KPIs: attempts 2 (patch needed), streak broken, presence ~3 vs the optimal 2.

the /review verdict — JSON with severity classes
{
  "success": false,
  "review_summary": "CSV export works end to end",
  "issues": [
    {
      "class": "blocker",
      "issue": "export button not placed left of hide button"
    }
  ],
  "screenshots": ["review_img_1.png", "review_img_2.png"]
}
POST-MORTEM Why review missed the blocker: the /review prompt captured screenshots but never read them back into context (fix: "for every image you take, read it in during the review process"); Playwright MCP wasn't in --vision mode; and the test step had been skipped entirely — adw_plan_build_test_review would likely have caught it.
adws/ adw_review.py # review vs spec + screenshot proof adw_patch.py # surgical fixes, specs/patch/ adw_document.py # app_docs/ + conditional_docs.md adw_plan_build_review.py adw_plan_build_document.py app_docs/ # docs about YOUR app ai_docs/ # third-party docs
adw_review. Review against the spec with screenshot proof; blockers trigger a fresh patch-plan agent plus a fresh implement agent, capped by max retries, before results post back to the PR.
4

The Workout

GOAL Add review and document agents to your pipeline, with image proof and conditional docs.
PREREQS TAC-5 pipeline; Playwright MCP; any public bucket (R2/S3) or GitHub image upload for screenshots.

  1. Write /review.md

    Variables at top (ADW ID, spec file, agent name, screenshot dir); instructions to git-diff against main, compare against the spec, capture screenshots of critical functionality, read each screenshot back in, and output JSON {success, review_summary, issues[{class: skippable|tech_debt|blocker}], screenshots}.

  2. Write /patch.md

    "Create a focused patch plan resolving one specific issue with minimal targeted changes" → plan into specs/patch/.

  3. Write adw_review.py

    Load state → run /review → upload screenshots → for each blocker, fresh patch-plan agent + fresh /implement agent (max 3 retries) → post results with image links.

  4. Write /document.md

    A template meta prompt generating a doc in app_docs/ from git diff + spec, then updating conditional_docs.md with when-to-read conditions.

  5. Wire in conditional docs

    Reference conditional_docs.md in the Relevant Files section of /chore, /bug, /feature, and /prime.

  6. Run the full lifecycle

    Plan → build → test → review → document on one feature; deliberately give a precise UI placement instruction and check whether review catches deviations.

DONE WHEN The review step posts screenshot proof with a severity-classified JSON verdict; a follow-up plan automatically pulls in the new documentation via conditional docs.
LEVEL UP Make review self-healing — auto-run the patch loop on blockers without your involvement — and upgrade proof from screenshots to screen-recorded video.
Next · Zero-Touch Engineering  →
08 — Vol I · Tactic 7
tac-7 · target zero touch engineering

Drop the R. Target zero touch engineering.

With a full agentic layer you fire five issues in parallel — git worktrees, model-set knobs — and agents ship end to end. Once your streak proves you add no value reviewing a problem class, you drop the R off PITER. The secret: TAC was never about the SDLC — it's composable agentic primitives that solve any problem class.

Tactic 7PITER → PITEgit worktreesmodel setspresence = 1
In this chapter 1The Idea 2The Nuggets 3What Dan Did 4The Workout
1

The Idea

Tactic #7: Target Zero Touch Engineering. "Just like you've stopped coding, you'll stop reviewing… This is YOLO mode for your AI developer workflows. Maximum confidence mode for agentic engineering."

The ZTE realization: "This human in the loop model is a bottleneck. I'm not catching any bugs anymore. I'm not adding any value outside of the agentic layer. I'm going to remove the human review for this class of problems." Threshold intuition: ~90% confidence, rising with every model, tool, and template improvement.

"You'll drop yourself off the end of the Outloop Peter framework and it becomes Pete, not Peter. You'll drop off the review."

PITER → PITE. Prompt input, trigger, environment stay; the review — you — comes off the end once the streak proves you add no value there.
Three levels of velocity. In loop → out loop → ZTE. The best out-loop coders sit at presence 2 (prompt + review); ZTE is presence 1 — the prompt only.

The scale is a progression, not a switch: "Think in the gray. We're not jumping to out-loop. We're progressively handing off more and more work." Two knobs make it practical:

ISO workflows (git worktree isolation). adw_sdlc_iso (plan → build → test → review → document, isolated), adw_sdlc_zte_iso (SDLC + ship), adw_patch_iso, adw_ship_iso. Every workflow creates its own worktree under trees/ with its own ports, env, and install (/install_worktree) — "the easiest way to parallelize your workflows." Docker and VMs work too; "it only matters that you can."

Model sets (base | heavy). An encoded IDK passed in the issue body; agents.py maps each slash command to a base or heavy (Opus-class) model, parsed by /classify_adw at workflow start — "a knob to turn to increase the compute that your workflow uses."

The ship step. Checkout main outside the tree → merge the feature branch → post success — "approve and merge the PR agentically… deploy, ship."

"The secret of tactical agentic coding is that it's not about the software developer lifecycle at all. It's about composable agentic primitives you can use to solve any engineering problem class."

— quote of the lesson

The SDLC "is powerful, but it's a remnant of the past… a package for me to deliver a message to you." What remains are the primitives — and a choice among the four postures toward the future: run away · stand still · walk toward it · sprint ("beat everyone else there").

2

The Nuggets

"I parallelized myself across five features."

— one machine, five worktrees, zero collisions

"You'll witness something most engineers dismiss as impossible: a codebase that nearly runs itself."

"We can scale our impact as easily as copying a file" — engineering digitized into the agentic layer. "The agentic layer is where 1x input becomes 10x output, and 10x input becomes 100x."

01

One day at a time

"It's not in loop versus out loop. It's in loop moving toward out loop, one day at a time." Engineering "is not black and white. It's gray."

02

Solve the layer, not the problem

"We're not solving the problem directly anymore… the rate at which you can solve your specific problem is drastically scaled up when you put your effort into your agentic layer."

03

Chores → bugs → features

"First, go after chores, then bugs, then features. Solve the stupid, simple problems that waste your time, then scale up."

04

Keep the review edge

"You do want to stay on the review system as much as possible… a comprehensive review system is a massive edge for increasing your review velocity."

Proof of shipping

"Agents took images and gave us proof that the feature has shipped… Remember what review answers: not 'does it work?' but 'is what we built what we asked for?'"

The belief change

"If you don't believe your agents can run your codebase, you're cooked." "You must update your beliefs… for 10-100x scaled engineering with agents."

On compounding autonomy

  • "If you don't invest in the agentic layer, how will you know when agents can solve your problem class end to end with no oversight?"
  • "I can guarantee you right now, you are doing engineering work you do not have to do."
  • "You can encode any information dense keyword you want that changes your workflows."

    With the dedicated agent box listening to webhooks: (1) increase drag-and-drop surface area (feature, adw_sdlc_iso); (2) JSON export (feature); (3) generate random synthetic data from a table's schema (mid-level feature, model_set heavy); (4) upgrade the query-generation model to o4-mini (chore); (5) CSV label mismatch (bug via adw_patch_iso). "This is zero touch engineering."

4

The Workout

GOAL Run two ADWs in parallel via git worktrees, then take one trivial problem class to zero-touch (auto-merge).
PREREQS TAC-6 composed pipeline; webhook or local trigger; a repo where auto-merging to main is safe.

  1. Add worktree isolation

    At ADW start: git worktree add trees/<adw-id> -b <branch>, run /install_worktree (copy envs, install deps, assign unique ports), and run all agents with cwd inside the tree.

  2. Add a model-set knob

    A classify_adw step that parses model_set: base|heavy from the issue body, and a map from each slash command to a model.

  3. File two issues simultaneously

    One chore, one small feature — confirm both worktrees run concurrently without collisions.

  4. Write adw_ship.py

    From the root repo: checkout main, merge the ADW branch, push, close the issue with a success comment.

  5. Compose adw_sdlc_zte.py

    Plan → build → test → review → document → ship. Pick a genuinely trivial class (style tweak, copy change) and run it end to end with zero review.

  6. Verify by pulling main

    The change should already be there.

DONE WHEN Two parallel worktree runs complete cleanly, and one issue goes prompt → production merge with your presence = 1 (the prompt).
LEVEL UP Harden the ZTE gate: require N consecutive one-shot successes on a problem class (tracked in a log) before ship is allowed; add a parallel double-review — two review agents must both pass — as the merge condition.
Next · The Agentic Layer  →
09 — Vol I · Tactic 8
tac-8 · prioritize agentics

The agentic layer is the work.

All eight tactics compress into one: prioritize agentics — spend the majority of your engineering time on the layer of adws/, prompts, and plans that rings your codebase, because you can teach compute to engineer your way. The daily question: am I working on the agentic layer, or in the application layer?

Tactic 8agentic layeradw_promptthe guiding question
In this chapter 1The Idea 2The Nuggets 3What Dan Did 4The Workout
1

The Idea

Tactic #8: Prioritize Agentics. "Agentics refer to systems or entities capable of autonomous action and decision-making. This tactic represents all others… all tactics are compressed into this tactic."

The agentic layer is "the combination of traditional deterministic code stored in your ADWs directory as a scriptable layer, with the new non-deterministic agentic technology — the language model, the agent, Claude Code and whatever comes next… the old world of deterministic code and the new world of agentic prompts."

Against it sits the application layer: DevOps + infrastructure + database + the raw app. The rule of thumb: at least half your engineering time on the agentic layer. "Command your agentic layer to run and control your application layer."

The ring. Deterministic scripts + non-deterministic agents wrap the application and operate on it — the layer where 1x input becomes 10x output.

"Am I working on the agentic layer, or am I working in the application layer?"

— the final guiding question. "If you forget everything else, this is the only question you need."

The Minimum Viable Agentic Layer has three pieces, kept strictly isolated from the application: an ADWs directory (any scripting layer — uv, bun, shell), prompts (.claude/commands), and plans (specs/).

Its thinnest expression is the gateway script, adw_prompt — a script that calls one Claude Code agent with one prompt and manages surrounding state and logging. "This script is the gateway into agentic coding."

And the endgame framing: "with agentic coding, not only is code a commodity, but the implementation as well" — early signs of "the commoditization of engineering." Value moves up to system design, system architecture, encoding your domain expertise, quality control, and most importantly creative problem decomposition.

Eight tactics, one compression. The canonical order, collapsing into the tactic that represents all others — and the one question you ask every day.
2

The Nuggets

"When the agents arrived, something changed. We weren't just AI coding anymore. We started orchestrating intelligence."

— quote of the lesson

"Build the system that builds the system — we're talking about the new agentic layer of your codebase."

"Machines that operate with your judgment, shipping your way, getting work done autonomously in your product." And a warning against tool worship: "This is not a Claude Code course… Claude Code represents the first viable tool that unlocks agentic engineering, but it won't be the last. Keep your eyes on the primitives. Focus on the pieces that make the whole."

01

Parabolic returns

"Building an agentic layer is parabolic. Put 10 minutes in, get two hours of value out or more."

02

In-loop is a starting line

"In-loop is a great place to start, a great place to do deep work, and a terrible place to stay." Later: "Not only will you stay out the loop — getting in the loop will be harmful."

03

Many, not one

"The power of agents isn't in one, it's in many." And: "Focus on problem classes, not one-off fixes."

Problem classes get solved

"I have solved the problem class of building Astral UV MCP servers. It's done." One prompt → a full MCP server.

05

The validation set exists

"There is a set of validations in your codebase that once run ensures everything works perfectly. The only question is: do you know what it is?"

06

Peter becomes Pete

"You will become a bottleneck in the process… then you'll convert the Outloop framework from Peter to Pete."

On competition

  • "Don't make the mistake of thinking you can outperform your team's best engineering practices encoded into a template meta prompt that kicks off five to ten agents per problem."
  • "There is no competing with an agentic engineer. You must have fleets of agents operating on your behalf to compete with an agentic engineer."
  • "An agentic engineer operates on living pieces of software that work while they sleep."
  • "Your place of competition is now net new innovation."
  • "The big labs are all doing this right now — insane feedback loops where agents iterate against some validation function. If they can do it for world-changing problems, you can do it on your codebase."

On the moment

  • "You are here very early. It might not feel like it, but you're here early."

  • "You only need to catch one wave to change everything."
