The Workout

GOAL Bootstrap a minimum viable agentic layer on YOUR real codebase and prove it with one gateway run and one chained ADW.
PREREQS Any real project you maintain; Claude Code; uv (or bun / shell).

  1. Create the three pieces

    adws/, .claude/commands/, specs/ — strictly outside your app directories.

  2. Write /prime.md and /start.md

    For this codebase; verify in-loop.

  3. Write the gateway script

    adws/adw_prompt.py: takes a prompt string, calls claude -p with stream-json output, logs raw + parsed output to agents/<id>/. Run it with a read-only question about your repo.

  4. Port your templates

    Bring /chore + /implement from TAC-3 with THIS codebase's plan format and validation commands.

  5. Chain two agents

    adws/adw_chore_implement.py: the plan path parsed from agent 1's output, passed to agent 2.

  6. Run a real chore, then answer the question

    Ship one item from your backlog through the chain. Then answer, in writing, today's guiding question: what % of your day was agentic layer vs application layer?

  7. Schedule the layer's growth

    Pick the next problem class (bugs), and the trigger you'll add — cron file-watcher, GitHub webhook, or a task board.

DONE WHEN A real chore in your real repo ships via the chained ADW with logs under agents/, and your agentic layer directories contain zero application code.
LEVEL UP Build one of the alternative shapes: a tasks.md + cron trigger multi-agent worktree system, or a Notion / issue-board prototyper — whichever matches how work actually arrives in your life.
Next · Context Window Mastery  →
10 — Vol II · Lesson 1
agentic horizon · elite context engineering

Context window mastery: the R&D framework.

The context window is your agent's most precious, ephemeral, limited temporal resource — and there are only two ways to manage it: Reduce and Delegate.

AH-1R&D frameworkthe core four12 techniquescontext sweet spot
In this chapter 1The Idea 2The Nuggets 3What Dan Did 4The Workout
1

The Idea — Reduce & Delegate

A focused agent is a performant agent.

Obsessively measuring and managing context is what separates agentic engineering from vibe coding. Every one of the twelve techniques in this lesson fits into one or both of two buckets: Reduce what enters the window, or Delegate work into other windows. What accumulates when context isn't laser-focused is context rot and context bloat — and "search and destroy is the real skill in context engineering."

"There are only two ways to manage your context window, R and D: reduce and delegate."

— quote of the lesson
The R&D framework. One window, two levers — every context-engineering move is a reduce, a delegate, or both.
The four levels. "There are three levels of context engineering and a fourth hidden level if you're on the bleeding edge."

Underneath it all sits the Core Four — context · model · prompt · tools. "When aligned with the right model, prompt, tools and the right range of context, we can hit the core four bullseye over and over."

The target is the context sweet spot — "a range of context where your agent performs to its maximum possible capability for the task at hand." Hitting it once is easy; hitting it consistently matters more as you scale to hundreds or thousands of executions.

The sweet spot fails on both sides: too little context and the agent guesses; too much and you get context rot — which is why technique one is always measure.

Dan's future trends to bet on:

larger context windowsbetter effective windowshot-swapped contextmulti-agent delegationspecialized agents everywhere
The sweet spot. Keep your agent in the range where it performs at maximum capability — search and destroy everything else.

The 12 context engineering techniques, beginner → agentic

01

Measure your context

/context + an IDE token counter — "your most important tool for measuring and therefore managing your context window."

02

Purposeful MCP

Delete the default .mcp.json; load servers by hand with --mcp-config + --strict-mcp-config.

03

Priming over memory

"A dedicated reusable prompt… to set up your agent's initial context window specifically for the task type at hand." Prime, don't default.

04

Output styles

Output tokens cost 3–5x input. A "concise done" style cut a 150-token response to 2 — ~99% reduction.

05

Sub-agent delegation

Sub-agents are system prompts with a "partially forked context window." They respond to your primary agent, not to you.

06

Planner / builder

"One agent plans, the other agent executes the plan… an architect and an editor." Prompt chaining across two windows.

07

Reset + prime, not /compact

"After compact runs, do you know exactly what's in your context window every single time? No." /clear, then /prime. "Stop guessing and start knowing."

08

Context bundles

Hook-generated append-only logs per session; /load_bundle remounts a fresh agent to ~70% of the previous state.

09

One agent, one purpose

"There's no better way to control and manage your context window than to ship one thing at a time."

10

System prompt control

--append-system-prompt in print mode; the SDK's custom_system_prompt blows the whole system prompt away — "use this with caution."

11

Primary multi-agent delegation

A /background command boots a full background instance with its own prompt, model, report file. "Compute orchestrating compute."

12

Agent experts

"Fire off specialized agents that are experts at specific parts of your codebase… you have them auto update their own knowledge." Plan → build → improve.

2

The Nuggets

A focused agent is a performant agent.

the lesson's mantra
24.1K
tokens eaten by the default MCP config (~12% of 200K)
23K
more from a bloated CLAUDE.md (~10%)
25%
"boot up five agents over an hour, that's 25% of a context window completely wasted"
5x
"one write costs you up to five times more" — output tokens burn compute and wallet fastest

The game

"Context engineering is the name of the game for high value engineering in the age of agents."

A temporal resource

"Your agent's context window is a precious, renewable, but limited temporal resource."

Measure it

"What gets measured gets managed. So measure your context window."

Or you're vibe coding

"If you aren't actively paying attention to the state of your agent's context, you're just vibe coding — and you'll only be able to tackle the lowest hanging fruit."

The real trick

"Adding the right context is usually the easy part. The real trick is finding the context agentically and then removing and delegating it."

The memory-file paradox

"The claude.md file is incredible for one reason: it's a reusable memory file that's always loaded. Simultaneously it's terrible for the exact same reason."

Always-on only grows

"The problem with always-on context is that it's not dynamic or controllable… the claude.md file only grows." Worst case: contradictory information in the window.

Universal essentials only

Shrink your memory file "to contain only the absolute universal essentials that you're 100% sure you want loaded 100% of the time."

Don't waste the window

"There are many places to be wasteful as an engineer… The context window of your agents is not one of them."

Track the flow

"The flow of information in these multi-agent systems is critical" — if you lose track of a single agent's core four, you're not ready for sub-agents.

Go overboard, then delete

"By the end of your planning phase you should be deleting information that isn't relevant… go overboard, then delete."

Operate the layer

"You want your agentic layer operating your codebase and you operate the agentic layer."

The standing question

"The focus should always be: better agents or more agents?"

Spend, don't hoard

"It's not necessarily about saving tokens. It's about spending them properly."

The escalation

"What's better than a prompt? A prompt chain. What's better than a prompt chain? An agent. What's better than an agent? Many focused, specialized agents."

Do the hard part

"If it's easy, a vibe coder is probably doing it — and that isn't irreplaceable."

NEVER HAND IT OFF"The context window of your agents should not be handed off to any tool or team." /compact "is a bandaid fix for the true problem."
OVERFLOW = DESIGN BUG"No single agent should overflow their 200K tokens and trigger a compact. If it does, you should chop up the task."
3

What Dan Did

The full demo runs in disler/elite-context-engineering — twelve techniques, in order, on a live window.

  1. Measured on boot

    Ran /context: 63K tokens (31%) already gone — diagnosed MCP tools (24.1K) and CLAUDE.md (23K).

  2. Cut the MCP load

    Deleted the default .mcp.json; relaunched loading only Firecrawl (~4–6K tokens, suffix-labeled in the filename).

  3. Swapped the memory file

    claude.large.md → a 43-line claude.concise.md (350 tokens, 0.2%) → 92% free window on boot.

  4. Primed instead

    Ran /prime and /prime_cc — stacked prime commands with purpose / run / read / report structure.

  5. Output-style duel

    Side-by-side Sonnets, same codebase question: default vs a "done" output style — 150 output tokens vs 2.

  6. Sub-agent fan-out

    /load_ai_docs fired ~10 doc-scraper sub-agents to refresh ai_docs/ after a 24-hour staleness check; the primary window stayed at ~9K.

  7. Planner / builder

    /quick_plan (Opus) spec'd a 3-file TypeScript SDK wrapper; a fresh instance ran /build <spec> with zero priming, then ran it with bun.

  8. Reset over compact

    /clear + re-prime instead of /compact (Ctrl+R to inspect the compact summary first).

  9. Context bundles

    Inspected the per-session JSONL trail, then /load_bundle <path> replayed prior state into a fresh agent.

  10. System prompt control

    claude -p with an appended rule — "read in increments of 100 lines" — verified obedience in the bundle (read 100, read 100…).

  11. Backgrounded a plan

    /background — a background instance wrote a Python (uv/Pydantic) SDK-wrapper plan to a report file, renamed on completion.

  12. Agent experts preview

    The hooks expert ran plan (with an Expertise section) → build in a fresh instance (one-shot) → improve (reads git diff, updates the expertise sections).

terminal — reduce, then delegate
# measure first — your most important tool
/context

# load one MCP server by hand, override globals
claude --mcp-config .mcp.firecrawl.json --strict-mcp-config

# control the system prompt (print mode only)
claude -p "<prompt>" \
  --append-system-prompt "read files in increments of 100 lines"
.claude/commands/ prime.md # purpose / run / read / report quick_plan.md # planner — writes specs/ build.md # builder — takes only the spec path background.md # boots a full background instance experts/ # hooks expert: plan → build → improve agents/context-bundles/ # append-only JSONL per session agents/hook_logs/ # <session_id>/<hook_name>.jsonl ai_docs/ # refreshed by ~10 doc-scraper sub-agents specs/ # plans in, builds out
RESULTFrom 31% consumed on boot to 92% free — before a single prompt of real work.
4

The Workout

GOALCut your Claude Code boot context by >50% and replace always-on memory with priming, then delegate one job to a background context window.
PREREQUISITESClaude Code installed; any repo with a .mcp.json and/or CLAUDE.md; an IDE token counter extension.

  1. Baseline

    Run claude then /context. Record: total %, MCP tokens, memory-file tokens.

  2. Reduce MCP

    Move .mcp.json to .mcp.firecrawl.json (or per-server files). Relaunch with --mcp-config … --strict-mcp-config; re-run /context and confirm the drop.

  3. Trim the memory file

    Cut CLAUDE.md to <50 lines of universal essentials. Move everything else into .claude/commands/prime.md (Purpose / Run / Read / Report) plus a scoped prime_<area>.md.

  4. Control output

    Create .claude/output-styles/concise-done.md: "respond only with 'done' on success; explain only on failure or questions." Set it in a settings file; compare output tokens on the same prompt vs default.

  5. Planner / builder

    Write quick_plan.md (writes specs to specs/) and build.md (takes $1 = plan path, early-returns if missing). Plan in one terminal, /clear, build in a second.

  6. Go background

    Write background.md that shells out claude -p "<prompt>" --append-system-prompt "report progress to <report file>; rename file with -complete suffix when done" &. Fire one plan job through it.

  7. Break the compact habit

    On your next long session: /clear then /prime instead of /compact. Note the difference in confidence about window contents.

◴ /compact
  • Opaque summary — "do you know exactly what's in your context window? No."
  • "A bandaid fix for the true problem."
◵ /clear + /prime
  • You choose exactly what loads, every time.
  • "Stop guessing and start knowing."
DONE WHEN/context on boot shows ≥50% reduction vs baseline; the planner/builder pair one-shots a small feature with the builder receiving only the spec path; the background agent writes and renames its report file with zero babysitting.
LEVEL UPAdd a PostToolUse hook that appends prompt/read events to agents/context-bundles/<session>.jsonl, plus a /load_bundle command that deduplicates reads and re-mounts a fresh agent from the trail.
Next · Seven Levels of Prompts  →
11 — Vol II · Lesson 2
agentic horizon · agentic prompt engineering

Seven levels of agentic prompt formats.

The prompt is now the fundamental unit of engineering — a force multiplier for three audiences at once: you, your team, and your agents.

AH-2stakeholder trifectaLego-block sectionsS-tier workflow7 levels
In this chapter 1The Idea 2The Nuggets 3What Dan Did 4The Workout
1

The Idea — the Ladder & the Lego Blocks

"We're now engineering for three audiences: you, your team, your agents. This is the stakeholder trifecta for the age of agents."

Master seven escalating prompt formats built from consistent, swappable Lego-block sections — with the workflow section as the S-tier pillar. The on-ramp is simple: "Three times marks a pattern — copy whatever you're doing and just write it as a high level prompt, then move up the levels."

The stakeholder trifecta. Every prompt you invest in multiplies for all three at once.
The seven-level ladder. "The high level prompt is a great place to start. It's a terrible place to end."
L1

High Level Prompt

"The reusable ad hoc static prompt" — the one-off you throw into a file when work repeats. 1–3 sections: Title, High Level Prompt, Purpose.

L2

Workflow Prompt

"Easily the most important one" — a sequential list of tasks the agent executes. Adds Metadata, Variables, Workflow, Report.

L3

Control Flow Prompt

The workflow gains "conditions, loops, and early returns" — e.g. "if no path to plan is provided, immediately stop and ask."

L4

Delegation Prompt

"A prompt that kicks off other agents to do work" — "our primary agent is the prompt engineer for our sub agents." S-tier useful, A-tier skill.

L5

Higher Order Prompt

"We are passing prompts into prompts" — a stable top-level scaffold with a swappable payload, e.g. /build <path_to_plan>.

L6

Template Meta Prompt

"The most powerful prompt you can write. It's the prompt that creates your other prompts" — with a Specified Format the agent fills in.

L7

Self-Improving Prompt

"We're having our agents update our agents… by having our prompts update our prompts," via a dynamic Expertise section.

Swappable Lego blocks. Input → Workflow → Output — "a very consistent three-step pattern you can use to think about how to design and build your prompts."

The sections are composable Lego blocks, each graded by Dan on usefulness / skill-to-write. "You can call these sections whatever you want. I'm using the most consistent, most information dense keywords to represent every section."

Title C/DPurpose B/DMetadata C/CWorkflow S/CReportInstructionsVariables A/BContext map C/CExamples ARelevant Files C/CExpertise L7

Variables come in two flavors — dynamic ($1 / $ARGUMENTS) and static in-prompt constants. Examples "can drastically increase your agent's performance." And information dense keywords (IDKs) — words like IMPORTANT — carry extra weight for models.

ONLY WHAT YOU NEED"Only add what you need — these are composable pieces of your agentic prompts."

System prompts

"The big idea is scope and level of persistence. System prompts are rules for all conversations… mistakes scale to every user prompt you run." Key sections: Purpose, Instructions, Examples — Workflow only when you're sure, because "workflows reduce agency to increase determinism."

User prompts

Scoped to one task, one run. This is where the seven levels live day-to-day — and where you'll be "creating, reading, updating and deleting your prompts at light speed."

2

The Nuggets

"The prompt is the fundamental unit of engineering."

— quote of the lesson

"One well-crafted prompt can generate tens and hundreds of hours of productive work. One bad prompt compounds failure at the same rate." The stakes cut both ways — which is why "the winners here are building libraries of reusable battle-tested agentic prompts with composable sections that work like Lego blocks."

Three times marks a pattern

"Copy whatever you're doing and just write it as a high level prompt, then move up the levels."

Start there, don't end there

"The high level prompt is a great place to start. It's a terrible place to end."

The step-by-step play

"The workflow is the step-by-step play of exactly what you want your agent to do."

Great communicating

"Great prompting is great communicating."

Two kinds of variables

"This is a dynamic variable in contrast to a static variable" — statics save you from re-referencing paths everywhere.

The greatest weapon

"Consistency is the greatest weapon against confusion for both you and your agent."

Format = capability

"Every prompt format is about the capability that it offers you and your agent."

Prompts that write prompts

"We're writing prompts that write prompts" — "design agent prompts: create detailed self-contained prompts for each agent."

Where you'll live

"Levels one through four will be 80 or 90% of all of your prompts… usually level three, level four is all you need."

The winners

"Engineers creating more and better prompts at scale are the winners here."

Agency vs determinism

"Workflows reduce agency to increase determinism."

The whole job

"A bad system prompt is bad instructions for the whole job." The system prompt is "one order of magnitude — really more like two or three" more important than a user prompt.

CRUD at light speed

"You're gonna be creating, reading, updating and deleting your prompts at light speed."

Asymmetric engineering

"The prompt is the fundamental unit of engineering. Invest in your prompts for the trifecta to achieve asymmetric engineering in the age of agents."

Name with IDKs

"You can call these sections whatever you want. I'm using the most consistent, most information dense keywords to represent every section."

THE COWORKER TEST"If I handed this to a coworker, could they complete this work top to bottom? If that's true, you've probably written a high quality prompt."
3

What Dan Did

Every prompt lives in the agentic-prompt-engineering codebase — and each one was graded live on a tier-list app.

  1. Level 1 — two ad hoc prompts

    all_tools.md (title + high level prompt: list your tools from the system prompt) and start.md (adds Purpose; cd / bun install / bun dev).

  2. Level 2 — the workflow four

    prime.md (metadata, purpose, workflow, report); build.md (allowed-tools, argument-hint, PATH_TO_PLAN referenced throughout); quick-plan.md (dynamic USER_PROMPT + static PLAN_OUTPUT_DIRECTORY = specs/); prime_tier_list.md (context map + "read these files exclusively" with IMPORTANT).

  3. Level 3 — control flow

    build.md's early return demoed live; create_image.md ($1/$2 args, static vars, an XML-wrapped image_loop, Replicate MCP); edit_image.md (loop + exit-immediately conditionals, no Report section on purpose).

  4. Level 4 — delegation

    parallel_subagents.md (prompt_request + count; fired 5 agents to extract CLAUDE.md-worthy essentials); load_ai_docs.md (24-hour delete rule, a loop calling the doc-scraper sub-agent directly); background.md (delegation + control flow + appended system prompt).

  5. Level 5 — higher order

    build.md reframed: plans from /quick_plan (or chore/feature builders) passed in as the lower-order prompt.

  6. Level 6 — template meta

    meta_prompt.md — the prompt that builds prompts (reads the slash-command docs first, fills a Specified Format) — and a plan_vue_view rapid-prototype planner with a Plan Format template section.

  7. Level 7 — self-improving

    The experts/ hook expert with its dedicated Expertise section — "we're basically updating the context inside of our prompts in a live way."

  8. System vs user prompts

    Distinguished them with the doc-scraper sub-agent (a system prompt with purpose, static output dir, workflow) and the background prompt's --append-system-prompt.

.claude/commands/build.md — levels 2 → 3 → 5 in one file
---
description: Build the given plan
allowed-tools: Read, Write, Bash
argument-hint: [path-to-plan]
---

# Build

## Variables
PATH_TO_PLAN: $1

## Workflow
1. IMPORTANT: If no PATH_TO_PLAN is provided,
   immediately stop and ask the user for it.
2. Read PATH_TO_PLAN and execute it step by step.

## Report
- Summarize the work completed and files changed.
.claude/commands/ all_tools.md # L1 — title + high level prompt prime.md # L2 — purpose · workflow · report quick-plan.md # L2 — dynamic + static variables build.md # L3 early return → L5 higher order create_image.md # L3 — XML image_loop parallel_subagents.md # L4 — prompt_request + count background.md # L4 — background instance meta_prompt.md # L6 — the prompt that builds prompts experts/ # L7 — Expertise, live-updated
4

The Workout

GOALBuild a seven-level prompt ladder in one repo — one prompt per level, all sharing the same section format.
PREREQUISITESClaude Code; a small app repo; the AH-1 workout done (you have prime.md).

  1. Level 1

    all_tools.md: Title + one high-level line — "List all tools available to you from your system prompt in a bullet list."

  2. Level 2

    prime.md with exactly: metadata (description, allowed-tools), Title, Purpose, Workflow (numbered reads), Report. Run it; confirm the agent's todo list mirrors your workflow steps.

  3. Level 2.5

    plan.md: dynamic USER_PROMPT: $1, static PLAN_OUTPUT_DIRECTORY: specs/; reference both variables at least twice in the workflow.

  4. Level 3

    build.md: PATH_TO_PLAN: $1; first workflow line: "IMPORTANT: If no PATH_TO_PLAN is provided, immediately stop and ask the user for it." Test by running /build with no args.

  5. Level 4

    parallel_subagents.md: PROMPT_REQUEST: $1, COUNT: $2; workflow step "design agent prompts — create detailed self-contained prompts for each agent, define clear output expectations." Run with count 3 on a research question.

  6. Level 5

    Chain them: /plan "add X" → copy the spec path → /build specs/<file>.md.

  7. Level 6

    meta_prompt.md: takes a request, reads the slash-command docs, emits a new command file matching your exact level-2 Specified Format. Use it to generate a /test.md prompt.

  8. Level 7

    Add an Expertise section to one prompt plus a self_improve.md that reads git diff and updates only that section.

"Consistency beats complexity. Consistency makes you faster."

— why every level shares one skeleton
DONE WHENAll seven commands exist and collapse to the same header skeleton; /build early-returns without args; the meta prompt generates a working new command in your format; the self-improve run edits only the Expertise section.
LEVEL UPGrade each of your sections on Dan's usefulness/skill tier list, then delete every section that isn't earning its tokens — "only add what you need."
Next · Custom Agents  →
12 — Vol II · Lesson 3
agentic horizon · building specialized agents

Building domain-specific custom agents.

Out-of-the-box agents are built for everyone's codebase, not yours — the Claude Agent SDK lets you control the Core Four at bare metal, and the system prompt is the most important element of all.

AH-3Claude Agent SDKthe core foursystem prompt is the law8 agents built
In this chapter 1The Idea 2The Nuggets 3What Dan Did 4The Workout
1

The Idea — Better, More, Custom

Every engineer's path runs "better agents, more agents, and then custom agents."

Out-of-the-box agents "are built for everyone's codebase, not yours" — and custom agents push one agent, one prompt, one purpose to its limit. When you build one, keep one habit: "Whenever you see ClaudeCodeOptions, isolate the core four" — context, model, prompt, tools.

The path. This mismatch of generic agents on your specific problems "can cost you hundreds of hours and millions of tokens."
The SDK loop. "You set up your options, you set up your agent, you run your prompt, you then manage the responses."

Two ways to run it: "query is for one-off prompts and the Claude SDK client is for continuous conversations" (with resume / session-id for continuation). Message types arrive as assistant, system, and result — carrying text, tool-use, tool-result, and thinking blocks.

"Your system prompt is the law for your agent." Embed a workflow in it only when you want that exact flow every single time the agent executes.

There are two species of custom agent. Extension agents append the system prompt to extend Claude Code. Complete custom agents overwrite the system prompt and add custom tools — "this is not Claude Code anymore… you change the product."

But don't confuse ambition with scope: "Don't try to reproduce your own engineering agent. Claude Code is that."

Two species. Append to extend; overwrite to build a new product on the same engine.
◴ stay out-of-the-box when
  • You're operating in-loop
  • The workflow is generic and the SDK covers it
  • You're exploring and prototyping
  • A balanced generalist works in 80% of situations
  • The task is short-lived, lightweight, non-repeat, non-domain
◵ build custom when
  • You need programmatic agents
  • You have repeat workflows
  • The problem is domain-specific
  • You want costs down and performance up
  • You need permission checks & safety (hooks)
  • "Most importantly… you want to stay out of the loop"
2

The Nuggets

"The system prompt is the most important element of your custom agents. With zero exceptions."

— the one rule of custom agents

"The system prompt affects every single user prompt the agent runs. All of your work is multiplied by your system prompt." When you read anyone's agent code: "Always look for the system prompt and then look for the custom tools."

Not your codebase

"They're built for everyone's codebase, not yours. This mismatch can cost you hundreds of hours and millions of tokens."

Where the alpha is

"This is where all the alpha is in engineering. It's in the hard specific problems that most engineers and most agents can't solve out of the box."

What builds the agent

"The system prompt is truly what builds the agent."

Everything lands in the window

"Everything that's going into your agent winds up in the context window at some point" — 15 default tools "consume space in your agent's mind."

Descriptions teach tools

"The description of your tool tells your agent how to use it."

Deterministic inside

"In your tools, you're back in traditional deterministic code… do whatever you want, but respond in the proper format."

Can your agent pivot?

"If an error occurs, can our agent pivot?" — give agents information so they can reflect and correct.

Start small

"Don't start with big things, start with small things. Everything large is a few small things that you understand fundamentally first."

You're the limit now

"It's not the models that are the limitation anymore. It's you and I — our ability to wield better agents, more agents, and now custom agents."

Workflows in the law

"You can embed workflows in your system prompt if you're sure that is what you want to happen every single time your agent executes."

Subtract by purpose

"This agent cannot write. It's a QA agent. Why would it need to write anything?"

Don't compete with Claude Code

"Don't try to reproduce your own engineering agent. Claude Code is that." / "In no way do I think you should try to compete with Claude Code."

Start there, don't end there

"You can solve your problems better than any agent can. Out of the box is a great place to start — don't end there."

Caching for free

The SDK "has built-in prompt caching. You will be saving money by using this out of the box."

Teach, don't do

"Agentic coding is not so much about what we can do anymore. It's about what we can teach our agents to do."

HOOKS = GOVERNANCEHooks are "essential for governance, for control, for permissions in all of your agents."
CONCRETE OBJECTS"Whatever response format you return from your agents… create a concrete object" — Pydantic or Zod, validated on every response.
3

What Dan Did — Eight Agents

Eight agents in building-specialized-agents/apps/, all Python + uv run, in ascending capability.

  1. Pong agent

    Overwrote the system prompt — "You are a Pong agent, always respond exactly with Pong." Every prompt → "Pong". ~150 lines, mostly logging. Lesson: total system prompt control.

  2. Echo agent

    Custom @tool (reverse / uppercase / repeat) in an in-memory MCP server via create_sdk_mcp_server; downgraded to Haiku; ClaudeSDKClient for a follow-up query — and discovered 15 leftover Claude Code tools still in context.

  3. Calc agent

    Terminal-UI form factor; only two tools (calculate expression, unit converter); a big disallowed_tools block — "these tools do not end up in its context window"; resume session id for continuous chat.

  4. Social hype agent

    Bluesky firehose WebSocket stream: deterministic keyword match → queue → agent sentiment analysis (submit_analysis tool) → conditional notify tool (AppleScript notification + TTS); workflow embedded in the system prompt; max 3 turns.

  5. QA agent

    Codebase question-answering expert; external Firecrawl MCP via a mcp_config path; read-only allowed tools (Task, Bash, Glob, Grep, Read, web search); PreToolUse/PostToolUse hooks blocked reads of .env files live; thinking blocks parsed.

  6. Tri copywriter

    Agent in a web UI; ALL tools disallowed ("tool calling is useless for this use case"); NUMBER_OF_VERSIONS templated into the system prompt; JSON validated by a Pydantic MultiVersionCopyResponse; drag-and-drop file context; 6 variations per prompt.

  7. Custom seven — micro SDLC

    Multi-agent kanban (plan → build → review → ship) over WebSockets; the planner's write-hook restricts writes to the plans directory; the builder keeps the stock Claude Code system prompt; the reviewer appends. Shipped a title change end-to-end from a dragged ticket — "a perfect example of an out-loop review system using the PITER framework."

  8. UltraStream agent

    Two agents on a 42K-token log stream: a stream agent with fine-grained tools (read in 5-line chunks, produce summarized log, create alert) that manages its own context window, plus an inspector agent consuming its produced logs. "You cannot do this without serious prompt engineering with an out-of-the-box agent."

building-specialized-agents/apps/ pong_agent/ # total system prompt control echo_agent/ # custom @tool + in-memory MCP calc_agent/ # disallowed_tools + resume social_hype_agent/ # firehose → analyze → notify qa_agent/ # read-only + security hooks tri_copywriter/ # zero tools, Pydantic JSON custom_seven/ # micro SDLC kanban ultrastream/ # two agents on a 42K log
the smallest possible custom agent — pong
# prompts/pong_system_prompt.md
You are a Pong agent. Always respond exactly with "Pong".

# pong_agent.py — isolate the core four
# options: model=haiku, system_prompt=<loaded file>
query("summarize this codebase", options)  # → "Pong"
READ ANY AGENT"Always look for the system prompt and then look for the custom tools." Two looks and you understand any custom agent.
4

The Workout

GOALBuild three custom agents of increasing capability on the Claude Agent SDK: a Pong agent, a tool-wielding Echo agent, and a read-only codebase QA agent with a security hook.
PREREQUISITESPython + uv (or TS + bun); claude-agent-sdk installed; an ANTHROPIC_API_KEY.

  1. Pong

    Scaffold apps/pong_agent/ with pong_agent.py + prompts/pong_system_prompt.md. Use query() with options model: haiku, system_prompt: the loaded file. Prove that "summarize this codebase" returns "Pong".

  2. Echo

    Define an @tool echo(text, reverse, uppercase, repeat); wrap it with create_sdk_mcp_server; use ClaudeSDKClient and send a follow-up "summarize our conversation" query. Then ask "list your available tools" and count the stock tools leaking in.

  3. Seal the leaks

    Add disallowed_tools for every stock tool your echo agent doesn't need; re-ask for tools and confirm only yours remain.

  4. QA agent

    Overwrite the system prompt (Purpose / Instructions / Examples), allow only Task, Bash, Glob, Grep, Read, and add a PreToolUse hook that blocks any Read whose path matches .env with a concise "blocked: environment file" reason.

  5. Test the hook

    Prompt "read the .env file" → expect a block. Prompt "summarize the key modules, think hard" → expect thinking blocks plus a real answer.

  6. Log everything

    Log every message type (system / assistant / result; text / tool_use / tool_result / thinking) with clean panels — "if you don't know what your agent is doing… it will be hard to improve them."

DONE WHENPong always pongs; the echo agent's tool list contains only your tool; the QA agent answers a codebase question with citations, refuses .env, and reports session cost from the result message.
LEVEL UPBolt the QA agent onto a stream: pipe a growing log file through a stream agent with a read-in-chunks tool, and have a second agent answer questions against its produced summaries — the UltraStream pattern.

"Agentic coding is not so much about what we can do anymore. It's about what we can teach our agents to do."

— quote of the lesson
Next · The Orchestrator Agent  →
13 — Vol II · Lesson 4
multi-agent orchestration · the o-agent

You can't scale one terminal at a time. Meet the O-Agent.

Better, more, and custom agents eventually hit the wall of the interface. The Orchestrator Agent applies the single interface pattern to your fleet: one custom agent that CRUDs, commands, and observes all your other agents.

AH-4single interfaceCRUD your agentsobservabilityout-loop
In this chapter 1The Idea 2The Nuggets 3What Dan Did 4The Workout
1

The Idea

Once you have better, more, and custom agents, the bottleneck moves to you — you can't scale one terminal and one prompt at a time.

Dan's answer is the Orchestrator Agent — "the one agent to rule them all... the single interface pattern applied to your fleet of agents." Crucially, it is itself a custom agent: "its system prompt is completely overwritten, its tools are specialized, it's designed to manage other agents."

It sits at the top of the agent scale framework — "base agents, better agents, more agents, custom agents" → orchestrated agents. "At each step, you scale your compute to scale your impact." And it's the R&D framework at scale: the orchestrator's context is reduced, the work is delegated to primary agents.

The single interface pattern, applied to the fleet. One prompt into the O-Agent becomes create / command / status / delete across primary agents — resting on the three pillars.
01

The Orchestrator Agent

Your unified interface. A custom agent whose only job is to CRUD, command, and monitor other agents — never to do the engineering work itself.

02

CRUD for your agents

Agents at scale: read → create → update → delete. "The best code is no code at all. You learn to delete."

03

Observability

Real-time monitoring of performance, costs, and results. "If you can't measure it, you can't improve it. And if you can't measure it, you can't scale it."

You must treat your agents as deleteable temporary resources that serve a single purpose.

— the quote of the lesson

Consumed vs produced assets. "Every agent should produce some concrete result. Otherwise, what was the point in the first place?" The orchestrator tracks what each agent read and what it shipped.

Agent templates. Sub-agent definitions in .claude/agents/ get promoted into full primary agents — "we've just pulled it up a level... you can't do all this with a sub-agent. You're locked in, it's too narrow."

PITER · OUT-LOOP The orchestrator UI is "an out-loop PITER system": Prompt input, Trigger (HTTP), Environment, Review (observability) — "remember tactic four, stay out the loop."
2

The Nuggets

3×
"With a single prompt, I've deployed three times the compute an engineer working in the terminal has."
16
"One time I had 16 agents operating, completing work at absurd scales."
50%+
of your time, as a rough guideline, invested into your agentic layer (tactic eight)
01

Lose track, lose control

"At scale, if you lose track of your agents, you lose control of your results."

02

Your agents set your pace

"The rate at which you can create and command your agents becomes the constraint of your engineering output. When your agents are slow, you're slow."

03

Ten wrong agents

"If you have 10 agents doing the wrong thing, does it matter that you have 10?"

04

Orchestrate, don't work

"We don't want our orchestrator agent getting involved in the actual work. It is just here to orchestrate."

05

Glue beats handoffs

"The orchestrator is now glue for the workflow. I think this is vastly superior to agent handoffs... superior to blind sub-agents."

06

Send them home

"Force it to focus and then let it go home back to the data center. Delete it."

"200K context window is plenty. You're just stuffing a single agent with too much work, just like your boss did to you at your last job."

— IndyDevDan
  • "Our orchestrator agent takes our high-level prompt and then writes out in much more detail the actual concrete work" — "it is doing some serious prompt engineering."
  • "These aren't just one-off sub-agents where the context is blown away... These are primary agents that we can tap into over and over until that specific job is done."
  • "If you do not adopt your agent's perspective, if you do not know what they can do, you do not know what you can do."
  • "On top of every feature any lab builds, any UI you see — it's all just the core four. Don't let anyone confuse you."
  • "Once you build a custom agent, you can then write prompts specialized for that custom agent" — the orchestration prompt: plan-with-scouts → build → review.
  • "If you can do your job with an out-of-the-box agentic coding tool, how defensible is your product really?"
  • "If you can deploy a little bit more compute to have more confidence and more trust in your agents, why wouldn't you?"
  • "Specialized tools like this are more powerful than some out-of-the-box cloud tool... designed for everyone's codebase, not yours."
STILL MISSING — DAN'S OWN LIST Human-in-the-loop decision points · better orchestrator management (multi-codebase) · context-window / agent forking ("one tool away") · prompt autocomplete · and ADW integration — "the big missing piece... deterministic code."
3

What Dan Did

The multi-agent-orchestration codebase — and he pointed the system at itself.

A Vue/Pinia front end, HTTP + WebSocket services, a Claude Agent SDK orchestrator service, and a Postgres/Neon data layer. Every demo below runs the orchestrator against its own repository.

multi-agent-orchestration/ frontend/ # Vue + Pinia observability UI services/ # HTTP + WebSocket services orchestrator/ # Claude Agent SDK service .claude/agents/ scout-report-suggest-fast.md # template → primary agent # data layer: Postgres / Neon

  1. Ping, then fan out

    Command-K prompt bar → ping → the orchestrator replies. Then one prompt spun up 3 agents (front-end, back-end, QA) to write an app-structure summary with mermaid diagrams — 6 tool calls: 3× create_agent, 3× command_agent.

  2. Walk the observability UI

    Per-agent name, status, context window, model, cost; responses, tool calls, hooks, and thinking filters; consumed vs produced assets with one-click diffs into the editor.

  3. "list agents and check status"

    The orchestrator used check-status tools (agent name + tail of response logs) rather than reading raw logs — protecting its own window.

  4. "command all agents..."

    With thinking enabled — it reprompted existing primary agents, not ephemeral sub-agents.

  5. "delete all agents"

    Three delete tool calls. The CRUD loop, complete.

  6. Ecosystem interop

    Created an agent to run the /question slash command ("can our orchestrator and agents use skills?" — yes); ran /prime for the orchestrator, with the caveat that it loads the orchestrator's context.

  7. Template promotion

    Created a primary agent from the scout-report-suggest-fast sub-agent template, commanded a regex-field summary; it produced a report doc.

  8. Scout and build

    The orchestrator thought, created a scout + builder, slept in a 15-second check-status loop, then wrote a detailed builder prompt from the scout's report — shipping gray stat pills to the app header.

  9. The full orchestration prompt

    plan_w_scouters → build → review: three phases plus a final report, shipping a responsive-UI feature (collapse the agent list under 650px). Planner at 21K tokens, then builder, then reviewer — all observable end to end.

Scout and build, orchestrated. The O-Agent creates a scout, polls status on a 15-second loop, turns the scout's report into a detailed builder prompt — and deletes everyone when the job ships.
WHY IT WORKS One agent, one prompt, one purpose (tactic six) — applied to fleets. The orchestrator's context stays reduced; the work is delegated.
4

The Workout

GOAL Build a minimum viable O-Agent: a custom agent whose only tools are create / command / status / delete over background Claude instances, with a file-based observability trail.
PREREQUISITES AH-3 workout done (you can build a custom agent with custom tools); the Claude Agent SDK; a target repo.

  1. Overwrite the system prompt

    Create apps/orchestrator/: "You are an orchestrator. You never do engineering work yourself. You create, command, monitor, and delete agents. Every agent must produce a concrete file result."

  2. Implement the four tools

    create_agent registers a row in agents/registry.json; command_agent spawns claude -p with that agent's config, streaming output to agents/logs/ and a produced-files list; check_agent_status returns status + log tail; delete_agent.

  3. Disallow everything else

    All stock tools off except your four (plus TodoWrite).

  4. Ping, then a real command

    "Create two agents, one to summarize the backend and one for the frontend, into docs/*.md; check on them until done; report consumed vs produced files."

  5. Add agent templates

    create_agent can read a .claude/agents/<template>.md file as the new agent's system prompt.

  6. Write one orchestration prompt

    Scout → build → review, phases + a final report format — then run a small real feature through it.

  7. End clean

    Finish every session with "delete all agents" and verify the registry is empty.

the O-Agent's only tools
# CRUD over background Claude instances
create_agent(name, model, system_prompt)
    # registers a row in agents/registry.json
command_agent(name, prompt)
    # spawns claude -p with the agent's config
    # streams to agents/logs/<name>.jsonl + produced-files list
check_agent_status(name)
    # returns status + log tail — tail, not full log
delete_agent(name)
    # the loop, closed

Tail, not full log — the orchestrator's context window is the one you protect hardest.

DONE WHEN One high-level prompt yields ≥2 concurrently-working agents, each producing a concrete file; the orchestrator's own context stays small (verify it never reads full logs); agents are deleted after the job.
LEVEL UP Swap the JSON registry for Postgres + a WebSocket UI showing per-agent status, cost, and produced assets — the three pillars in full: orchestrator, CRUD, observability.
Next · Agent Experts  →
14 — Vol II · Lesson 5
agent experts · act, learn, reuse

One executes and forgets. The other executes and learns.

The massive problem with agents is that they forget — which means they don't learn. An agent expert is "a concrete form of a self-improving template meta prompt": it acts, learns, and reuses its own expertise at runtime, with no human in the loop.

AH-5act · learn · reuseexpertise.yamlmeta-agenticsmental model
In this chapter 1The Idea 2The Nuggets 3What Dan Did 4The Workout
1

The Idea

Your agents forget. An expert doesn't — because it carries its own mental model with it, and updates it after every job.

The expert's secret is a mental-model data structure — an expertise file. And Dan is emphatic about what it is not: "This is not a source of truth... You have a working memory file. You have a mental model, a data structure that you constantly update." "The true source of truth is always the code." He keeps his in YAML with a MAX_LINES cap (1000) that the agent enforces on itself.

Seeding creates version one: "Ideally you run your self-improve prompt and it creates the expertise from blank... rerun it until your agent stops finding new things."

Act, learn, reuse. The build step acts; self-improve validates and surgically updates expertise.yaml; the next plan or question reads the mental model first instead of searching.
01

Act

Take a useful action — e.g. the build step. Meta-agentics act but don't learn, "so they are not agent experts."

02

Learn

Store new information — the self-improve prompt updating the expertise file after the work is done.

03

Reuse

Apply that expertise on the next execution — the plan and question prompts read the expertise file first.

Meta-agentics sit beside experts: "meta prompts, meta agents and meta skills... they help you build elements of the system that builds the system." Dan's rule: "Every codebase must have meta agentics."

The self-improve prompt is the learn step's anatomy: check git diff (conditional) → read current expertise → validate against the codebase → identify discrepancies → update expertise → enforce the line limit + validate YAML (closed loops). Its key lines: "Prioritize actionable, high value expertise over verbose documentation," and "after your thorough search, there may be nothing to do — this is perfectly acceptable."

The learn step, drawn. Conditional git diff in, surgical YAML out — with closed loops enforcing MAX_LINES and valid YAML before the agent may finish.
◵ deploy experts where
  • Billing — Dan's #1: "literally saved me money"
  • Database · DevOps · 3–4-way integrations
  • ML / data science · API (front/back sync)
  • Data types & models across microservices
  • "The higher the risk, the higher the intensity, experts are really great here."
  • Product experts: per-user expertise files powering adaptive, generative UI — "personalization to the max"
◴ skip the expert when
  • The problem doesn't evolve
  • Simple or brand-new codebases
  • "If you yourself do not have a mental model in place... you will build an expert that can't solve the problem because you can't."
  • Known trade-offs: seeding, the "another source of truth" objection, finite context, false expertise, too-granular expertise, too many experts
2

The Nuggets

01

Agents forget

"Traditional software improves as it's used... Agents of today don't. Your agents forget. And that means your agents don't learn."

02

In their DNA

"You don't need to tell an expert to learn. It's in their DNA." — "Real experts don't relearn their craft every time they have a new task."

03

The differentiator

"It first reads this expertise file... It didn't need to go search or find these files. It knew where they were. This is the differentiated factor of an agent expert."

04

Surgical changes

"I get super hyped when agents change just the right things... I wanna see surgical changes."

05

Revenue-catching

"My agentic experts have caught revenue changing details in these systems."

06

Compounding value

"If your product is successful, it will grow in complexity and speciality. As that happens, agent experts become more and more valuable."

◴ memory files
  • "Global forced context that always loads"
  • Same tokens for every task, every session
  • A generalist "that forgets every time they work"
◵ expertise files
  • "Expertise requires breaking rules when the time is right"
  • Loaded by the expert's own prompts, when relevant
  • "An expert that remembers"

One executes and forgets, the other executes and learns.

— the quote of the lesson: "The difference between a generic agent and an agent expert is simple."
  • "Build the system that builds the system. Do not work on the application layer. You're wasting your time."
  • "This mental model is simply put a data structure that evolves over time."
  • "You don't have a source of truth in your mind. You have a working memory file."
  • "Give your agents a mental model by giving them an expertise file."
  • "There is no codebase I create that does not have meta agentics."
  • "Three times marks a pattern" — hence the meta expert that generates whole expert prompt sets.
  • "More compute equals more confidence" — "sometimes if you throw five agents at the problem... you compose that together, you get a better result."
  • "It's their mental model, not yours" — steer toward autonomy over prescription.
  • "Your mental model doesn't infinitely grow... once you get to a certain level, you just forget. That is okay."
  • "Prompt first so that you can execute it by hand manually... then scale it into whatever abstraction you want" — skill, sub-agent, custom agent: "everything becomes the core four."
  • "Do you want a generalist working on your codebase that forgets every time they work? Or do you want an expert that remembers?"
HANDS OFF THE YAML "If there's something we don't like, we're not going to update the expertise file... you wanna update the self-improve step that operates your expertise." — "Don't directly update this expertise file. Teach your agents how to directly update it."

  1. Meta-agentics demo

  2. The database expert

    The question prompt read expertise.yaml first, validated against code, and wrote a report with an ER mermaid diagram. The expertise: "lightweight pydantic models over raw SQL, no heavy ORM," plus file paths, line numbers, query patterns, migrations. Self-improve confirmed the mental model in sync — line-count check + YAML compile as closed loops. Note: Reuse + Learn but no Act step — a partial expert.

  3. The WebSocket expert, full loop

    plan_build_improve chained three sub-agents via the Task tool: plan (80K tokens, expertise-informed), build (61K tokens, 41 tool uses), self-improve with CHECK_GIT_DIFF=true — the expertise gained the new counter event, session state, and line numbers. The feature one-shotted.

  4. Experts at fleet scale

    Through the AH-4 orchestrator: "create three agents and run /experts:websocket:question" — three parallel experts, non-deterministic consumed-file sets, one failed, and the orchestrator synthesized the answer (21 event types). Then it offered "create three more experts using the Opus model... to ultra validate."

  5. The product expert ("Niall")

    A shopping app keeping a per-user expertise data structure (viewed products, cart adds, checkouts) updated on every action; the agent regenerates personalized landing-page sections — generative UI — on return. Opus for generation quality, dropped to Haiku for speed.

The Workout

GOAL Stand up one complete agent expert (Act–Learn–Reuse) for a real domain in your codebase — e.g. your database layer.
PREREQUISITES AH-2 prompt formats internalized; a codebase with a non-trivial subsystem you understand well — you need your own mental model first.

  1. Scaffold the expert

    Create .claude/commands/experts/database/ with four files: expertise.yaml (empty), question.md, plan.md, self-improve.md.

  2. Write self-improve.md — the heart

    Variables, instructions, and the six-step workflow ending in the wc -l line-limit check + YAML validation. See the skeleton alongside.

  3. Seed

    Run /experts:database:self-improve on the empty file. Let the agent invent the structure. Rerun until it reports "nothing to be done."

  4. Write question.md

    "IMPORTANT: question-answering only — DO NOT write/edit files"; "validate the information from EXPERTISE_PATH against the codebase before answering"; report = direct answer + file/line evidence + mermaid where useful. Ask it a hard question; confirm it reads expertise first and skips searching.

  5. Chain it

    plan.md reads expertise before planning; plan_build_improve.md runs Task → plan, Task → build, Task → self-improve true. "DO NOT STOP between steps."

  6. Ship one real change

    Then git diff the expertise file to inspect exactly what the agent learned.

  7. Correct the teacher, not the notes

    If the expertise is wrong or too verbose, do NOT edit the YAML — edit self-improve.md and rerun.

.claude/commands/experts/database/self-improve.md
---
allowed-tools: Read, Grep, Glob, Bash, Edit, Write, TodoWrite
---
# Self Improve

## Variables
CHECK_GIT_DIFF: $1 (default false)
FOCUS_AREA: $2
EXPERTISE_FILE: .claude/commands/experts/database/expertise.yaml
MAX_LINES: 1000

## Instructions
- Think of the expertise file as your mental model,
  not a source of truth.
- Prioritize actionable, high value expertise over
  verbose documentation.
- There may be nothing to do — this is perfectly acceptable.

## Workflow
1. git diff (conditional)   2. read expertise
3. validate vs codebase     4. identify discrepancies
5. update                   6. wc -l line check + YAML validation

The learn step is a prompt like any other — which means your expert improves whenever you improve this file.

DONE WHEN The question prompt answers from the mental model with correct file/line citations; a plan → build → improve run ships a change AND the expertise diff shows only accurate, surgical updates; expertise stays under MAX_LINES with valid YAML.
LEVEL UP Build a meta expert — a template meta prompt that scaffolds question/plan/self-improve/expertise for any new domain — then add a second expert for your highest-risk system (billing/security) and wire both into your O-Agent as agent templates.
Next · Building Agentic Layers  →
15 — Vol II · Lesson 6
building agentic layers · the codebase singularity

Build the ring around your codebase until it runs itself.

Everything in TAC and AH builds toward one moment — the codebase singularity: "My agents can now run my codebase better than I can. I trust them to ship more than I trust myself or my team." The path there is three classes and their grades.

AH-6the agentic layerclass 1 · 2 · 3ADWssingularity
In this chapter 1The Idea 2The Nuggets 3What Dan Did 4The Workout
1

The Idea

The Agentic Layer is "the new ring around your codebase where you teach your agents to operate your application on your behalf as well and even better than you and your team ever could."

The application layer bundles everything app-side — front end, back end, DB, scripts, DevOps, even multiple repos as git submodules under apps/. The agentic layer wraps it all.

And the destination: the codebase singularity — the moment you realize "my agents can now run my codebase better than I can... Nothing ships to production without my teams of agents." "There is an agentic layer that could exist inside your codebase so powerful that your codebase runs itself."

The ring, drawn. You operate the agentic layer; the agentic layer operates the codebase. Each class wraps the last until nothing ships without your teams of agents.
Three classes, twelve grades. From a lone prime prompt to an orchestrator running AI developer workflows — agentic autonomy on top, deterministic raw code underneath.

Class 1 — in-loop agentics

  • G1 · prime prompt / memory files — "the thinnest possible agentic layer you can have."
  • G2 · plan prompts + sub-agents — fetch-docs, test-writer; specs/ for plans, ai_docs/ for pulled documentation.
  • G3 · custom tools — skills, MCP servers, prime commands with tool access: "all three give us the same thing: custom tools that enhance our agent's core four."
  • G4 · closed-loop prompts — code review, reproduce bug, test back/front end. "Request, validate, resolve." "You're effectively adding more compute to get more confidence in your agent's results."
  • G5 · templates — bug, feature, chore, new-API-endpoint: "where you truly template your engineering and teach your agents to build like you do."
  • G6 · prompt chains — "one prompt now references many prompts and/or sub-agents." Context engineering "becomes a requirement" here.
  • G7 · agent experts — mental models. "This is where we start to see your agents solving problems end to end without you" — the doorstep of the singularity.

Class 2 — out-loop

  • G1 · webhooks/triggers + a core agent SDK layer — "you can execute prompts against your codebase from an external service" (Slack, Jira, GitHub, Discord). The PITER framework realized.
  • G2 · AI Developer Workflows — "deterministic code plus non-deterministic agents. You get the best of both worlds": logging, validation, retries between agent steps. "Once you find yourself deleting your prompt chains and replacing them with concrete AI developer workflows... you will be at class two, grade two."

Class 3 — orchestrated

  • G1 · the Orchestrator Agent — "a custom agent that is a lead engineer that can CRUD, command and manage other agents."
  • G2 · Orchestrator Developer Workflows — entire multi-team workflows with open streams of communication: "we can talk to any one of these agents from this workflow at any time."
  • G3 · the orchestrator runs ADWs — agentic autonomy on top, deterministic raw code underneath. The final grade.
T1

Stop coding

Use agents.

T2

Adopt their perspective

See the task as your agent will.

T3

Template your engineering

Teach agents to build like you do.

T4

Stay out the loop

Out-loop systems over babysitting.

T5

Always add feedback loops

Request, validate, resolve — "more compute should equal more trust, not less."

T6

One agent, one prompt, one purpose

Split compound problems.

T7

Target ZTE

Zero-touch engineering — "not a dream anymore. It's not a meme."

T8

Prioritize agentics

The meta tactic: "build the system that builds the system."

The TAC tactics, recapped as the closing framework of the series.

2

The Nuggets

01

The core four's origin

"Compute in its most useful form is in a prompt and a loop with tools — the agent. This transformed the big three into the core four."

02

A prompt beats an MCP

"Skills and MCP servers can both be replaced with just a simple prompt." — "MCPs are very token heavy and we can just prompt engineer properly to get our agents the tools they need."

03

The grade-three trap

"A lot of engineers do get stuck at grade three... their tools are terrible and they won't scale and they're chewing up tokens." — "Designing tools is an entire game on its own."

04

Trust is bought with compute

"Always add feedback loops... More compute should equal more trust, not less. If it's less, you're doing something wrong."

05

Templates make it yours

"When you start building templates, your agent starts operating more like you would — the way you solve a bug is very different from the way you solve a chore."

06

The winning structure

"You want all of the actual application layer code to run under apps/, and if they're coming from a different git repository, set up git submodules. This is the winning codebase structure."

We are now commanders of compute.

— "You're building the machine that builds your product now."
  • "If you have this [a prime prompt], you technically have an agentic layer."
  • "Your agentic layer should be specialized to fit and wrap your codebase... Don't focus on reuse. Focus on making these prompts great for that one codebase."
  • "I would absolutely never build a plan, build, review workflow that cannot be run individually" — composability + isolation for debugging and parallelization.
  • "You can't write a five agent long prompt chain without knowing the context that every agent will have at every step."
  • "Everything is just a context, model, prompt [and tools]. It doesn't matter what it's called... everything eventually becomes the core four."
  • "The composite type doesn't matter" — expert as prompt set, skill, sub-agent, or custom agent: "do you have a specialized agent that can operate specific parts of your codebase better than you can?"
  • "By this point you have to have adopted your agent's perspective. There's no way around that."
  • "That small chore, that small bug — you absolutely are wasting your time [doing it in-loop]. I guarantee you that."
  • "Imagine you are your agent right before you hit enter... could you solve the problem you're going to ask your agent to solve?"
  • "If you cannot encode what you would do into your prompts... you will not get repeated agentic success."
  • "You will not scale your agentic layer if you are stuck in the terminal prompting back and forth."
  • "There are two problems in there and the chance they accomplish both go down" — one agent, one prompt, one purpose.
THE REAL GOAL "The goal here is not to try to climb to the highest class... The goal is to recognize where you are, what pieces you have, what pieces you're missing."
ZTE "Target zero-touch engineering... This is not a dream anymore. It's not a meme."
3

What Dan Did

  2. Walked a concrete codebase per grade

    A graded tour of mock codebase structures — every grade of the rubric made tangible as files on disk (see the tree alongside), from a lone prime.md up to an orchestrator directory with fixer, reviewer, and builder command-level agents and orchestrator tools.

  3. Split the application layer

    Client/server split into two git-submodule codebases under apps/ — while one agentic layer wraps them all.

  4. Closed the series

The Workout

GOAL Grade your own codebase against the class/grade rubric — and climb exactly one grade this week.
PREREQUISITES A real repo you work in daily; workouts AH-1..AH-5 give you the pieces for grades 1–7.

  1. Audit

    Create agentic_layer_audit.md with a checklist row per grade (C1G1 prime/memory → C3G3 orchestrator + ADW). For each: have it / partial / missing, with file paths as evidence.

  2. Identify your current grade

    The highest grade where everything below is solid. Most engineers: C1G3–G4 — "a lot of engineers are probably missing agent experts."

  3. Pick the next grade and build its distinguishing artifact

    → G4: one closed-loop prompt (test_backend.md) with explicit request → validate → resolve steps that loops until tests pass.
    → G5: bug.md — a template with your exact debugging steps, a relevant-files section, and a mandatory validation step; have your agent fill it in for a real bug.
    → G6: plan_build.md chaining your existing /plan and /build — and verify each piece still runs standalone.
    → G7: build the AH-5 database expert.
    → C2G1: expose one HTTP webhook that fires claude -p "/plan_build <task>" against your repo from outside the terminal.
    → C2G2: wrap that chain in a script (deterministic code) with logging, a validation gate between plan and build, and one retry.

  4. Run one real task at the new grade

    Keep the produced artifacts — spec, review file, expertise diff — as your trail of success.

  5. Re-grade

    Update the audit file. Schedule the next climb.

DONE WHEN The audit file exists with evidence paths; one real work item shipped through the new grade's mechanism without you editing code by hand; you can state your class/grade in one sentence.
LEVEL UP Convert your most-used prompt chain into a true ADW (deterministic script + agent SDK calls + logging/validation/retries), then put your O-Agent in front of it — Class 3, Grade 3, knocking on the codebase singularity.

When the agents arrived, something changed. We weren't just AI coding anymore. We started orchestrating intelligence.

— the quote of the lesson, and of the series
Next · CEO Agents  →
16 — Vol III · Lesson 1
multi-agent · ma-1 · ceo agents

Promote your agents from worker bees to a CEO and board.

With a true 1M-token context, an agent harness you own, and expertise that persists, you can build a multi-agent adversarial system that takes your hardest strategic question in as a brief — and hands you back a memo. Uncertainty in, decisions out.

CEO & board1M contextagent expertiseadversarial by designone-shot system
In this chapter 1The Idea 2The Nuggets 3What Dan Did 4The Workout
1

The Idea

These are not coding agents. This is a CEO and a board built to make strategic decisions on your behalf.

The system has a function signature: uncertainty in, decision out. Three nodes carry it. The brief is the input — and the harness rejects any brief missing its required sections (Situation, Stakes, Constraints, Key Question): "We are kind of forcing great prompt engineering as a pattern into the agent harness." The deliberation is the debate — a multi-agent adversarial pattern where perspectives deliberately don't agree, "so we can really flesh out the mental model that we need to make the best decision possible." The memo is the output: a Jeff-Bezos-style recommendation with a decision map SVG, board stances, tensions, risks, next actions, and an ElevenLabs MP3 summary.

And it is a one-shot multi-agent system. Freeform prompting is refused; the only command is ceo begin. "This is not a conversation."

Uncertainty in, decisions out. The three nodes — brief → deliberation → memo — with six opinionated seats debating in parallel under a hard time and budget cap.
The three innovations. Each alone is nice; stacked, they promote agents from workers to decision-makers.

Why now? Three innovations converged. A true 1M-token context window at one flat price — "the core four just got a massive buff" (context, model, prompt, tools). A customizable agent harness — Pi, where "there are many coding agents, but this one is mine." And agent expertise: "Memory is quite vague, but expertise is memory and patterns surrounding a specific problem" — per-agent scratchpad files that persist and grow across deliberations (Dan's production ones run tens of thousands of tokens).

A constraints engine keeps the meeting honest: time (2–5 min) and budget ($1–$5) are enforced in code, and the harness injects "we're over time/budget, wrap it up" straight into the CEO's conversation.

2

The Nuggets

"At this next tier of agentic engineering, it's not just you making key decisions anymore… It's you, your team, and a country of geniuses in a data center."

— the quote of the lesson

This lesson is a tier shift, not a tooling tip.

"We're engineering and solving problems with our agents. We're not just coding anymore." Coding, in Dan's framing, "is just the beginning… it's the lowest hanging fruit." And the 1M window cuts both ways: in-loop, you simply compress less; out-loop, plugged into your systems, "the leverage you can get is now massive."

01

Price the decision

"How much is a high-leverage decision really worth to you?" Size time and budget to the value of the decision, not to habit.

02

Inputs, then outputs

"You must know what's your inputs and what's your outputs" — then design the workflow between them.

03

Spot the fan-out

Whenever a single node goes out to multiple agents, "immediately think multi-agent orchestration."

04

The 250K reality

Around 250K tokens, long-context retrieval starts degrading — but "you don't need perfect retrieval. You need retrieval that doesn't lose the key ideas."

05

Template your engineering

"If you template your engineering, your agents can do exactly what you did."

06

Serious in, serious out

"Don't just write a stupid two-sentence prompt. Think. Give your agents serious information to work with and it will give you a serious result."

07

Prompt engineering lives

"Prompt engineering is very much still alive — don't let anyone make you think that it's not."

08

Same page, every seat

Every board member gets ALL the extra context — product overview, business metrics: "they're all on the same page. This is key."

09

Measure or stall

"Observability is a key element of building powerful agentic systems. If you don't measure it, you cannot improve it, full stop." Full conversation.jsonl plus tool-use logs.

10

Engineer the conflict

You want agents "opposing each other at odds, so that they poke holes in each other's strategy."

11

Model diversity

"Agent model diversity is super super important for creating better conflict and unique opinion" — the one constraint: every model must hold long context.

12

Specialization → trust

"Specialization is the advantage, and specialization once again increases the trust we have in our agents."

"If you get a bunch of Chads on your team, every Chad is going to say 'Yeah, we should use React.'"

— why you engineer diverse, conflicting personas

The seats even argue visually — every agent can generate SVGs "to visually compel the CEO toward their argument." This depth is the moat: "If you're not building specialized agents… and customizing your agent harness, you're in the normal distribution of what everyone else is getting." And "this is where vibe coders have no shot… The awareness just isn't there" — you have to know the primitives, down to the system-prompt and subprocess level.

The result is "micro applications that do one thing extraordinarily well, the agentic way." And the value compounds: as briefs and memos stack per domain, you get "a chain and a stack of decisions… that's where there's real value" — expertise gets more valuable with every brief.

3

What Dan Did

Dan built the CEO-and-board app as a Pi extension — roughly 2,000 lines, one extension file — with its own directory layout.

"With the Pi agent harness you can make your own structures." Each board persona is a markdown file with front matter (expertise, skills, model) parsed by the harness and hot-loaded into the system prompt at boot with runtime variables — over the classic prompt format: Purpose / Variables / Instructions / Workflow / Context / Report. The revenue agent even drew a bull/base/bear multi-year exit plan as an SVG; a tts skill reads the memo aloud via ElevenLabs.
