launch — the whole interface
j ceo        # J = alias for `just` — boots the extension
ceo begin    # the only accepted command. lists available briefs.

  1. Run the acquisition brief

    Mock company BlendStack (D2C supplement-stack builder) gets a $12M cash offer — 11x its $1M ARR — from PE firm Neutra Holdings. Auxiliary files business-metrics.md and product-overview.md ride along to every board member.

  2. Watch the deliberation

    CEO gathers context → updates its expertise file → broadcasts via its converse tool → all six seats respond in parallel → loop repeats while inside time and budget → harness injects "[MAX REACHED]" → CEO demands "final position, one statement each" → end_deliberation → memo written.

  3. Read the memo

    Board recommends accepting the $12M offer roughly 5-to-1, with conditions: a retention-linked earnout and a 90-day knowledge transfer. Moonshot dissents — "reject and run a 30-day investor test."

  4. Prove reuse

    Dan kicked off a second deliberation (an FDA-warning brief), having earlier run a shorts-platform marketing brief (verdict: fix the retention engine first, then YouTube Shorts).

"Nobody in three rounds could name the root cause of five quarters of decelerating growth. And that silence was the signal."

— the memo's TTS kicker
COSTThe entire live deliberation — six seats, multiple rounds, memo, SVGs, audio — ran about $2.50.
The one-shot meeting. No back-and-forth with you — the workflow runs itself from brief to memo, and the constraints engine ends it.
4

The Workout

GOALBuild a one-shot "board meeting" multi-agent decision system: brief in → parallel opinionated debate → memo out, under a time/budget constraint.
PREREQUISITESAny scriptable agent harness (a Pi extension, or Claude Code + headless claude -p subprocesses), a model with long context, and just.

  1. Lay out the three nodes

    mkdir board && cd board && mkdir agents briefs memos deliberations expertise — mirroring Dan's essential nodes: briefs, deliberation, memos.

  2. Write the personas

    agents/ceo.md plus 4 board seats with front matter (model, expertise, skills) and fully overridden system prompts: revenue.md (maximize cash in 90 days), compounder.md (multi-year compounding advantage), contrarian.md (attack assumptions), moonshot.md ("What if we're thinking too small?"). Give each Temperament / Reasoning patterns / Decision-making heuristics sections.

  3. Enforce the brief

    Write briefs/_template.md with required sections ## Situation, ## Stakes, ## Constraints, ## Key Question — and a validator that refuses to run a brief missing any section.

  4. Build the orchestration loop

    CEO reads the brief plus every .md context file in the brief's folder, broadcasts to all board members in parallel (each a separate agent process with a persistent session, preloaded with the same context), and appends every message to deliberations/<id>/conversation.jsonl (from/to/message).

  5. Add the constraint engine

    After every round, append elapsed time and cumulative cost to the CEO's context; at 80% of max, tell it to wrap; at max, force "final position, one statement each," then the memo.

  6. Add expertise

    Each agent reads expertise/<name>.md on boot and appends notes after the meeting.

  7. Run a real brief

    Write a brief about a real decision you're facing (job offer, pricing change, stack migration) with two auxiliary context files; run it end-to-end; read the memo.

DONE WHENA malformed brief is rejected; a valid brief produces a memos/<id>.md containing a recommendation, per-member stances, at least one explicit dissent, and unresolved tensions; conversation.jsonl replays the whole debate; and the meeting self-terminates within your time/budget caps.
LEVEL UPPut different vendors' models behind different board seats (model diversity → better conflict); add an SVG-generation skill so members argue visually; add "backroom talk" (member-to-member messages); grow one agent's expertise file across 5 briefs in the same domain and compare memo quality on brief #1 vs #5.
Next · Lead Agents  →
17 — Vol III · Lesson 2
multi-agent · ma-2 · lead agents

One agent is not enough — go multi-team.

The step past Claude Code is multi-team agentic coding: a three-tier system of specialized agent experts that remember, stay locked to their part of the codebase, and are all driven through one chat interface — so your cognitive input stays flat as the agent count grows.

orchestrator → leads → workersdomain lockingagent expertszero micromanagementtrust & scale
In this chapter 1The Idea 2The Nuggets 3What Dan Did 4The Workout
1

The Idea

The three-tier architecture. "You are just talking to the orchestrator, the orchestrator delegates to the teams, and the teams delegate to their workers." Thinkers get the best models; workers "just need to follow instructions and execute."

Multi-team agentic coding means "specialized teams of agents to outperform the normal distribution of results."

The three default teams map to the three key nodes of building software: planning, engineering, and validation. Discipline comes from a handful of small parts: a shared zero-micromanagement skill ("You're a leader — delegate, never execute"), an active-listener skill ("read the conversation log before every response") installed in all 10 agents, a conversational-response skill deliberately withheld from workers ("we want our builders to be very, very verbose and very detail-oriented"), and the delegate tool — which orchestrator and leads have and workers don't.

Domain locking is the enforcement layer: front-matter rules for "what the agents can and cannot do and where they can and cannot go" — per-path read/update/delete. Planners read everything, change nothing. "No one should be touching the DevOps files and folders except the DevOps agent — this is like the holy grail of operating mid-to-large-size codebases." And every agent is an agent expert: it loads a personal expertise file at boot and updates it after work — "a working mental model about how things work, and this grows over time" — governed only by a ~77-line mental-model skill. Dan never touches the files.

The scaling claim. "Your cognitive input into the system… doesn't need to increase as you increase the total number of specialized agents" — the chat interface plus a single orchestrator is what makes customization scale.
2

The Nuggets

"I am not spending enough tokens, you are not spending enough tokens, and you are wasting time doing things the old way — and now the old way is one agentic coding tool in the terminal, prompting back and forth."

— the quote of the lesson

Dan is blunt about the economics.

"If you're a cost min-maxer and you care about saving money over getting results, this video is not for you." The stance: "We are not afraid to spend to win here. We're not afraid to give our agents all the relevant context they need." And to the $8-per-session objection: "You're thinking about right now. You're not thinking far enough into the future. Models will improve, costs are going down."

01

The premise

"One agent is not enough."

02

Experts, not amnesiacs

"You stopped using agents that forget and you started using agent experts."

03

Blocks are features

A lead stepping out of its domain and getting blocked "might seem like an annoyance or a waste of tokens, but it's actually very valuable" — the block routes work to the right specialist.

04

Where the ball is going

"Always be thinking about where the ball is going, not where it is." Three converging trends: rising intelligence + bigger context windows, agent expertise, customizable agent harnesses.

05

Multi-perspective answers

Ask all teams one question and you get consensus and split recommendations surfaced — "a multi-perspective answer."

06

Everyone hears everything

Every agent can see the conversation (session JSONL) — "every agent is aware of the conversation happening."

07

Leads never build

"The lead cannot do work. It is a thinker. It's a planner. It's a coordinator."

08

The edge moved

"The way that you're building your agentic coding system is the edge now."

09

Tier your models

Leads and orchestrator on Opus, workers on Sonnet — tune the tier-to-model mapping; "if you're going all out, you'll make everything the top best possible model."

10

Compounding memory

"Every time I boot this up, my agent teams, down to the individual workers, has a stacking, compounding set of memory."

11

Let go

"You can't have your fingers on everything… You have to let go. You have to teach your agents to operate as you would." The mental-model file is the prime example.

12

Guidelines, not shackles

Don't be over-rigid with agent experts — give guidelines (a 77-line skill) and let them maintain their own mental model.

13

Experts beat generics

"Agent experts will always outperform a generic agent" — the same reason you trust the engineer who's done it over and over.

14

Specific beats sprawling

"This isn't just Claude capturing memory with everything you've ever said to it over years. That's not going to go well. What is going to go well is specialized agents that have specific purposes."

15

Input stays flat

Your cognitive input doesn't need to grow with agent count — the single-orchestrator chat interface is what makes customization scale.

NEXT-GEN IDEASA meta agent / meta team to fine-tune the team itself; team-based reusable prompts ("plan, build, validate — one of the oldest workflows known to agentic engineers"); and read-only expertise (updatable: false) for opinionated knowledge — billing, migrations — no agent may corrupt.

"Can you build systems that build systems on your behalf?"

— the question the whole lesson builds to
3

What Dan Did

Dan re-engineered the Pi harness into a chat room with agent teams.

The footer shows orchestrator, leads, context, and total cost; /toggle workers reveals the full roster — 10 specialized agents. Teams are config, not code: "At any point in time we can just copy our config, change up the teams, and specialize how we need." To prove it, he killed the validation team in the YAML and relaunched.

The working codebase was a prompt-complexity classifier that routes user prompts to high/medium/low-tier models — "You don't want to pay for intelligence that you don't need" — biased toward high as the safe default. j predict "summarize this code base" returned medium with confidences.

multi-team config — teams are one YAML edit away
orchestrator: agents/orchestrator.md
teams:
  - name: planning
    lead: agents/planning-lead.md
  - name: engineering
    lead: agents/engineering-lead.md
    members: [agents/front-end-dev.md, agents/back-end-dev.md]
  - name: validation      # kill or add a team by editing one file
    lead: agents/validation-lead.md
    members: [agents/qa-engineer.md, agents/security-reviewer.md]
a worker's domain block — the holy grail
domain:
  read:   ["**/*"]           # see everything
  update: ["front-end/**"]   # write only your own directory
  delete: ["front-end/**"]

  1. Climb the demo ladder

    ping (orchestrator ping-pong) → "ping each team lead" (two-layer fan-out and compose) → "have engineering present tree structure of the most important files" — the engineering lead hits a domain wall, then delegates to the front-end and back-end devs. Three tiers live on screen.

  2. Ask all teams one question

    "What are two additional scikit-learn classifiers we can test against?" Planner, engineer, and validator teams each delegate internally; the orchestrator synthesizes: LinearSVC + CalibratedClassifierCV unanimous, ComplementNB/SGD split noted.

  3. Run the classic workflow in one prompt

    "Plan, engineer, and then validate — add just commands to test both models (just prompt both)." The plan lead reads all context first, the back-end dev builds, and the QA engineer finds issues the security reviewer missed ("ship it, no issues" vs QA's findings — different context windows, different perspectives). The orchestrator composes. About 18 minutes of team work.

  4. Verify through the hierarchy

    New just commands (predict both, train both, head-to-head eval on the holdout set) — the orchestrator can't run them, so it delegates to the engineering lead, who delegates to the back-end dev.

  5. Walk the internals

    The multi-team YAML (teams/leads/members as paths to system prompts plus a team color); orchestrator front matter (expertise, max lines 10,000, skills); shared vs withheld skills; the domain blocks; runtime-injected variables (session dir, conversation log, teams from YAML); session storage of every tool call and each agent's fully-templated system prompt — and the back-end dev's ~5,000-token self-maintained mental model.

4

The Workout

GOALStand up a three-tier orchestrator → leads → workers team over a real small codebase, with domain locking and per-agent persistent mental models, driven through a single chat entry point.
PREREQUISITESA codebase with at least two separable domains (e.g. frontend/ + backend/, or src/ + tests/); Pi (or Claude Code with subagents as the limited fallback); just.

  1. Write the team config

    multi-team.yaml: one orchestrator (best model) and two teams — engineering (lead + backend-dev + frontend-dev workers) and validation (lead + qa-engineer) — each entry a name plus a path to a system-prompt .md.

  2. Write the shared skills

    Small markdown files: zero-micromanagement.md ("You're a leader — delegate, never execute"), active-listener.md ("Read the conversation log before every response"), conversational-response.md (orchestrator/leads only), mental-model.md (~75 lines: when to read, when to update, how to structure, max lines).

  3. Set the front matter

    Per agent: model (Opus-tier for orchestrator/leads, Sonnet-tier for workers), skills, expertise (path to its mental-model file), tools (only orchestrator/leads get delegate; only workers get write/edit/bash), and domain (workers: read **/*, upsert/delete only their own directory; leads: read-only everywhere plus their expertise dir).

  4. Enforce domains in code

    A tool-call hook (Pi hooks or a Claude Code PreToolUse hook): no matching rule = deny; the blocked message should say who to delegate to.

  5. Run the ladder

    ping → "ping each team lead" → "have engineering present tree structure of the most important files" — confirm the lead delegates instead of executing.

  6. Ship one feature in one prompt

    "Plan, engineer, and then validate: <small real feature>. Add just commands to test it." Watch plan → build → validate flow through the tiers.

  7. Let expertise grow

    Run 3+ sessions; inspect each agent's mental-model file growing; never edit the files yourself.

DONE WHENA lead attempting a file write gets blocked and delegates instead; the front-end worker cannot modify backend files (hard block, not just prompt); one prompt to the orchestrator produces planned + built + validated work with a composed summary; and the mental-model files contain content you didn't write after session 3.
LEVEL UPAdd a read-only expertise file (updatable: false) for a "billing"-style sensitive domain; add a team-based reusable plan-build-validate prompt; swap one worker to a different vendor's model via config; build a meta-agent whose job is critiquing and tuning the team's own prompts and skills.

18 — Vol III · Lesson 3
multi-agent · ma-3 · ui agents

The agent harness is the product.

The Claude Code leak's real signal: models are being commoditized, so the most viable new skill is harness engineering. Dan proves it by pointing his multi-team system at a domain — an infinite UI generation system that builds unlimited brand-consistent UIs. A problem class, not a one-off task.

harness engineeringinfinite UI systemtill-done listproblem classesagentic security
In this chapter 1The Idea 2The Nuggets 3What Dan Did 4The Workout
1

The Idea

"Without the agent harness, there are no agents, no agentic coding… no agentic engineering."

Harness engineering is "one of the most viable skills an agentic engineer can learn." The harness gives you "deterministic code, token caching, agent orchestration, prompts, skills, and of course, model control." The one takeaway from the Claude Code source leak: a $2.5B-ARR business is the harness, not the model — and you can build a domain-specialized harness that captures "fractions of that ARR."

The lesson's demo domain is itself a pitch for the adjacent opportunity, agentic security — "anyone can sit down right now and just write a prompt to exploit an application." The flagship brand, Aegis, is an agent security command center.

The harness is the product. Everything above the model line is yours to engineer — and it's where the $2.5B-ARR business actually lives.
The infinite UI system. Brand → product/application → tree → branch/leaf versions. "Design a system that allows us to build infinite UIs within a consistent brand design."

"When you stop vibe coding and you start agentic engineering teams of agents in your agent harness, you can solve problem classes, not just one-off tasks."

It's the same three tiers as MA-2 — "Leads don't work, orchestrators don't work — they think, they plan, and then they delegate the building" — but the workers are domain specialists now: scaffold worker, view generator, animation specialist, brand analyst, soft/hard validators. Each is path-scoped by a domain block in front matter, and each obeys one agent, one prompt, one purpose: "Specialization and that focused context window… is a massive advantage in these multi-agent team systems."

The big three themes of the lesson: "harness engineering, multi-agent orchestration, and then trust and scale. Trust and scale really underlines these first two."

A

The till-done list

"Not a to-do list, not a to-done list, a till-done list. Our agents are going to work until all these tasks are complete."

B

Orchestrator as prompt engineer

"I have taught my orchestrator how to prompt engineer" — the orchestrator writes full prompts to teams; leads prompt workers. Meta, then meta again.

C

Claude Code as meta builder

"80% of the time I'm spinning up Claude Code agents to not work on the actual product… I'm building the system that builds the system." In ands, not ors.

D

Input must not scale

Horizontal team scaling behind a single orchestrator: "our input to the system doesn't scale with the number of agents that we have. This directly limits what you can do."

2

The Nuggets

"The Claude Code leak tells us that the agent harness is the product… one of the most viable skills an agentic engineer can learn is harness engineering."

— the quote of the lesson

"Here's the truth of it all: you are the bottleneck. It's not the models, not the tools, it's not the agent. It's you."

The answer is leverage — "I'm scaling my compute to scale my impact" — aimed at a North Star: "We are going to build with agents until we have agents that work while we sleep… agents that operate your product end to end." And under it all, "the theme of 2026 is increasing the trust you have in your agents to do larger scales of work over time."

"The agent harness matters a ton."

"The models are being quickly commoditized."

Beyond the mainstream

"Claude Code is everywhere. It is now the mainstream. If you want to push beyond, the next step is to learn how to harness engineer."

Teams that remember

"Spinning up parallel agents with blank memories is an improvement, but what we really want is teams of agents that remember."

"You observe, you act, you learn, and then you iterate" — what expert teams do that one-shot agents don't.

Don't prompt the build

"There is very little value in prompting your agent to build the thing. What you want… is a team of agents that knows how to build the application on your behalf."

Specialize for trust

"The point of the agent harness is to increase the trust you have in your system by specializing."

Problem classes

"When I sit down to build with agents now, I think about solving problem classes, not just the individual problem."

RESILIENCE, LIVEWhen the MiniMax and Step-flash workers returned nothing: "if one agent fails, another agent can pick up the work" — and the leads broke their no-write rule to finish. "Very team lead-like… if someone doesn't perform, it's up to you to get the job done."

What Dan Did

Dan framed the Claude Code source leak as evidence the harness is the product — then demoed the infinite UI agents codebase, the third Agentic Horizon asset: an implementation of MA-2's multi-team tool pointed at a domain.

The product is a workspace of three brands — Aegis (the agent security command center), plus AgentX and AgentDan — each brand → applications → trees → branch/leaf UI versions, all rendered in a navigable gallery. The existing UIs (threat dashboards, false positives, coverage, performance, deployment control, activity logs) were built by the agent teams in consistent brand style.

multi-team/ # the custom harness structure agents-os/ # open-source model variants expertise/ # 7K-token self-maintained mental model multi-team-config-min.yaml # 9 agents multi-team-config.yaml # full — 15 agents brands/aegis/ # tokens: colors, type, radii apps/<brand>/<product>/<tree>/<branch-N>/
the stored reusable command
/generate <brand> <product> <tree> <count> <prompt>
# standard format: Variables / Instructions / Workflow
One pasted prompt, one product operated. Orchestrator → till-done list → setup team scaffolds → horizontally scaled UI-gen teams A/B/C in parallel → validation loops fixes → three new agent-stream prototype UIs inside Aegis (glassmorphism dashboard, agents-chatting sidebar, timeline view, compact dashboard), brand-consistent.

  1. Paste one stored prompt

    A reusable prompt creates a net-new application: the orchestrator writes a till-done list, then at-mentions the setup team with a full engineered prompt.

  2. Watch the fan-out

    Scaffold worker builds three trees; the orchestrator deploys UI-generation teams A, B, C in parallel — A/B on open-source MiniMax 2.7 and Step 3.5-flash, C on Sonnet — and leads prompt the view-generator and animation-specialist workers.

  3. Survive the failure

    Open-source workers return "no response"; team A and B leads break the no-write rule — "The task must complete. Let me update and write the component myself." Validation flags two variants; the orchestrator re-delegates fixes to teams A and C until done.

  4. Walk the harness

    The custom multi-team/ structure, agents-os/ variants, the expertise directory, min (9 agents) vs full (15 agents) configs, the view-generator's front matter (expertise file, hot-loaded skills, tools, domain), and the /generate command.

"There is very little value in prompting your agent to build the thing. What you want… is a team of agents that knows how to build the application on your behalf."

— why this team operates the product
4

The Workout

GOALTurn MA-2's team pattern into a system that operates a product: an N-variant generator producing brand-consistent artifacts (UI pages) in parallel, with validation and till-done tracking.
PREREQUISITESMA-2 workout completed (three-tier team + domain locking); a static-site or Vite playground repo; a brand token file.

  1. Define the domain model on disk

    brands/<brand>/brand.yaml (colors, type, radii as CSS custom properties) and apps/<brand>/<product>/<tree>/<branch-N>/index.html for generated leaves.

  2. Build the team config

    A setup team (scaffold worker), ui-gen teams A/B/C (view-generator + validator workers), one orchestrator. Domain-lock each ui-gen team so it can only write inside its assigned branch directories — parallel writes can't collide.

  3. Teach the orchestrator prompt engineering

    Its system prompt must require it to (a) write a till-done list of every branch, (b) send each team a complete prompt (brand file path, target dir, brief), (c) keep re-delegating until every item is done.

  4. Write the reusable command

    generate.md with parameters <brand> <product> <tree> <count> <brief> and a numbered Workflow: scaffold dirs → load brand context → write N briefs → round-robin generate across teams A/B/C → validate → fix failures → finalize.

  5. Run it

    /generate <yourbrand> app landing 3 "bold hero with live activity feed" — then open the three generated leaves in a browser.

  6. Chaos-test resilience

    Point team B at a flaky or cheap model; confirm the run still completes (another team or the lead picks up the work) and the till-done list ends fully checked.

  7. Prove the rebrand

    Change one token in brand.yaml and regenerate — confirm all variants pick up the rebrand (no hardcoded values).

DONE WHENOne command yields N distinct, brand-consistent UI variants written by different teams in parallel; a validator catches at least one issue that gets fixed without you prompting; a dead model does not stall the pipeline; zero hardcoded brand values in the output.
LEVEL UPAdd a brand-analyst worker that scores variants against brand.yaml; add model rotation on failure; add a second brand and prove the same team serves both; use Claude Code as your meta agent to improve the team's own prompts — "build the system that builds the system."
Next · The Verifier Agent  →
19 — Vol III · Lesson 4
multi-agent · ma-4 · the verifier agent

Builder plus verifier: every claim becomes an atomic claim.

Every benchmark tests one model in isolation — but the real capability is combinations. The simplest, highest-value multi-agent system is a two-agent builder + verifier pair: an un-promptable observer that decomposes every builder turn into atomic claims, proves each against actual state, and prompts the builder back. It attacks the review constraint head on.

builder + verifieratomic claimsstop hook · unix socketAND, not ORun-promptable
In this chapter 1The Idea 2The Nuggets 3What Dan Did 4The Workout
1

The Idea

"If you're doing things right, you're spending your time planning and reviewing. With the verifier agent, we can improve our review constraint."

The verifier agent is a second Pi agent — auto-spawned, input-locked — that "validates the individual claims the builder agent has made" and "makes sure that what we asked for is what it built." Your normal primary agent is the builder, the verifiable: "the verifier is independent of the verifiable" — it layers on top of whatever your primary agent is. It's a top-down observer: "the builder doesn't know the verifier exists." Only lifecycle ticks (start/stop/error) cross the Unix socket, and the verifier can touch the builder through exactly one tool: verifier_prompt. The loop caps at max_loops: 3, then escalates to the human.

And it holds a special seat in the 2026 theme: "Most agents increase trust or scale, not both. The verifier does both."

The two-agent system. Builder builds; the verifier observes the session JSONL, fires on the stop hook, proves claims, and — when needed — prompts the builder itself.
The art of verification is decomposition. Claims proven one by one; the grade drives a green/orange/red status bar on the verifier's tmux window.

"Everything you prompt into your agents — they're all small axioms… units of information that can be proven true or proven false."

Atomic claims are the unit of trust: evidence beats assertion, and the builder's final message "is a CLAIM, never proof." The verifier proves each claim non-deterministically and deterministically — LLM judgment on top of read-only script ground truth. Its bash is restricted to exactly one read-only script, because "the bash tool is the most dangerous tool you can give your agents… a ticking time bomb." Anything else is "blocked, fully blocked" — "the highest level of control you can give."

And it's un-promptable by design: "I can't prompt this… If something goes wrong, I don't just fire off another prompt. I'm effectively forced to improve one or more of the core four: context, model, prompt, or tools." Every report ends with "What could you not verify?" and "What do you need from me to verify this next time?" — the gap becomes the next thing you template. "This is a compounding system." Finally, run different models on each side: "It's not GPT 5.5 or Opus 4.7. It's GPT 5.5 and Opus 4.7… Stack intelligence. Orchestrate intelligence."

2

The Nuggets

"Quite literally spending 5x tokens to make sure that I spend less of my time… Tokens are cheap. Your time is not."

— the quote of the lesson

"Model performance is no longer the bottleneck. It's you and I. It's our ability to extract value out of agentic systems at scale, safely."

Dan sees two sets of engineers with a widening gap: those "stuck in the loop prompting back and forth" vs those "stacking up code plus agents to outperform either alone." And the antidote to hype: "The winning formula is often much more simple and less hype-filled than you realize. All the top performers do two or three things over and over."

01

Density per action

"One of the highest-leverage things you and I can do is increase the information density per action for agents."

02

Hands off the keyboard

"Our verifier agent has kicked off unprompted… My hands are off the keyboard."

03

Agent prompts agent

"My verifier agent just prompted my builder agent" — feedback mode writes the corrective prompts you'd have written yourself.

04

Verify even the cracked

"There is no harm in having an agent validate that that is true" — even for a cracked model's output.

05

One, one, one

"One agent, one prompt, one purpose. A focused agent is a performant agent."

06

Stack verifiers

"You can stack more than one of these verifier agents" — an image verifier, a SQLite verifier, a Postgres/Mongo/whatever verifier. Specialize and stack.

07

Both kinds of proof

Verifications run "non-deterministically, and… deterministically as well" — LLM judgment on top of read-only script ground truth.

08

Trust, atomically

"I am increasing the trust I have in my agent system by having this verifier agent validate the atomic claims."

09

A flywheel

"This is a positive feedback loop. It's a flywheel." Each report's unverifiable gap becomes the next thing you template.

10

Benchmarks miss it

"Every benchmark is missing the true capability of what you can really do with these models" — they run a single model in isolation.

11

Orchestration, not delegation

"I'm not talking about delegation. I'm talking about multi-agent orchestration… teams of agents that operate better together than alone."

12

Yours, and stable

"There are many coding agents, there are many agent harnesses, but this one is yours… it doesn't change underneath your feet as soon as a new update rolls out."

THE RULE THAT FIREDNo more than 10 distinct text blocks per generated image — "An image should be worth a thousand words. It shouldn't force you to… read a ton of text. That's what text is for."
NEXT ON THE HORIZON"There is a big hole in everyone's agent right now, and that hole is the bash tool."

"Vibe coding is the floor. Agentic engineering is the ceiling that no one has even imagined the capabilities of yet."

— MA-4

"There's no vibe coding this stuff. I cannot just fire a one-off prompt… There's no one-offs. I am forced to template my engineering into my verifier agent." That's the point: "You don't want to be working on your application anymore… you work on the agentic system, the system that builds the system." Because "2026 is the year of trust. Do you trust your agents to do what you ask them to? And then, how much? At what scale?"

3

What Dan Did

Two side-by-side specialized Pi agents: a builder on Opus 4.7 and a verifier on GPT 5.5 — and the verifier harness "takes no input."

The persona's custom front matter carries the whole design: read-only tools plus the one special verifier_prompt, a pinned model from a different provider, a domain, a loop cap, and a bash policy locked to a single read-only script. The report contract closes the flywheel.

.pi/verifier/agents/verifier.md — front matter
---
tools: read, grep, find, ls, bash, verifier_prompt  # no write, no edit
model: gpt-5.5        # AND, not OR — a different provider than the builder
max_loops: 3          # then escalate to the human
domain: …             # bash policy: exactly one read-only script
---
the report contract
## Report
STATUS · CONFIDENCE (PERFECT | VERIFIED | PARTIAL | FEEDBACK | FAILED)
What did you verify?
Claims: verified / failed / unverified
What could you not verify?
What do you need from me to verify this next time?

  1. Demo 1 — the image verifier

    In the builder: run prime, then a GPT-Image-2 skill to generate arch.jpg describing the verifier system's architecture. On builder completion (stop hook → Unix socket), the verifier fired unprompted, decomposed the work into claims — script ran, file exists, right size, is a JPG, visually depicts the two-agent system — found a violation of the max-10-text-blocks readability contract, and prompted the builder itself. The builder regenerated; second cycle: 7/7 atomic claims verified, clean green status.

  2. Demo 2 — the human in the diagram

    Asked for a human figure added ("we are the ones prompting the verifiable, also known as the builder"). Costs on screen: ~4% Opus vs ~23% GPT 5.5 — "spending 5x tokens" to save review time.

  3. Demo 3 — the SQLite verifier

    j sql boots a builder plus a verify-SQLite persona on GLM 5.1. Prompt: "find all SQLite DBs in this repo, break down tables, columns, and relationships." The verifier re-proved every claim through its single allowed script (any other bash call hard-blocked), spent ~2x the builder's tokens, and reported: all claims verified, nothing unverifiable, no feedback needed.

  4. Released it two ways

    A free public repo — disler/the-verifier-agent — and a souped-up members version with all the domain verifier specialists plus the GPT-Image-2 skill.

disler/the-verifier-agent/ apps/verifier/ verifiable.ts # the builder-side extension verifier.ts # the top-down observer cross-agent.ts # the unix-socket bridge .pi/verifier/agents/ verifier.md # the persona — tools, model, max_loops verify_on_stop.md # prompt template fired on the stop hook justfile # just v — builder + tmux verifier, locked input

just v boots the builder in your terminal and auto-spawns the verifier in a sibling tmux window — input locked, with a status bar colored green/orange/red off the confidence ladder. The persona, the on-stop prompt template, and the three extensions are the entire system: the simplest multi-agent architecture in the whole course, and the one that pays back on every single turn.

4

The Workout

GOALRun and then extend the real two-agent builder + verifier system so every builder turn is automatically decomposed into atomic claims and verified — including writing your own domain verifier.
PREREQUISITESThe public repo (local copy at youtube/_repos/disler__the-verifier-agent), the Pi coding agent installed, tmux, API keys for two different model providers, and just.

  1. Read the contract first

    README.md and the persona at .pi/verifier/agents/verifier.md — note the front matter (tools: read, grep, find, ls, bash, verifier_prompt; model:; max_loops: 3) and the confidence ladder (PERFECT / VERIFIED / PARTIAL / FEEDBACK / FAILED).

  2. Boot it

    Copy .env.sample → .env; run /install in Claude Code (or the manual install), then just v — builder opens in your terminal, verifier auto-spawns in a sibling tmux window with a locked input bar.

  3. Feed it claims

    Give the builder a claim-heavy prompt (e.g. "create a SQLite db with users and orders tables, FK between them, seed 5 rows each, report what you did"). Watch the verifier fire on the stop event, read the session-JSONL slice, and emit a ## Report with per-claim verdicts.

  4. Force the loop

    Prompt the builder to do something that violates a verifiable expectation (or manually break its output between turns); confirm the verifier calls verifier_prompt and the builder receives corrective feedback as a follow-up message — up to 3 loops, then human escalation.

  5. Work the flywheel

    Try to type into the verifier window — you can't. Instead, take one item from its "What could you not verify?" section and template it: edit the persona or verify_on_stop.md so the next run can verify it.

  6. Build your own domain verifier

    Copy verifier.md → verify-<yourdomain>.md (e.g. verify-pytest.md or verify-postgres.md); write one read-only script that is its only allowed bash command; pin a different model than the builder's in front matter — AND, not OR.

  7. Clean up

    just clean tears down stale sockets and tmux sessions.

DONE WHENThe verifier runs unprompted after every builder turn; a deliberately false builder claim produces a FEEDBACK-grade report and an automatic corrective prompt to the builder; a bash call outside the allowed script is blocked; and after templating one gap, the previously unverifiable claim verifies on the next run.
LEVEL UPStack a second verifier persona for a different domain; port the pattern to Claude Code with a Stop hook plus a headless claude -p verifier reading the session transcript; add a rule contract of your own (like the 10-text-block image rule) and watch it get enforced; instrument token spend to see your own tokens-for-time ratio.

Nine videos, one spine: the Big Three — context, model, prompt.

From July 14 to September 8, 2025, IndyDevDan laid the foundation course in public: observe your agents, understand the primitive, delegate, pick your model tiers, eval fairly, raise the information rate, choose the right pattern, leave the chat UI, and finally engineer the context window itself.

1

See everything your agents do

yt-01 · I Can SEE EVERYTHING — Claude Code Hooks for Multi-Agent Observability

Once you scale past one Claude Code instance, observability is everything — hooks streaming every lifecycle event into a one-way client/server pipeline let you "see everything" your agents do.

"When it comes to multi-agent systems, observability is everything." Running a single agent "is just the beginning."

The architecture is deliberately minimal: hooks fire an HTTP POST to a Bun server, which persists to SQLite and streams over WebSocket to a Vue client. "This one-way data stream keeps things really simple." Every hook event is tracked — PreToolUse, PostToolUse, Notification, Stop, SubagentStop, PreCompact — with a session ID and source app on every row.

The whole stack, drawn. Hooks → HTTP POST → Bun → SQLite + WebSocket → Vue. No framework magic, no return path.
01

Color by hash

Hash the app name + session ID into a color so you can visually tell agents apart at a glance — cheap UX that matters at 5–10 agents.

02

Haiku summarizes first

Each event runs a summarization prompt via a small fast model before sending — "thousands of events... less than 20 cents." "This is where small fast models really shine."

03

Stop = the full transcript

The Stop event carries the full chat transcript path — the perfect time to copy the entire log and filter it: all reads, all globs, all assistant responses.

  • Hooks give "deterministic control over Claude Code's behavior" — steer, monitor, control.
  • "Frameworks are irrelevant when it comes to raw productivity. Your agent knows all of them" — the Vue client is disposable; convert it to any framework with a prompt.
  • The codebase-wrapping agentic layer (.claude/, hooks, apps) is a new layer around your code that "will change the way you engineer forever."
  • Endgame: "prompting back and forth, one prompt at a time is not the way to engineer. It's a great place to start. It's a terrible place to finish." Push toward off-device, fully programmable agentic coding.

"If you don't measure it, you can't improve it. If you don't monitor it, how will you know what's actually happening?"

— yt-01, the case for observability
Claude Code hooks (6+ lifecycle events)Bun serverSQLiteWebSocketsVue.jsAstral UV single-file PythonHaiku event summariesTTS notifications

The drill — build it

  1. Wire the hooks

    Clone the repo; wire the .claude/hooks send_event script into one codebase's settings.json.

  2. Confirm the pipe

    Start the Bun server + client; fire a Claude Code session and confirm events land in SQLite and stream to the UI.

  3. Add a second instance

    Add another codebase/instance and confirm both stream in with distinct session colors.

  4. Turn on summaries

    Enable the --summarize flag path so PreToolUse/PostToolUse rows get Haiku one-liners.

  5. Walk a transcript

    Open a Stop event and walk the full transcript from the payload.

REPOdisler/claude-code-hooks-multi-agent-observability — the full hooks→server→client observability stack for multiple concurrent Claude Code instances.
DONE WHENTwo simultaneous Claude Code sessions stream live color-coded events with model-generated summaries, and you can filter by event type and session.
2

A new engineering primitive

yt-02 · How Claude Code CHANGED Engineering Forever (and what's next)

Claude Code isn't a tool or an assistant — it's a new engineering primitive, programmable from any terminal, and betting on that architecture is how you position for what's next.

"Just like deep learning worked, the agent architecture worked."

This is phase 2 of the generative AI age: we're not chatting with LLMs, we're "commanding compute." And the primitive has exactly three essential elements — powerful LLMs, the right tools, and the agent loop. "You can't have one of these. You can't have two of these. You need all three."

The architecture is everything.

— the three-element thesis, yt-02
01

Powerful LLMs

"Benchmark ghosting models": since Sonnet 3.5, Anthropic models show emergent behavior that "doesn't show up in benchmarks... They didn't just pass benchmarks. They made benchmarks irrelevant." The only way to find it: time with the model.

02

The right tools

"You want just the context window" — skip unnecessary RAG; give the model the tool you would use. The terminal is "the highest leverage point for engineers": maximum control over the flow of information, minimal friction.

03

The agent loop, programmable

claude -p from any terminal is a fundamental building block for your own agentic systems. Composability and interoperability are "a key feature of true engineering."

  • Simplicity as a feature: "There's no complexity. It's just the context window... No setup... No friction. It's just the prompt." (Contrast: Cursor as an app that outgrew simplicity.)
  • "Writing code is a fraction of what engineering is" — why vibe coding falls apart, and why agentic coding (not AI coding) was needed.
  • Predictions: multi-agent swarms in ADWs (AI developer workflows); dedicated agent environments — his Mac Mini agent box, and "one of these companies is going to become the new Vercel for AI agents"; codebases refactored while you sleep.
  • The engineering gap widens before it shrinks — a "dark age" between 10x engineers who understand what's underneath and vibe coders who "are just building. They're not learning."
  • Action items: master Claude Code; "more prompts, better prompts, then more agents"; keep agents running longer and longer; "your codebase is too big" = you haven't written the right prompts; close the loop — "write a prompt and then say validate your work with X."

"Focus on principles, not tools, not models. Both of these will change. Principles never change."

— the closing law of yt-02
Claude Code plan modehookssub-agentsclaude -p programmable modeSonnet 3.5 → Claude 4 seriesAider (historical credit)artificial analysis benchmarksMac Mini as agent device
WATCH & ABSORBNo repo — internalize instead: (1) agent = model + tools + loop, and the primitive is programmable via claude -p; (2) benchmark-ghosting — judge models by time spent on real tasks, not leaderboards; (3) sequence your scaling: better prompts → longer-running agents → more agents.
3

Agents building agents

yt-03 · My Claude Code Sub-Agents BUILD THEMSELVES

Sub-agents are tools of delegation for your primary agent — you write their system prompts, they respond to the primary agent (not to you) — and a meta-agent can generate new sub-agents on demand.

◴ the two big mistakes
  • Treating the agent .md file as a user prompt you're sending.
  • Assuming sub-agents report back to you.
◵ what's actually true
  • The .md file is the sub-agent's system prompt.
  • Sub-agents respond to the primary agent — "the flow of information here is absolutely critical."

The description field is the routing contract.

It's what the primary agent reads to decide when to invoke a sub-agent. Encode trigger phrases ("If they say TTS, TTS summary, use this agent") and even how to prompt it ("When you prompt this agent, describe exactly what you want them to communicate"). Raise adherence with Anthropic's information-dense keywords (IDKs): "use PROACTIVELY", "MUST BE USED", "IMPORTANT:". And write the report section explicitly: "Claude, respond to the user with this message."

Flow of information. You → primary → sub-agent → primary → you. Engineer both hops explicitly.
01

The meta-agent

A sub-agent whose system prompt knows how to generate complete new sub-agent files from a description — "my agents are building my agents." Dan has built 50–100+ agents with it. It re-pulls live Claude Code docs rather than trusting stale context.

02

Problem → solution → tech

Workflow discipline, in that order. "Noobs and beginners start with the technology and work backward." Validate the workflow manually in the primary agent first (run the ElevenLabs tools by hand), then encode it into an agent.

03

Context preservation — and the trap

A sub-agent has its own isolated context window, saving your primary's — but "it has only what your primary agent prompts it with": equivalent to a one-shot claude -p call with zero history.

  • Cons to watch: hard to debug (you don't see internal prompts/params); decision overload as agent count grows; dependency coupling in chained agents — "you're going to change the planner agent and it's going to ruin everything else"; and sub-agents can't call sub-agents.
  • Use /all_tools-style listing prompts to discover MCP tool signatures before wiring agents to them.
  • The only rule that scales: keep sub-agent workflows isolated and focused — "a focused agent is a performant agent" (foreshadows yt-09).
  • "If deleting causes you pain, you probably aren't deleting enough."

"Code is a commodity. Your fine-tuned prompts can be valuable."

— yt-03, on what to actually keep
.claude/agents/meta-agentcustom slash commandsElevenLabs MCP (TTS + play audio)hooks-driven TTS on StopCursor for prompt editingClaude docs

The drill — build it

  1. Read the source

    Clone claude-code-hooks-mastery; read .claude/agents/meta-agent.md and one simple agent (hello-world).

  2. Validate by hand

    Pick a real problem (e.g., work-completion TTS summary). Validate the tool workflow manually in a primary agent session.

  3. Generate the agent

    Prompt the meta-agent to generate the new sub-agent; review the generated file.

  4. Tighten the contract

    Sharpen the description with trigger words + prompting instructions for the primary agent; add tool restrictions.

  5. Trigger naturally

    In a fresh instance, trigger it via natural phrasing and confirm the primary delegates correctly.

  6. Trace the flow

    Read the hook logs to trace the primary→sub prompt and the sub→primary response.

REPOdisler/claude-code-hooks-mastery — hooks (all lifecycle events) + sub-agents + meta-agent playground.
DONE WHENA meta-agent-generated sub-agent fires from its description alone, and you can point to the exact primary→sub→primary information flow in the logs.
4

Weak, base, strong

yt-04 · I'm ADDICTED to Claude Code — Rate Limits, Agent Models, and CC Alternatives

With sub-agent model selection you should run a weak/base/strong model stack — the right model for the job — while diversifying away from over-dependence on any one tool as rate limits arrive.

"Weak, base, strong. Three levels of model control."

Model selection solves two problems at once: model overkill (wasted tokens and money) and model underperformance (wasted time). Behind it sits the eternal trade-off triangle — performance, speed, cost — plus a "hidden fourth dimension": rate limits. And when it matters, "performance over everything": "When I want to use the best, I don't hesitate... You pay to play."

Haiku
weak — file naming, moves, quick summaries (CC's one-word status popup is a Haiku call)
Sonnet
base — the workhorse: "what model is responsible for the most code in the world... very clearly Sonnet 4"
Opus
strong — pedal to the metal, + thinking mode for serious/production work

Thinking mode is a second axis on top of model choice: "Sonnet 4 thinking beats Opus 4 base." IDKs: think hard / ultrathink.

01

A/B/C-test with thin wrappers

One shared prompt across models: cryptocoin-analyzer-haiku/-sonnet/-opus all reference the same _agent_prompt.md. Only the model, name, and color change. Test sub-agent prompts at the top level first — run the isolated prompt in a primary agent end-to-end before burying it in a sub-agent.

02

Grade strength by format obedience

Haiku goes off the rails, Sonnet gets close, Opus matches the header exactly. "You can always check for model strength by looking to see if it can obey your output formats." His prompt anatomy everywhere: Purpose / Variables / Workflow / Output format — "Output format... forces your agents to report their output."

  • We're "clawholics" — overexposed, too many eggs in one basket. "If you're an industry leader, you will always be copied and cloned." Diversify (Qwen3 Coder, Gemini CLI, open source) — "but let's be super real, no one is close to Claude Code."
  • Reusable prompts (custom slash commands) + sub-agents = the way to scale compute: prompt-chaining and fusion-chaining with bigger compositional units.

"There's nothing more important in the generative AI age as an engineer than your ability to scale up compute and use it to solve real engineering problems."

— yt-04
sub-agent model: selection@-mentions for agents & hidden filesthinking mode / ultrathinkHaiku 3.5 / Sonnet 4 / Opus 4Gemini Flash/Pro tiersQwen3 Coderrate limits (Aug 28)/crypto-research 12-agent parallel workflow

The drill — build it

  1. Extract the prompt

    Take one analysis-style prompt you use; extract it to _agent_prompt.md (Purpose/Variables/Workflow/Output-format).

  2. Make three wrappers

    Create haiku/sonnet/opus sub-agents that differ only in model: and name, all referencing that prompt.

  3. Fan out

    Write a top-level slash command that runs all three in parallel and writes outputs to outputs/ with model-tagged filenames.

  4. Diff obedience

    Diff the three outputs against your specified output-format headers.

  5. Pin the tier

    Decide the cheapest model that passes, and pin it.

REPOdisler/claude-code-hooks-mastery — includes the crypto research agent teams (haiku/sonnet/opus variants sharing one agent prompt).
DONE WHENYou have a written verdict of which tier the task actually needs, backed by side-by-side format-obedience diffs.
5

Flatten the field, then judge

yt-05 · GPT-5 Agentic Coding with Claude Code

To judge agentic models fairly you must flatten the playing field — a minimal "nano agent" MCP server gives every model the same context, tools, and prompt, then Claude Code (Opus 4.1) plays LLM-as-judge on performance, speed, and cost.

"It's not about a single prompt call anymore. It's about how well your agent chains together multiple tools to accomplish real engineering results."

"A single prompt eval is not enough anymore." The rig is HOP/LOP: a higher-order prompt (grading rubric, S–F grades, orchestration) that takes lower-order prompts (individual benchmark tasks) as input — a reusable eval harness in pure prompts. The nano agent itself is a micro Claude Code: one MCP tool (prompt_nano_agent) wrapping an OpenAI Agents SDK loop with ~7 primitive tools (read, write, list, edit...). Same scaffolding for every model = a fair fight.

The fair fight. One rubric, one task, identical nano-agent scaffolding per model, one judge.
  • Judged as a package (perf + speed + cost), GPT-5 nano/mini beat the giants on simple agentic tasks; judged on raw performance, Opus 4.1 / Opus 4 / Sonnet 4 stay S-tier. Both things are true — grade against the trade-off you care about.
  • GPT-OSS 20B/120B on an M4 Max (128GB, via Ollama/LM Studio): "viable agentic coding happening on device right now... total cost: $0."
  • Instruction-following is the eval: exact response formats, exact file outputs ("MODEL_NAME was here") — Sonnet lost points for preamble; nano nailed the format.
  • "Don't trust any individual benchmark. It's their job to tell you that they're doing a great job... crack open the hood of all these models." Most engineers can't feel what "+2% on a benchmark" means in agentic work — "it's actually massive." Run models on real problems to know.
  • It isn't just the model: "there's a lot of work that goes into something like Claude Code to make it the best... most consistent agent." Keep the SOTA top-level agent; delegate selected tasks downward. The OpenAI Agents SDK is a great way to learn the year's most important architecture — the agent loop — by building one. "The true value proposition of models is being exposed. It's real work end to end."

Atomize your units of compute so you can test them and scale them up.

— every sub-agent independently runnable from the terminal
nano-agent MCP serverOpenAI Agents SDKGPT-5 / mini / nanoGPT-OSS 20B & 120B on-device (M4 Max)Opus 4.1 / Opus 4 / Sonnet 4 / HaikuLLM-as-judgeHOP/LOP orchestrationsub-agents as model launchers

The drill — build it

  1. Configure providers

    Clone nano-agent; configure Anthropic, OpenAI, and Ollama for a local model.

  2. Run the dummy eval

    Run the dummy test HOP/LOP through Claude Code; watch parallel sub-agents fan out.

  3. Verify by hand

    Run the file-operations LOP; manually verify each model's output files.

  4. Write your own LOP

    Pick a task from your real work: read X, transform, write Y in an exact format.

  5. Audit the judge

    Read the grade table; sanity-check one S and one F grade by hand.

REPOdisler/nano-agent — MCP server + eval harness (HOP/LOP under .claude/commands/perf/, per-model sub-agents, eval_results files).
DONE WHENYour custom LOP produces a defensible S–F ranking across ≥4 models including one local model, and you disagree with the judge zero times after manual spot-checks.
6

Raise the information rate

yt-06 · Engineers… Claude Code Output Styles Are Here — Don't Miss This TREND

Output styles — system-prompt-level control of how the agent responds — plus stateful status lines increase the information rate between you and your agents. And the HTML style is the first useful generative UI.

"Never bet on your first initial reaction without doing the work."

Dan thought output styles were useless — until a dev session proved otherwise. They literally swap the system prompt: "I'm really happy that they're being clear about the fundamental unit this is updating." Big Three thinking makes new features instantly legible. Six styles demoed: default, table, YAML, ultra-concise, TTS summary, HTML. YAML "typically outperforms the rest" — highly structured output formats can improve agent performance — and ultra-concise cuts token consumption. Remember: "New features are only valuable in relation to existing features" — output styles × hooks × status lines compound.

"This is the first useful application of generative UI. This is not a gimmick."

— the GenUI style: the agent writes an HTML file per response and opens it, building UI on the fly
01

Better agents → output formats

"Two directions to grow: better agents or more agents. Output formats help us with better agents." Extension idea: conditional branches inside the output-style system prompt to pick the output type per prompt.

02

More agents → status lines

A status line is just a script that prints anything; combine it with a UserPromptSubmit hook writing per-session JSON state to show the last prompt (and trailing 3) per instance. Killer when juggling 3–10 instances: glance and know what each was doing. A local Ollama model generates one-word agent names (5-second timeout, fallback) — small local models for tiny jobs.

  • The /quest question prompt primes your agent while extracting decision-grade info — "ultra valuable for speeding up the information flow between you and your agent," and great for team onboarding.
  • Hot take / warning: Opus-plan-mode and background bash feel like "the beginning of loss of directional focus" — opinionated overreach. The guiding question for any feature: "Do I know exactly what the context, model, and prompt are at every step of the process?" Anything that obscures the Big Three is a red flag.

Increase the information rate between you and your agent.

— the core metric he keeps repeating
output stylesstatusLine in settingsUserPromptSubmit hook + JSON session stateGenUI / HTML generationElevenLabs TTSOllama on-device namingFirecrawl (HN research demo)Opus plan mode (critiqued)

The drill — build it

  1. Install GenUI

    Copy the GenUI output style into .claude/output-styles/; set it, run a /quest-style question, and confirm an HTML answer file is generated and opened.

  2. Wire status line v3

    UserPromptSubmit hook → session JSON → status line script in settings.json.

  3. Go parallel

    Open 3 instances, prompt each differently, verify each status line shows its own last prompt(s).

  4. Name the agents

    Add agent-name generation (local model, or hardcode a fallback).

  5. Roll your own style

    Write one custom output style (e.g., YAML) and compare response quality on the same prompt.

REPOdisler/claude-code-hooks-mastery — output-styles/, status_lines/ v1–v4, session data under .claude/data/sessions/.
DONE WHENThree parallel instances are distinguishable at a glance via stateful status lines, and one prompt produces a rendered HTML (GenUI) response.
7

The five-pattern ladder

yt-07 · 5 Agent PATTERNS to Simplify Your Agentic Coding

There are five agent interaction patterns — ad-hoc human-in-the-loop, reusable prompts, sub-agents, wrapper MCP server, full application — and you climb that ladder left-to-right only as the problem earns it. The bet behind everything: "a majority of the economic value of AI will come from AI agents."

The ladder. "Keep it simple, scale when needed... always go from left to right, never right to left."

  1. Iterative human-in-the-loop

    High control, but "you are quite literally stuck in the loop... you are in fact the bottleneck." Great start, terrible end.

  2. Reusable prompts (slash commands)

    "The 80/20 of agent interaction patterns... write it once, reuse all the time." "Three times marks a pattern" — automate on the third repetition.

  3. Sub-agents

    The decision rule is crisp: "only use sub-agents when you need specialization and parallelization. If you don't need to parallelize and you don't need to specialize, just create a reusable prompt." Every sub-agent is another context/model/prompt to manage — plus Claude Code lock-in, the "gray box" debugging problem, and the primary↔sub prompt flow you must engineer.

  4. Wrapper MCP server

    Build when you must integrate multiple services or expose repeat functionality behind your own interface. "The MCP server is an interface layer." Sleeper feature: MCP prompts — "the real value of the MCP server is in the prompts that represent the reusable workflows of specific tool calls... insanely underrated, insanely underused."

  5. Full application

    CLI + MCP + UI + API — only with a long-term vision or non-engineering consumers. "What's the difference between a customer-facing application and a developer tool? It's the interface layer."

  • "Larger working systems are almost always built from simple working systems." / "Don't overengineer. Let the patterns emerge naturally from usage."
  • Skipping levels 1–2 means "there are things you don't know about the problem... because you didn't do it by hand" — where "by hand" now means with your agent in the loop.
  • For the images-for-a-presentation example he'd stop at level 2, go to 3 only for 100+ images, and 4 only when other services join.

Complexity should be earned, not assumed.

— the master framework of yt-07
Claude CodeGemini CLI (TOML reusable prompts)Codex CLI (dinged for lacking reusable prompts)Replicate MCP serverQwen image editMCP tools / prompts / resources
WATCH & ABSORBNo repo — internalize instead: (1) the five-level ladder and its costs — start ad-hoc, codify on the third repetition; (2) the sub-agent gate: specialization + parallelization, or don't; (3) MCP servers are interface layers whose prompts (not just tools) carry the workflow value. Bonus: classify every automation you currently own into levels 1–5 and check nothing skipped a level.
8

Beyond the chat UI

yt-08 · Agentic Workflows — BEYOND the Chat UI (Claude Code SDK + Nano Banana)

Chat is the lowest-hanging AI interface. File-drop-triggered agentic workflows — Agentic Drop Zones built on the programmable Claude Code SDK — automate repeat, file-based work end-to-end.

"Chat is just the first, simplest, and most overused AI interface."

"They're called agents for a reason. They're capable of agency. Let's lean into the autonomy." The architecture: a file dropped into a watched directory matches a drop-zone config, loads a prompt template, runs an agent via the Claude Code SDK, and streams output — all configured in one drops.yaml. "A lot of engineering work is file-based" — so reuse "the oldest interface known to engineers": folders and files, the file system as UI.

agentic-drop-zones/ sfs_agentic_drop_zone.py # ~700-line single-file app (UV inline deps) drops.yaml # every zone: dir → prompt → agent zones/ echo.md # hello-world zone image_gen.md # nano banana via Replicate morning_debrief.md # audio → Whisper → prioritized notes

"I love when I can pack a ton of value into a single script."

01

Prompt gospel, again

Purpose / Variables / Workflow / Report. "LLMs are good enough to reference your variables throughout the rest of the prompt" — {{drop_file_path}} templating keeps prompts agent-agnostic (avoid $ARGUMENTS lock-in).

02

The demo zones

Image generation (Gemini 2.5 Flash "nano banana" via Replicate), image edit, finance categorizer (CSV → charts + report), morning debrief (audio → Whisper transcript → prioritized notes + extensions + questions), training-data expansion (CSV/JSONL row generation), and echo (hello-world).

03

The killer personal workflow

Morning voice memo → drop → transcribed, formatted, prioritized, extended with ideas and leading questions. Repeat daily, zero chat.

  • "You need programmatic agents" — the Claude Code SDK (Python/TypeScript) is the leader; Gemini/Codex lack SDKs so you're stuck with subprocess. "It's too early to pick a winner... prevent lock-in," but CC has the best support for real work.
  • Use LLMs to generate the input files at scale, then drop them — agents on both sides of the pipe.
  • "Think about the repeat workflows you can now automate with agents" — every drop zone is an encoded, reusable solution.

"The models, the agents, are no longer the bottleneck. It's now a complete skill issue."

— yt-08
Claude Code Python SDKdrops.yamlWatchdog file watcherAstral UV single-file scriptsRich loggingReplicate MCP/APIGemini 2.5 Flash image (nano banana)Qwen image editOpenAI Whisper (tiny)Gemini CLI / Codex (partial support)

The drill — build it

  1. Run echo

    Clone agentic-drop-zones; run the echo zone end-to-end (drop echo.txt, watch the streamed response).

  2. Map the wiring

    Read drops.yaml + echo.md to map config → prompt → agent invocation.

  3. Design a real zone

    Pick a genuine repeat workflow (meeting-notes formatter, CSV categorizer): add a drops.yaml entry + a Purpose/Variables/Workflow/Report prompt.

  4. Prove autonomy

    Drop a file; verify output artifacts + the archive step run unattended.

  5. Stress it

    Have an LLM generate 5 varied input files and drop them all.

REPOdisler/agentic-drop-zones — the single-file drop-zone runner (sfs_agentic_drop_zone.py), drops.yaml, example zones and prompts.
DONE WHENYour custom zone processes a dropped file to finished artifacts with zero chat interaction, repeatably.
9

Reduce & Delegate

yt-09 · Elite Context Engineering with Claude Code

"A focused agent is a performant agent" — context engineering is managing the context window with only two moves, R&D: Reduce and Delegate, applied across beginner → intermediate → advanced → agentic levels.

"There are only two ways to manage your context window: R and D — reduce and delegate."

Reduce #1 — don't preload MCP servers. A default mcp.json ate 24.1k tokens (12% of the window) before work began. Delete it; load per-task with claude --mcp-config <file> (+ --strict-mcp-config to override globals). Reduce #2 — context priming over CLAUDE.md. "A CLAUDE.md file is incredible for one reason: it's a reusable memory file that's always loaded. Simultaneously it's terrible for the exact same reason." Memory files only ever grow (his bloated demo: 23k tokens ≈ 10% of the window). The rule: "only the absolute universal essentials that you're 100% sure you want loaded 100% of the time" — his trimmed file is 43 lines / ~350 tokens. Context priming = per-task-type /prime slash commands (prime_bug, prime_feature, prime_cc...) — "your hot-loading memory file," fully controllable per run.

R&D. Everything in context engineering is one of these two moves.
L1

Beginner · Reduce

Kill the default mcp.json; trim CLAUDE.md to universal essentials; prime per task with /prime commands.

L2

Intermediate · Delegate

Sub-agents are "a partially forked context window" — definitions are system prompts, costing the primary ~122 tokens for three agents. /load_ai_docs fanned out ~10 scraper sub-agents, keeping ~40k scrape tokens out of the primary.

L3

Advanced · Bundles

Hooks append a trimmed log (prompt + reads/searches, not writes) per session; after a context blowout, /load_bundle <path> re-mounts a fresh agent to ~60–70% of the prior agent's state.

L4

Agentic · Background

/background boots a whole background Claude Code instance (via CLI/SDK) with a report file it must write to and rename on completion — "compute orchestrating compute, agents orchestrating agents." Get out of the loop.

  • If you're losing track of a single agent's context, "you probably aren't yet ready for sub-agents" — master one window first. Track every agent's core four: context, model, prompt, tools.
  • The scaling-law justification: model performance degrades as context grows — "it's a safe bet to bet on context engineering."
  • Ladder mantra: "What's better than a prompt? A prompt chain. What's better than a prompt chain? An agent. What's better than an agent? Many focused, specialized agents."
  • Teaser: agent experts (.claude/commands/experts/ with expertise.yaml + self-improve) as potentially the most powerful technique.

"It's not necessarily about saving tokens. It's about spending them properly. We manage our context window so that we don't waste time and tokens correcting our agents' mistakes."

— yt-09
/contextclaude --mcp-config / --strict-mcp-configCLAUDE.md vs /prime commandsdoc-scraper sub-agentsFirecrawl MCPhooks-driven context bundles/load_bundle/background (CLI + Python SDK)experts directoryTAC: in-loop → out-loop → ZTE

The drill — build it

  1. Measure the baseline

    In one of your real repos, run /context and record MCP tokens + memory-file tokens.

  2. Delete and trim

    Remove the default mcp.json; cut CLAUDE.md to universal essentials only; re-measure.

  3. Prime instead

    Write two prime commands (/prime general, /prime_<tasktype> focused) and use them instead of memory.

  4. Bundle sessions

    Wire the context-bundle hook (append prompt + reads to agents/context_bundles/…) and test /load_bundle in a fresh instance.

  5. Go background

    Add a /background command that launches a background CC instance with a report file; kick off a plan while your primary stays free.

REPOagentic-horizon/elite-context-engineering — CLAUDE.md variants (concise/large), prime commands, bundles, background delegation, experts scaffolding.
DONE WHENStartup context is ≥90% free, a fresh agent can resume a dead session from a bundle, and one background agent completes a delegated task with a written report — no babysitting.
✦

The through-line

Nine videos, one chronological arc.

Observe your agents (01) → understand the primitive (02) → delegate via sub-agents (03) → pick model tiers (04) → eval models fairly (05) → raise the human↔agent information rate (06) → choose the right interaction pattern (07) → leave the chat UI entirely (08) → engineer the context window itself (09). Each video assumes you've absorbed the last; the batch is a curriculum disguised as a channel.

A focused agent is a performant agent.

— the law the whole batch keeps landing on

Recurring laws — the batch in eight cards

01

The Big Three

Context, model, prompt — every feature and tool is legible through them.

02

Prompt anatomy

Purpose / Variables / Workflow / Report — used in every repo.

03

Focused > sprawling

A focused agent is a performant agent. Master one window before many.

04

Better, then more

Better agents first, then more agents — in that order.

05

Three times = pattern

On the third repetition, codify it as a reusable prompt.

06

Earn complexity

Complexity should be earned, not assumed. Keep it simple, scale when needed.

07

Get out of the loop

In-loop → out-loop → background, off-device agents.

08

Close the loop

Every prompt should include its own validation step.

1

The Prompt Is the Fundamental Unit

YT-10 · Agentic Prompt Engineering with Claude Code (For you, your team, and your AGENTS)

"The prompt is now the fundamental unit of engineering." The 2–100x returns come from engineering every reusable prompt for the stakeholder trifecta — you, your team, and your agents: "There's a new user... it's your agent."

Seven levelled formats built from swappable Lego-block sections. Workflow is S-tier usefulness at C-tier difficulty — "the most important section you'll write is the workflow" — the play your agent maps straight into its todo list.

Variables in, Workflow works, Report defines what returns.
  • "One well-crafted prompt can generate tens and hundreds of hours of productive work. One bad prompt compounds failure at the same rate."
  • Variables are "super underappreciated" (A-tier): dynamic ($1, $ARGUMENTS) take input; statics are defined once, referenced everywhere — change one line, the whole prompt updates.
  • Metadata and Codebase Structure are C-tier — the latter is really "a context map" that spares the agent burning tokens on search.
  • Instructions are guardrails around the steps — "a lot more valuable for your system prompt versus your workflow."
  • Control flow (level 3) is plain natural language: "If no path to plan is provided, immediately stop and ask the user to provide it."
  • Only add sections you need — composable pieces; no output requirements, no Report section.
  • Delegate prompts (level 4, S-tier): "we're writing prompts that write prompts" — your primary agent becomes its subagents' prompt engineer; nondeterminism becomes a feature when you fan out N agents.
  • "Consistency is the greatest weapon against confusion for both you and your agent." "Consistency beats complexity."
  • "Great prompting is great communicating" — the coworker test: could a colleague run your prompt top to bottom?
  • Information-dense keywords (IMPORTANT, exclusively) when the agent must not deviate.
  • Push to B-tier to "capture most of the value"; levels 3–4 cover 90% — template metaprompts (level 6, "the prompt that builds the prompt") scale velocity hardest.
  • CRUD your reusable prompts "at light speed at the right level" — delegation is the D in R&D (Reduce & Delegate).
slash commandsYAML frontmatter$ARGUMENTSparallel subagentsbackground CC via CLIReplicate MCP demoClaude Code SDK
REPOagentic-prompt-engineering (agentic-horizon) — the 7 levels (High Level → Workflow → Control Flow → Delegate → Higher Order → Template Metaprompt → Self-Improving), working commands in .claude/commands/, a 12-section prompt dictionary, a system-vs-user-prompt guide.

Drill

  1. Prime

    .claude/commands/prime.md as level-2: Metadata, Title, Purpose, numbered Workflow, Report.

  2. Guarded build

    build.md with $1 = path-to-plan throughout; no path → stop and ask.

  3. Static variable

    quick-plan.md with one static output dir in several steps; change it once, the prompt follows.

  4. Delegate

    parallel_subagents.md: prompt-request + count; the primary designs input/workflow/output prompts per subagent.

  5. Verify

    Run all four; each behaves per its Report section.

DONE WHEN/build with no argument early-returns asking for the plan path, and /parallel_subagents <task> 5 fires 5 subagents reporting in your format.
2

Custom Agents Are the Endgame

YT-11 · Agentic Coding ENDGAME: Build your Claude Code SDK Custom Agents

The single path: better agents → more agents → custom agents. Out-of-the-box tools are "built for everyone's codebase, not yours" — the endgame is your own specialized agents on the Claude Code SDK, starting with the system prompt.

Overwrite it and "the Claude Code agent that you know and love is now gone... This is not Claude Code anymore." Two modes — overwrite for a true custom agent, append to merely extend Claude Code. Both valid; know which you're doing.

"The system prompt is the most important element of your custom agents with zero exceptions."

— "all of your work is multiplied by your system prompt"
  • The SDK loop: "you set up your options, you set up your agent... you run the query, and then you handle the response" — all incrementally adoptable.
  • query for one-off prompts; ClaudeSDKClient for continuous conversations with memory.
  • Custom tools ship as an in-memory MCP server (create_sdk_mcp_server) — "the description of your tool tells your agent how to use it."
  • The default toolset leaks: ask "list your available tools" and find ~15 you didn't ask for. "Everything that's going into your agent winds up in the context window... it consumes space in your agent's mind." Fix with allowed_tools.
  • Downgrade the model when the job is simple — the echo agent runs Haiku: "It doesn't need powerful intelligence."
  • "Keep track of the core four [context, model, prompt, tools]... you will understand compute."
  • Micro SDLC: planner → builder → reviewer on a kanban, streaming via WebSockets, on their own codebase — "this work shipped with a single prompt outside the loop" (in-loop → out-loop → ZTE).
  • "If you're doing one-size-fits-all work, use the out-of-the-box agent." Custom agents are for specialized, high-ROI work — "this is where all the alpha is in engineering."
  • "Agentic coding is not so much about what we can do anymore. It's about what we can teach our agents to do."
Claude Agent SDK (Python)override vs appendquery vs ClaudeSDKClientcreate_sdk_mcp_serverallowed_toolsuv single-file scriptsFastAPI + Vue UIs
REPObuilding-specialized-agents (agentic-horizon) — 8 custom agents, one concept each: pong (system prompt override), echo (custom tool), calculator (REPL + resume), social hype (Bluesky firehose), QA agent, tri-copy-writer (structured JSON web app), micro SDLC (kanban), ultra stream (dual-agent logs).

Drill

  1. Pong

    uv single-file script: overwrite the system prompt with three lines ("Always respond exactly with pong."), run query, parse the blocks.

  2. Echo

    One custom tool (message, repeat, transform) via create_sdk_mcp_server, model on Haiku.

  3. Audit the leak

    "List your available tools" — count the baked-ins, strip with allowed_tools, verify context shrinks.

  4. Memory

    Swap query for ClaudeSDKClient; a follow-up summarizes the conversation.

  5. Out-loop

    Run the micro SDLC kanban, drag a ticket to Plan, watch it ship hands-off.

DONE WHENPong answers "pong" to everything, echo runs on Haiku with only your tool exposed, and one kanban ticket ships end-to-end untouched.

Five Bets on the Future

YT-12 · My TOP 5 Agentic Engineering Bets: Anthropic, Custom Agents, & Multi-Agent UI (Not for beginners)

"Every plan is a bet on the future." Five bets for 2026 — where to put your agents, time, money, and compute. The mission behind them: "Build living software that works for us while we sleep."

ChatGPT Pulse validates the two-way-prompt prediction — "at some point the conversation will flip and our agents will prompt us more than we prompt them." And: "In 2026, we're going to see our first compute crunch."

"You, the engineer, are the bottleneck, not the models, not the tools, not the agents... It's a skill issue."

— the thread through all five bets
  • Bet 1 — custom agents. "The 80/20 of software engineering can be summarized in one word: agents." In production they put you "immediately in the top 1% of engineers" — "repeatable problem solving with compute."
  • Bet 2 — deprecate old skills. The pencil: "growth isn't just about writing the next line, but knowing when to erase and start again." "We have guaranteed skill atrophy. Think about the calculator. Think about Google Maps." "It's not about your skills. It's about what you can teach your agents to do."
  • Bet 3 — limit break the constraints. "Planning and reviewing. These are the constraints of agentic coding." Custom agents inside ADWs are "the most powerful abstraction that exists for engineers right now" — "a 1 to 2 order of magnitude improvement" awaits, but "you cannot teach something to do something that you do not know how to do." This filters the vibe coders.
  • Bet 4 — multi-agent UIs + voice. "The chat interface is the laziest and most overused interface for agents." Raise the information rate — voice. "Not another Claude Code wrapper... Everyone's going way too wide. It's time to go deep" — use-case-specific UIs in your own product.
  • Bet 5 — compute maxing. "Unless you have an always-on 24/7 agent solving problems for you on your behalf all the time, you can and should be using more compute."
  • The announcement. Tactical Agentic Coding phase 2 (8 lessons/8 tactics) + Agentic Horizon — "become an engineer that builds the system that builds the system"; guiding question: "What if your codebase could ship itself?"
custom agentsADWsmulti-agent UIsvoice interfacesalways-on agentsChatGPT PulseQwen (cheap-compute bet)

Drill

  1. Name the constraints

    Planning and reviewing — every tooling investment should attack one of them.

  2. See the progression

    Single agent → custom agents in production → always-on compute maxing; each step a deliberate bet whose risks you accept on purpose.

  3. Write your own

    Your own 5-bet plan for the year — "those who plan the future tend to create it."

WATCH & ABSORBNo public codebase — a strategy video, not a build video. The drill is internalization: if these three points don't change where your next month goes, rewatch.
