Composable Commands: Scout, Plan, Build

YT-13 · Claude Code 2.0 Agentic Coding: No, other agents aren't even close.

"CodeX CLI, Gemini CLI, they're nowhere near as close as you think they are... You can't be close to the leader when you just copy their feature set over and over."

CC 2.0's killer feature: slash commands can call slash commands — "You can compose your agentic prompts. Okay, this is huge." One /scout_plan_build chains /scout → /plan_w_docs → /build. The scout protects the planner: "There are only two ways to manage your context window, R&D, reduce and delegate."

Mixed model families on purpose. Scouts emit byte offsets, not just filenames.
  • "Looking through files isn't exactly planning" — keep the planner's window free to actually plan.
  • Manage the "core four" — context, model, prompt, tools — for every agent you spin up.
  • "Prompt engineering... is just as important as context engineering." "I can guarantee you you're not pushing your prompts far enough."
  • Adopt your agent's perspective in high-level prompts — you'll catch what it will miss (the system-prompt-no-longer-default gotcha in the Agent SDK migration).
  • Auto-compact ate 22% of the window ("absolutely cooked") — off via /config for ~91% free. "You want a focused, clear agent with as many tokens as you can spend."
  • Even with delegation the chain burned ~51% — the hard limit of in-loop agents; the escape hatch is out-loop ADWs with isolated windows.
  • Agent device: an /AFK agents handoff sent plan → build → ship to an M4 Mac mini that built and git-pushed three OpenAI Agents SDK prototypes, polling every 60s. "You don't need to sit in the loop for every single task."
  • Rapid-prototyping ladder: build simple, validate, step up — like managing a junior engineer.
  • CC 2.0 truncating output makes it "the tool for everyone, not just the tool for engineers" — countered with a custom Observable output style (tools + diffs + TTS).
  • Higher-order prompts: /build "passes a prompt into a prompt."
  • "There is a direct causal link between your ability to control, build, and use agents, and your engineering output." "Build the system that builds the system" — "What if your code base could ship itself?"
CC 2.0 composable commands/context · /configoutput stylesAgent SDK migrationGemini/Codex/opencode/Haiku scoutsFirecrawl MCPElevenLabs TTSM4 Mac mini
REPOgist disler/c7719b79a107 — one file: the "Observable — Tools + Diffs + TTS" output style (git-diff report, chronological tool-call listing in TypeScript-interface syntax, ElevenLabs TTS summary). The scout/plan/build codebase itself is private course material.

Drill

  1. /scout

    3–4 parallel sub-agents (mix models) write relevant_files.md with offsets.

  2. /plan_w_docs

    Read relevant_files.md, scrape doc URLs into ai_docs/, write a plan.

  3. /build

    A higher-order prompt that executes the plan.

  4. Compose

    /scout_plan_build <prompt> <doc-urls>; run on a small real migration.

  5. Measure

    Auto-compact off; /context before and after to verify the savings.

  6. Optional

    Drop the gist's output style into ~/.claude/output-styles/; wire your own TTS.

DONE WHENOne /scout_plan_build invocation completes the task end-to-end and /context shows your primary agent kept a majority of its window free.

Think in Ands, Not Ors

YT-14 · BIG 3 SUPER AGENT: Gemini 2.5 Computer Use, OpenAI Realtime API, Claude Code

Stop picking one lab. Compose OpenAI Realtime voice, Claude Code coders, and Gemini 2.5 Computer Use browser agents into one voice-orchestrated system — "in order to scale your impact, you must scale your compute."

Provider loyalty optimizes for their business model, not your capability. The ladder: "First we have better agents, then we have more agents, then we have custom agents, and then we need to conduct them and orchestrate them in the best way."

"Loyalty in the generative AI age is not the winning strategy. The winning play is to use them all. Think in ands, not ors."

— voice orchestrator "Ada" commanding coders Sony & Blink
  • Keep orchestration super thin — "basically CRUD against agents" (list, create, command, delete) — agent-agnostic, ready for "whatever tool or model is coming next."
  • Closed loops are the killer feature: Claude Code validates its own work through a Gemini browser tool, getting concrete success/failure back. "Teach your agents how to correct their own work."
  • "If you can't see what your agents are doing at scale, you will not be able to scale your compute" — live hook event stream with cheap-model summaries; "only dive in if something goes wrong."
  • Slash commands are "reusable compute for your agents" — one agent prompts another to run /plan or /build.
  • Outputs loop back into inputs: "Build for you, your team, and your agents at the same time" — expose text AND voice so agents can satisfy the feedback loop too.
  • Boundaries matter: Sony (backend) trampled Blink's frontend because scope wasn't explicit — unclear boundaries produce duplicate code.
  • Make workflows prompt-activatable so your agents can test the tool for you.
  • "Build the system that builds the system" — Dan built the orchestration layer, then prompted it to build the Sora content-gen app end to end.
  • "You, the engineer, are the bottleneck. It's not the tools, it's not the models, it's not the agents."
  • "Voice-based agentic coding is right around the corner" — build fleet-control wrappers now for the edge; keep text workflows for the conditionals.
  • "There is a direct causal relationship between your compute use and your engineering output."
  • Expect failure: the voice agent crashed mid-demo, but session registries and logged work let it restart and relist all agents instantly. "Think in the gray... the people that are experimenting have more answers than the others."
OpenAI Realtime APIClaude Code 2 + Sonnet 4.5Gemini 2.5 Computer Use + PlaywrightSora 2 APIhooks observability/plan · /buildFastAPI + Vue
REPOdisler/big-3-super-agent — voice-controlled orchestrator (big_three_realtime_agents.py, 3000+ lines: GeminiBrowserAgent, ClaudeCodeAgenticCoder, OpenAIRealtimeVoiceAgent) exposing create_agent, command_agent, list_agents, browser_use; agents build a Sora video app in apps/content-gen/ with session registries and hook observability.

Drill

  1. Boot text mode

    Clone, set OpenAI/Anthropic/Gemini keys, playwright install, then uv run big_three_realtime_agents.py --input text --output text.

  2. Observability up

    Run the dashboard (npm install && npm run dev); hook events on localhost:3000.

  3. Two scoped agents

    Create two named coders; one /plans a feature into specs/ with explicit backend-only vs frontend-only boundaries.

  4. Build watched

    The second /builds from the spec — watch both sessions in the dashboard, not the terminals.

  5. Close the loop

    One agent validates via browser_use against the dev server; read the success/failure it gets back.

  6. Go voice

    Retry the whole flow with --voice, typing nothing.

Cheap Fast Compute for Agents

YT-15 · Claude HAIKU 4.5 is LIGHT SPEED Agentic Coding… BUT can it BEAT Sonnet?

Haiku 4.5 is "cheap fast compute for agents" — the skill is knowing which work to offload down your model stack and which still demands Sonnet.

"For every Sonnet prompt you write, you could have written three Haiku prompts" — and caching drops the price further: "this model is cheaper than you think it is." Performance, speed, and cost are "the three dials you and I have as engineers to tweak and tune."

2×
faster than Sonnet 4.5 — ~1s quicker between tool calls
⅓
the price, cheaper still with caching
3%
failure rate — missed 1 of 32 files
½
Sonnet's time on find-and-summarize
  • The 3% miss is "completely unacceptable" in some domains, "more than enough" in others — know yours.
  • Haiku loses instruction detail under complexity: ignored the two-sentence summary rule (2 hits vs Sonnet's 12) and violated an explicit "do not use sub-agents."
  • "Haiku is a fast hard worker, but it won't work for very long" — plans a third to a quarter of Sonnet's length; "Haiku is not a good planner."
  • A "scouter model": search, summarization, structured-data extraction, docs gathering, pattern matching, simple codegen.
  • Build test: a working regex search bar and 10-minute pulse timer shipped — but three new UI themes were created and never wired up. "Would Sonnet have missed this? Absolutely not."
  • Capability passes down the stack each release: "there are problems that Haiku can now solve that only Sonnet or Opus could solve."
  • Build a model portfolio — "an agent model stack, weak, base, strong" — vendor-agnostic; what matters is "the optionality to use the right one at the right time."
  • "To scale your impact, scale your compute" — tool calls are a rough corollary for results, assuming the work is worth doing.
  • The split: Haiku for summarization, docs, simple codegen, patterns; Sonnet for planning, architecture, debugging, long pipelines — Sonnet plans, Haiku executes, Sonnet double-checks on cheap read tokens.
  • Habit shift: "I stop and I first ask myself, do I even need Sonnet for this, or can I drop down to Haiku?"
  • Verdict: "a notch below Sonnet 4" — ~3.5/3.7-Sonnet level, not a replacement.
  • Dogfooding: the dashboard itself uses Haiku 4.5 to summarize hundreds of hook events live — "a perfect use case." "Observability and orchestration is the next frontier for agents" — without it you're vibe coding at scale.
Haiku 4.5 vs Sonnet 4.5hooksultrathinksub-agents · plugins · skillsswim-lane dashboardhigher-order /buildmodel distillation
REPOdisler/claude-code-hooks-multi-agent-observability — real-time monitoring via all 12 hook events: Python/uv scripts POST to a Bun+SQLite server broadcasting over WebSocket to a Vue 3 dashboard with swim lanes, pulse chart, filtering, and Haiku-run event summaries.

Drill

  1. Run the dashboard

    Clone, just start (Bun on :4000, Vue on :5173).

  2. Wire your project

    Copy the .claude directory in; set a unique --source-app in settings.json.

  3. Race the models

    One agentic prompt ("find and summarize all TS files, two-sentence summaries") in two instances: Haiku vs Sonnet.

  4. Compare lanes

    Time between tool calls, totals, completion; diff outputs for missed files and dropped instructions.

  5. Raise the stakes

    Repeat with a planning + /build task; note where the cheap model loses wiring details.

DONE WHENYou can name two task types you'll route to Haiku and two you'll keep on Sonnet, backed by your own dashboard evidence.
7

Skills Are Composition, Not Replacement

YT-16 · I finally CRACKED Claude Agent Skills (Breakdown For Engineers)

Skills don't replace MCP, sub-agents, or slash commands — they're a higher-level compositional unit for packaging repeat, agent-invoked solutions. The prompt remains the primitive underneath everything.

What distinguishes them: agent-triggered + context-efficient + a "dedicated modular directory structure" — "the approach that's new," not the capability. Progressive disclosure is the killer feature: metadata, SKILL.md, on-demand resources. "Unlike MCP servers, which just straight-up tortures context window."

Skills

Automatic, agent-invoked behavior.

MCP

External integrations — Jira, DBs, weather APIs.

Sub-agents

Isolated, parallelizable workflows.

Slash commands

Manual triggers — the "super primitive".

  • "Whenever you see parallel, you should always just think, just jump to sub-agents" — nothing else runs parallel; accept losing that context afterward.
  • Converting all your slash commands to skills is "a huge mistake" — never use a skill for a one-off a prompt or sub-agent can do.
  • The graduation rule: one prompt creates a worktree; managing worktrees (create, list, remove, merge) is a problem set — "this is where one prompt is not enough" — that's a skill.
  • "Do not give away the prompt. The prompt is the fundamental unit of knowledge work and of programming. If you don't know how to build and manage prompts, you will lose."
  • "Every agent is the core four" — context, model, prompt, tools; every feature is built on top of them.
  • Hierarchy: skills at the top (they can use prompts, other skills, MCP servers, sub-agents); MCP lower; slash commands both primitive and composer; sub-agents can't nest sub-agents.
  • The worktree-manager skill's instructions literally say "use the {slash} command tool" — skill composes prompt.
  • Starting out? "Just build a prompt. Don't build a skill. Don't build a sub agent. Don't build out an MCP server. Keep it simple."
  • Biggest complaint: skills "don't go all the way" — no commands/ or agents/ directories inside the bundle for nesting.
  • Reliability: an explicit slash-command sequence runs in order, guaranteed; a chain of five skills isn't (yet).
  • Hot take: "skills are effectively opinionated prompt engineering plus modularity... What's actually new here? I think the answer is not that much" — still a solid 8/10.
  • "Build the thing that builds the thing" — the meta-skill (a skill that creates skills) is "a very, very, very powerful agentic abstraction." Hooks stay the deterministic lifecycle layer — balance agent judgment with determinism (the ADW theme).
SKILL.md + progressive disclosureslash commandssub-agentsMCP servershooks · pluginsgit worktreesSlashCommand tool
REPOdisler/claude-code-hooks-multi-agent-observability — the demo codebase: .claude/skills/ ships the four skills Dan builds (create-worktree-skill, worktree-manager-skill, meta-skill, video-processor); its red/blue/yellow/purple worktrees with port offsets are the skill-vs-subagent-vs-command playground.

Drill

  1. Three ways, one job

    Create a worktree via /create_worktree, via a sub-agent calling it, and via create-worktree-skill — confirm identical trees.

  2. Read the composition

    worktree-manager-skill/SKILL.md — note how it delegates to slash commands.

  3. Force a chain

    One request: "remove red tree, create purple tree with port offset 4, list all trees."

  4. Meta-skill

    Generate a brand-new skill for a repeat workflow of your own (e.g., transcript processing).

DONE WHENThe manager skill auto-triggers from plain English, runs all three worktree operations in order via composed slash commands, and your meta-skill-generated skill triggers in a fresh session.
8

One Agent to Command the Fleet

YT-17 · The One Agent to RULE them ALL - Advanced Agentic Coding

After custom agents: multi-agent orchestration — one orchestrator with CRUD over a fleet of focused, disposable agents, plus real-time observability, so you scale compute instead of your own presence.

"The rate at which you can create and command your agents becomes the constraint of your engineering output. When your agents are slow, you're slow." The orchestrator is "the single interface pattern applied to your fleet of agents" — a decades-old paradigm, applied to agents.

Base → better → more → custom → orchestrator. "At each step, you scale your compute to scale your impact."
  • Three pillars combine: orchestrator as unified interface, CRUD for agents (create/command/check/delete), observability for performance, cost, and results.
  • "If you can't measure it, you can't improve it... If you have 10 agents doing the wrong thing, does it matter that you have 10? Of course not." That's why the orchestrator comes last, after custom agents.
  • These are NOT sub-agents: persistent primary agents you "tap into over and over until that specific job is done" — context isn't blown away.
  • Protect the orchestrator's window: it creates and commands but "is not always observing the logs. It cannot be." It never does the work itself.
  • The core four underlies every agent UI any lab ships: "Don't let anyone confuse you." Monitor the core four of every agent you boot up.
  • "200k context window is plenty. You're just stuffing a single agent with too much work, just like your boss did to you at your last job." Force focus, then "let the agents go home" — delete them.
  • The code journey ends in deletion — "the best code is no code at all" — so "treat your agents as deletable, temporary resources that serve a single purpose."
  • The orchestrator does "serious prompt engineering": your high-level prompt becomes far more detailed per-agent instructions; scout → builder → reviewer chains pass plans, logs, and results along.
  • Sleep-loop pattern: fire agents, sleep, poll check_agent_status every ~15 seconds.
  • "Compute can solve so many of your engineering problems if you put the compute to work" — more compute buys confidence and trust.
  • "These [cloud] tools are designed for everyone's codebase, not yours. When you build something like this, you get specialization all the way down."
  • Every agent "must produce a concrete result. Otherwise, what's the point?" The UI shows consumed vs produced assets per agent; PR-based systems "miss a lot of the story." Trade-off: upfront plumbing buys out-of-loop plus the option to "hop in the loop" — a spectrum of modes.
Claude Agent SDKSonnet orchestrator + Haiku agents8 MCP management toolsFastAPI + asyncpgVue 3 + PiniaPostgreSQL/NeonDBR&D framework
REPOmulti-agent-orchestration (agentic-horizon) — production-ready platform: orchestrator managing a fleet via 8 MCP tools, 3-column Vue UI (agents / event stream / chat), Postgres persistence, per-agent token/USD cost tracking, AI-summarized logs, git-diff file tracking, session resumption.

Drill

  1. Provision

    Clone; free NeonDB Postgres; set ANTHROPIC_API_KEY + DATABASE_URL; run the migrations.

  2. Boot the UI

    ./start_be.sh (:9403), ./start_fe.sh (:5175); open the 3-column UI.

  3. Spin the fleet

    Create three agents (two Haiku scouts split FE/BE, one Sonnet primary); command a codebase summary — watch the 6 tool calls stream.

  4. Chain

    Scout → builder on a small UI change; observe the sleep/poll loop and the detailed prompts written to the builder.

  5. Audit and delete

    List agents, check status, inspect consumed vs produced assets, then delete all agents.

DONE WHENA single high-level prompt spins up multiple agents, produces concrete file artifacts visible in the stream with per-agent costs, and all agents are deleted afterward.
9

Beyond MCP: Raw Code as Tools

YT-18 · Why are top engineers DITCHING MCP Servers? (3 PROVEN Solutions)

"My MCP server just ate 10,000 tokens before my agent even started working." One modest server burns ~5% of context; "stack up two or three more MCP servers, and I'll be bleeding 20% plus context in no time."

Three raw-code alternatives — CLI + prime prompt, file-system scripts, and skills — give the same tool access with progressive disclosure, full control, and a fraction of the cost. The unifying theme: "use raw code as tools." Everything reduces to the core four.

Four implementations, one API. Less garbage context, better agent.
  • CLI approach: a ~25-line prime prompt (/prime_kalshi_cli_tools) reads ONLY a README + the CLI file — "do not read any other Python files" — cutting context from ~10% to ~5.6%.
  • Scripts approach: a README maps "a condition mapped to files — this is a powerful agentic data structure you can use to activate or ignore context" — under 1%, ~2K tokens.
  • The trick: "I will not read scripts themselves unless --help doesn't provide information needed" — progressive disclosure by hand, "and it's not a complex prompt."
  • "Even before context comes prompt engineering… it is THE critical skill for engineers in 2025 and beyond. The prompt shows up before the context gets in your context window."
  • Skills = same scripts, different invocation: "with a skill, the prime prompt is the skill.md file" — model-invoked, self-contained, but "a Claude-specific lock-in mechanism."
  • Anthropic's "code execution with MCP" blog wraps scripts around MCP calls; Dan and Mario Zechner: "you can just cut the MCP server out completely" — benchmarks show "no degradation in quality by going right for scripts."
  • Single-file UV scripts (deps declared at top) buy isolation and portability; the duplication is worth it — "when you have less garbage context, your agent can perform better."
  • MCP still wins on features engineers gloss over — "tools, resources, prompts, elicitation, completion, sampling" — and zero-effort setup for external servers.
  • External tools: 80% just use MCP ("don't waste your time trying to reinvent the wheel"), 15% CLI when you must control tools and context, 5% scripts/skills for serious context preservation.
  • New tools: 80% CLI-first — "CLI works for you, your team, and your agents. The trifecta." 10% wrap that CLI in MCP for agents at scale, 10% scripts/skills.
  • "You can sidestep every single context engineering problem by just focusing your agents on one problem and then you delete them when they're done."
  • Bonus mental model: prediction markets as "info finance" (Vitalik) — agents reading Kalshi order books "help us understand the future before it occurs."
Haiku 4.5 driverFastMCP/context · /mcpprime promptsSKILL.mdUV single-file scriptsclick/typer CLIsKalshi API
REPOdisler/beyond-mcp — four parallel builds of one Kalshi toolset: 1_mcp_server (15 FastMCP tools), 2_cli (13 typer commands), 3_file_system_scripts (10 UV scripts + prime prompt), 4_skill (scripts under a SKILL.md) — plus a trade-off matrix and the 80/15/5 and 80/10/10 heuristics.

Drill

  1. Baseline MCP

    cp .mcp.testing .mcp.json && claude --mcp-config .mcp.json; record /context before any work.

  2. CLI + prime

    Restart without MCP, /prime_kalshi_cli_tools, prompt "kalshi: search markets <topic>"; record /context again.

  3. Scripts

    /prime_file_system_scripts — watch the agent use --help instead of reading script bodies; check /context a third time.

  4. Skill

    cd apps/4_skill && claude; trigger with "kalshi markets: ..." and watch model-invoked progressive disclosure.

  5. Make it yours

    Rebuild on an API you actually use: one UV script + a 5-line prime prompt.

DONE WHENYou have a table of context consumption across all four approaches and your own CLI-or-script toolset an agent can drive without an MCP server.

The Spine Across Nine Videos

One spine runs through the batch: the core four (context, model, prompt, tools) → the ladder (better → more → custom → orchestrator) → "scale your compute to scale your impact" → "build the system that builds the system."

Prompt formats give you the primitive, the SDK gives you custom agents, skills and raw-code tools keep context lean, and the orchestrator turns it all into a fleet.

"What if your codebase could ship itself?"

— the question the whole spine points at

Clone and run first

Highest replication value: YT-18 (beyond-mcp 4-way context A/B), YT-17 (orchestrator platform), YT-15 (drop-in observability dashboard), YT-14 (voice orchestration + closed-loop browser validation).

Rebuild from the transcript

YT-10 (the prompt-format ladder — foundational), YT-16 (skills graduation ladder in the observability repo), YT-11 (pong → echo → SDLC, easy to rebuild). YT-13 is medium: high-value pattern, private codebase, one public gist.

Watch and absorb

YT-12 has no codebase — it's the strategy layer. Its job is to reorder your priorities: attack planning and reviewing, bet deliberately, write your own five bets.

1

Give every agent its own computer

yt-19 · "E2B Agent Sandboxes: The Space to Place your Claude Agents" · nov 17, 2025 · 26:00

Give each agent its own ephemeral cloud computer and you can solve one problem many times in parallel — isolation, scale, and agency are the natural next step after orchestration.

  • Sandboxes give agents three things: isolation, scale, agency — environments "safe, destroyable, ephemeral."
  • "Think about your agents as deletable resources with a single purpose" — spin up, work, spin down; many context problems vanish instantly.
  • Best-of-n: "we can now solve one problem many times with compute" — 3 prompt variants × 3 forks = 9 agents. LLMs are non-deterministic; expect "complete duds" and dismiss them fast.
  • Demo: a Reddit roast of his landing page became 3 prompts → 9 sandboxed agents, each cloning the repo, fixing on its own branch, opening a PR, hosting a preview on e2b.app.
  • Layering: CLI → MCP wrapper → (optionally) skill — the wrapper lets any agent peer into a live sandbox.
  • The sandbox-fork agent's system prompt "completely overrides the Claude Code system prompt" — a true custom agent, one thing done extraordinarily well.
  • Sandboxes have lifetimes (1,800s here) — they expire like the agents that own them.
  • Deploy many versions and the bottleneck flips: "planning and reviewing are the big areas of focus for engineering with agents."
  • Trade-offs: costs API money (no Max/Pro), and "you have to engineer your sandbox."
  • Only Fortune-100s and big labs run this today — it's what Manus, ChatGPT, and Claude use to execute code. "Keep your eyes on this trend."
  • "Engineering is about much more than just writing code" — research, RL, computer use, data analysis, planning, reviewing, prototyping.
  • "The agent is the best tool for the job of engineering. How well can you use the best tool? This is the measure of our impact."
Best-of-n, drawn. Triple the attempts, expect duds, let review pick the winner.
E2BModalClaude Code · Sonnet · YOLOAgent SDK custom agentorchestrator · prime oboxCLI + MCP wrapperGitHub PRsbest-of-n

You never wrote a line. You planned, and you reviewed — the shape of the job now.

The model isn't the limit — the harness is

yt-20 · "I gave Gemini 3 Pro its own computer... it's official, Claude Code has COMPETITION" · nov 24, 2025 · 29:53

Gemini 3 Pro is the new benchmark king, but model intelligence matters less every release — the limitation is now the agent, the harness, and what you can build. Proved by racing Gemini, Claude, and Codex in 15 identical sandboxes.

  • "Model intelligence is not actually giving us that much new capability anymore... the limitation is no longer the language model. The limitation is in the agent."
  • "The only benchmark that truly matters" is how models perform inside your agent on your use case. "Every single release, they matter less."
  • Reprogramming via memory files: redefine the backslash so any CLI — Gemini, Codex, Claude Code — resolves \sandbox or \agent sandboxes:plan_build_host_test to prompts in one shared skill. "You can add your own special syntax to your agents."
  • One skill, three harnesses: GEMINI.md and AGENTS.md just point at CLAUDE.md — Claude Agent Skills go cross-agent.
  • plan_build_host_test is "a powerful agentic prompt that does a ton of work in a single prompt, a 170-line prompt" — a chained workflow whose steps keep using the backslash.
  • The race: 15 sandboxes, 5 tasks × 3 agents — SVG pelican on a skateboard, Pokémon evolution cards, SQLite CRUD app, Nano Banana image-gen UI, full-stack notes app.
  • Verdict: Gemini 3 Pro is "absolutely insane" on benchmarks, but Sonnet 4.5 in Claude Code delivered the most complete working versions — "there's something about the agent that puts it all together."
  • Judge on working software: "the most important thing your models can do is give you a working version... we want to sit through working prototypes."
  • Best-of-n implies tolerated failure: "you can just fully expect that some versions will not work successfully."
  • Anti-gravity: low signal — "a cursor ripoff that is actually Cascade"; "they can't beat the startup that's doing just one thing." Nano Banana Pro: high signal.
  • Ask on release day: "What can us engineers do that we couldn't do before?"
  • High-signal benchmarks: terminal-bench, tool calling, long agentic durations — "you want that duration constantly increase."
  • 100K subs; mission restated — "build living software that works for us while we sleep."

"I can guarantee you you're not using enough compute. I'm not using enough compute."

3

Opus 4.5, the delegator

yt-21 · "Claude Opus 4.5: The Engineers' Model" · dec 1, 2025 · 32:12

Opus 4.5's two unlocks: sub-agent delegation — Anthropic trains it to prompt-engineer other agents — and long-running hard tasks. So deploy more of it: multi-agent orchestration and browser testing at scale.

  • Anthropic says it outright: Opus 4.5 is "very effective at managing a team of subagents, enabling the construction of well-coordinated multi-agent systems." Most engineers miss this pillar.
  • "They're training Opus to prompt sub agents... if you can prompt a sub agent, you can prompt any agent. Agents will be calling agents in the future."
  • The flow: you prompt the primary → it writes prompts for sub-agents via the Task tool → they answer it → it answers you. You never talk to sub-agents.
  • "You don't just get the benefits one time. You get it n times if and only if you're delegating to multiple agents."
  • Pricing slashed to ~1/3 of Opus 4.1 ($15/$75 → state-of-the-art at ~60 tok/s). "Premium pricing for premium compute... if something is free and it is valuable... you are the product."
  • Cost logic: "If Claude Opus does the job in 5 tool calls and Sonnet takes 10... you have still saved money."
  • Model stack: fast/cheap Haiku → workhorse Sonnet → powerful Opus — "it looks like Opus is going to be both the workhorse and the powerful model."
  • Killer use case — browser testing at scale: /generic_browser_test <url> <plan-file> parallel:true headed:true spins 4–8 Opus sub-agents, each driving a real browser through user-story workflows. "Testing increases your review velocity."
  • Plan → build → host → browser-test: voice notes (ElevenLabs Scribe), a charting tool, a decision matrix — one-shotted end-to-end in E2B sandboxes.
  • Quote evolution: "the prompt is the fundamental unit of knowledge work" is superseded — "If you master the agent, you master knowledge work... the agent is the new compositional unit."
  • The ladder restated: single agent → better agent → more agents → custom agents → orchestration.
  • "Push your prompts harder, push your skills harder, push your agents harder, further, longer." Teaser: the fork-terminal skill, coming in yt-22.

"Agents will be calling agents in the future."

— the delegation pillar everyone skims past

4

Zero to skill, raw

yt-22 · "RAW Agentic Coding: ZERO to Agent SKILL" · dec 8, 2025 · 50:23

Building a skill from scratch — pen-and-paper plan first, then skill.md + tools + prompts + cookbook — is the fundamental craft. Skills package reusable prompts and code against a use case, and everything reduces to the core four.

  • Start off-computer: "Push the laptop aside... we never start by just blasting out prompts." Notebook first — purpose, problem, solution. "Begin with the end in mind": define the exact output file tree before any prompting.
  • "Those who plan the future tend to create it." On atrophy: "Engineers' brains are rotting due to this incredible technology... always understand what you're asking your agents to accomplish."
  • Canonical anatomy: skill.md (the "pivot file") + tools/ (single-file uv scripts) + prompts/ (user vs system, named explicitly) + cookbook/ (per-use-case docs, progressively disclosed).
  • The cookbook pattern is conditional prompting — if the user requests X and enable_X is true, read the file. "A conditional prompt — that's what progressive disclosure is."
  • Enable flags as static variables in skill.md (enable_gemini_cli: true) toggle capabilities without rewriting.
  • Prototype in the loop, then encode: "run it in the loop, write the prompt, observe the result, and then encode it in a skill." The osascript trick was validated live first.
  • Teach tools via --help: "Before executing the command, run command --help" — trades speed and context for reliability on fast-moving CLIs.
  • Information-dense keywords: "Astral UV single file script," "subprocess," a Python signature — each carries meaning the model already knows. Know your model's cutoff.
  • Agentic code reports: capture stdout + stderr, always return a string "so we can get concrete output every time it runs."
  • Debug order: "Don't assume it's the model's fault. First assume it's your fault, then... the tooling's fault."
  • Skills don't hot-reload — restart the session after editing skill.md; metadata loads at boot.
  • Fork-with-summary is a manual /compact: a YAML meta-prompt (summarized prompt/response pairs + next request) the base agent fills and hands to the fork. "The key is always to remember who's writing to who."
  • POC → MVP: "the simplest working version that gets our agents operating properly." Fresh context per task: "It's easy to know what your agent has seen when it's seen nothing."
  • "Everything always boils down to the core four: context, model, prompt, tools. If you master these, you'll master the agent, you'll master engineering."
.claude/skills/fork-terminal/ skill.md # pivot file · enable flags tools/ fork_terminal.py # uv + subprocess + osascript prompts/ fork_summary_user_prompt.md cookbook/ raw_cli.md · claude_code.md codex.md · gemini.md # conditional routes

"It's easy to know what your agent has seen when it's seen nothing."

— on fresh context windows per task
Claude Code · Sonnet 4.5Cursor tab-completionAGP / AG-SK snippetsosascriptAstral UV single-file scriptsGemini · Codex · Claude as fork targets/primegit checkpoint hotkey (GSTC)
REPOdisler/fork-repository-skill — the fork-terminal skill built live: skill.md with enable flags, tools/fork_terminal.py (Mac/Windows), cookbook routes with fast/base/heavy model variables, the fork-summary user prompt.

  1. Paper first

    Purpose / problem / solution and the exact file tree for a small skill of your own.

  2. Scaffold

    .claude/skills/<name>/ with skill.md (trigger phrases in the description), tools/, prompts/, cookbook/. Restart; verify via an "all skills" prompt.

  3. Validate in-loop

    Prove the mechanic live (osascript opening a terminal running ffmpeg --help), then encode it into the tool script + cookbook.

  4. Harden

    Add the --help-before-execute rule and one conditional cookbook route behind an enable flag.

  5. Fork with summary

    Add the YAML meta-prompt; chat a few turns, then "fork session codex fast, summarize work done" — confirm the fork receives the summarized history.

  6. Debug in order

    Fix your prompt first — never blame the model first.

DONE WHENThe skill triggers on its keywords, routes via the cookbook, opens a working forked terminal in the cwd, and a summarized fork carries the conversation into a fresh agent.

Highest replication value of the batch — steal the process, not just the repo.

5

Agents that actually learn

yt-23 · "Agent Experts: Finally, Agents That ACTUALLY Learn" · dec 15, 2025 · 18:54

The massive unsolved agent problem: agents forget. The fix is agent experts — self-improving template meta-prompts that maintain their own expertise file, a mental model of one domain, updated automatically after every job. No human in the loop.

  • "Your agents forget. And that means your agents don't learn." Traditional software improves with use; today's agents don't.
  • Everything existing fails the same way: memory files are "global forced context that always loads"; prime prompts, sub-agents, and skills all "have to be manually updated."
  • "The difference between a generic agent and an agent expert is simple. One executes and forgets, the other executes and learns."
  • "You don't need to tell an expert to learn. It's in their DNA." Experts don't relearn their craft per task; they update a mental model — "a data structure that evolves over time."
  • The definition: "The agent expert is a concrete form of a self-improving template metaprompt."
  • Meta-agentics first — prompts writing prompts, agents building agents, skills building skills: "There is no codebase I create that does not have meta-agentics." But alone, "they're acting, but they're not learning."
  • Anatomy: experts/<domain>/ with expertise.yaml (the mental model) + question.md + plan.md + self-improve.md. The question prompt's first step: read expertise, then "validate assumptions against the codebase."
  • The expertise file is not another source of truth: "The true source of truth is always the code... You have a working memory file. You have a mental model."
  • Payoff: "There's no searching here. There's just validating its mental model" — the websocket expert knows where everything is and one-shots plan → build → improve.
  • The three-step workflow chains sub-agents: plan (~80k tokens, context protected) → build → self-improve (diff-driven expertise update). Learning is a mandatory final step.
  • Scale for confidence: fire the same question at 3, 5, or 10 experts and cross-validate — "sometimes if you throw five agents at the problem, only one makes it... you compose that together, you get a better result."
  • "Three times marks a pattern" — build it thrice, automate it (he has a meta-expert that generates experts). Abstraction-agnostic: "I always focus on the foundational units: context, model, prompt, tools."
.claude/commands/experts/websocket/ expertise.yaml # the mental model — evolves question.md # read expertise → validate → answer plan.md # plan from the mental model self-improve.md # diff-driven expertise update

"One executes and forgets, the other executes and learns."

— generic agent vs agent expert
slash-command promptsexpertise.yamlmeta-prompt · meta-agent · meta-skillorchestrator app (Opus, Postgres, websockets)sub-agent chainingmermaid question prompt
REPOagentic-horizon/agent-experts (local course repo) — database + websocket experts: expertise.yaml, question / plan / self-improve prompts, the meta-agentics, and the orchestrator app they operate on.

  1. Read a real expert

    The websocket expert's expertise.yaml, question.md, self-improve.md.

  2. Seed your own

    Pick one domain (your API layer); create expertise.yaml seeded by an agent scan of the code.

  3. Question it

    Write question.md: read expertise → validate against code → answer. Ask 3 architecture questions; note it never searches.

  4. Close the loop

    Write self-improve.md: take a git diff, update expertise.yaml.

  5. Chain it

    Plan → build → self-improve on one small feature; confirm expertise.yaml changed without you.

DONE WHENAfter a build, the expertise file was updated automatically by the agent, and a follow-up question answers correctly from the new mental model.

The code stays the source of truth. The expert just stops re-reading the world every time.

6

2026: the year of trust

yt-24 · "TOP 2% Engineering: /PLAN 2026" · dec 22, 2025 · 48:15

2026 is the year of trust — every bet a top-2% agentic engineer should make reduces to one question: how much do you trust your agents?

  • The central metric: "How long can you let your agents go before making a mistake that you have to correct? This is what I mean when I say trust."
  • The releases — Gemini 3 Flash, Opus 4.5, GPT-5.2 — say "there are no winners": intelligence per token rising, cost falling. "Models are no longer the limitation."
  • Gemini 3 Flash "should not exist" — top-3 intelligence, top-5 price, ~200 tok/s.
  • The receipts: missed only infinite memory and "OpenAI stays #1." "Sometimes showing up on time is showing up too late."

"Trust equals speed. Speed equals iteration. And iteration equals impact."

— the frame every bet hangs on

2025 bets right
Gemini 3 FlashOpus 4.5GPT-5.2OpenRouter State of AIClaude Agent SDK (his pick)sub-agentssandboxesADWs

The bets, 1–5

  • 1 · Anthropic becomes a monster. "Claude Code was luck, Sonnet 3.5 was luck, but everything since then has been a masterclass in execution." One avatar: the engineer.
  • 2 · Tool calling is the opportunity. OpenRouter: only 15% of output tokens are tool calls. "Tool calls is a rough proxy for impact."
  • 3 · Custom agents above all — his most important bet: "A custom agent with 50 lines of code, three tools, and a 150-line system prompt could completely automate a massive problem... custom problems make you a ton of money." Pick one agent SDK, lock in.
  • 4 · Multi-agent orchestration. "Stop running a single Claude Code instance. Start running 3, 5, 10, hundreds." "What one planner or reviewer will miss, three will find."
  • 5 · Agent sandboxes. "Sandboxes let you defer trust... you don't need it until the very end when you're merging back to main."

The bets, 6–10 (+1 hidden)

  • 6 · In-loop vs out-loop. "Maximize the number of tasks your out-loop agentic coding system is doing and minimize the in-loop." Don't hand your core codebase to a cloud vendor — build "your own agentic ring around your codebase."
  • 7 · Agentic Coding 2.0: a lead agent that can "delegate, monitor, and coordinate" command-level agents. "We're not even the lead engineer. We're the executive."
  • 8 · Benchmark breakdown. Public benchmarks saturate; "top engineers will always have private benchmarks."
  • 9 · Agents eat SaaS. "Agents are the interface"; strip MCP servers to minimal tools; "if your application is just a UI on the database, expect it to be cooked."
  • 10 · AGI hype dies. "One of the greatest marketing schemes of all time... let's focus on shipping useful software."
  • 11 · (hidden) The first end-to-end agentic engineers: a feature shipped "prompt to production, no review, no human in the loop" — "the ultimate expression of trust in your systems."
WATCH & ABSORBNo repo this week. Internalize: (1) trust is the single metric — measure your longest unattended correct tool-call chain and push it; (2) 50 LOC + 3 tools + a 150-line system prompt automating one recurring problem is the highest-ROI artifact of 2026; (3) build private evals — public benchmarks are saturated noise.
7

The codebase singularity

yt-25 · "The Codebase Singularity: 'My agents run my codebase better than I can'" · dec 29, 2025 · 16:35

The agentic layer — a graded, class-based ring of prompts, agents, tools, and workflows around your application layer — is the highest-ROI thing you can build. Powerful enough, and you hit the codebase singularity.

  • "The agentic layer... the new ring around your codebase where you teach your agents to operate your application on your behalf as well and even better than you and your team ever could."
  • The singularity moment: "My agents can now run my codebase better than I can. I trust them to ship more than I trust myself or my team. Nothing ships to production without my teams of agents."
  • Two layers in every codebase: the application layer (front end, back end, DB, scripts, DevOps) and the agentic layer wrapping it so agents "see everything."
  • The progression is Class 1→3 with grades inside each — a maturity model you can locate yourself on.
  • C1G1 (thinnest possible): prime prompt + memory file. "If you have this, you technically have an agentic layer." Prime beats a bloated memory file — "you can activate this whenever you want and fine-tune it to run any specific workflow."
  • C1G2: plan prompts, specs/, ai_docs/, sub-agents (fetch-docs, test-writer). "Many engineers have passed this level."
  • C1G3 (custom tools): skills, MCP servers, prime-with-tool-access. Key insight: "Skills and MCP servers can both be replaced with just a simple prompt" — a script + a prompt teaching psql often beats an MCP server.
  • G3 is where engineers silently fail: "their tools are terrible and they won't scale... chewing up tokens, burning cash, overengineering." Most common sin: way too many tools.
  • C1G4 (feedback loops): review / code-review / reproduce-bug / test-backend / test-frontend prompts; closed loops follow "request, validate, resolve" so "your agent can spin and spin until the job is done."
  • "You should always add feedback loops into your agentic layer" — "you're effectively adding more compute to get more confidence in your agents' results."
  • As the app grows, prompts specialize (generic plan/build → test-backend, test-frontend); the two layers grow side by side.
  • Class 3 preview: an orchestrator that can "kick off arbitrary end-to-end AI developer workflows" from a single prompt.
  • Self-correcting agents are "a massive massive win for your agentic layer."
The maturity ladder. Grade yourself honestly, move up one grade at a time — G4's closed loops are where trust compounds.
prime commandsCLAUDE.md / agents.mdspecs/ + ai_docs/sub-agentsskillsmcp.json · Postgres / Firecrawl / Jira / Notionclosed-loop promptsorchestrator + ADWs
REPOtac/tac-8 (local Tactical Agentic Coding lesson-8 repo) — the agentic-layer reference implementation: class/grade structures and the orchestrator running ADWs.

  1. Grade yourself

    Score your main codebase honestly against C1G1–G4. Write it down.

  2. Move one rung

    Below G1: add a prime prompt + minimal CLAUDE.md. At G2: add one custom tool the right way — a script + skill/prompt (not an MCP server) that starts/stops the app or migrates the DB.

  3. Close a loop

    Add one test-backend-style prompt: request → validate → resolve, looping until green.

  4. Ship through the layer

    Run plan → build → review on a small real feature using only the agentic layer.

DONE WHENYou've moved up exactly one grade, and an agent's own review/validate loop caught and fixed at least one issue without you.

More compute for more confidence — that's all a feedback loop is.

8

Damage control

yt-26 · "Claude Code is Amazing... Until It DELETES Production" · jan 5, 2026 · 22:23

One hallucinated command can evaporate years of work. Layer deterministic pattern-blocking, ask-permission patterns, path protection, prompt hooks, and global hooks into every production codebase — damage control removes the need for trust.

  • "It doesn't matter how fast, powerful, or autonomous your agents are. All of your months and years of hard work can be evaporated in a single misinterpreted or hallucinated command."
  • The stack — four damage controls: local project hooks, global (user-level) hooks device-wide, ask-permission patterns, and the prompt hook most engineers don't know exists.
  • The prompt hook: an LLM prompt run as a PreToolUse hook on every bash command — catches destructive commands "even though it's never seen it before." The "last-ditch effort... as soon as you find that command, you should add it to your deterministic hook."
  • The layered wrapper: settings.json hooks (PreToolUse on Bash/Edit/Write) → uv/bun scripts → patterns.yaml: blocked regexes, ask: true patterns (confirm with you), zero_access_paths (.ssh — no read/write at all), read_only_paths (.bashrc), no_delete_paths (hooks, commands).
  • Hooks in settings run in parallel — whichever finishes first wins the block.
  • "Not everything is a 100-to-zero command" — ask-patterns cover the gray zone, like SQL DELETEs on a prod-like DB.
  • The /install pattern: an agentic installation workflow on the AskUserQuestion tool (global or project? Python or TypeScript? merge or override?) — a reusable way to ship skills to a team, driven by a nine-step cookbook.
  • "You just don't want to think about your security over and over. You just want great defaults, and then run with it."
  • The confession behind it: "A few weeks ago, I almost ran a command that would have been catastrophic in one of my production applications."
  • Trust framing: "You can build trust or you can defer it or you can make sure that you don't need trust." Hooks = not requiring trust; sandboxes = deferring it ("so they don't even need damage control").
  • Odds math: "One out of 100,000 — that's enough to really ruin your day." At hundreds of thousands of tool calls, you will hit it.
  • The prompt hook is slow — "but running this prompt every single time is going to be faster than potentially deleting a valuable production asset." Claude Code's trained-in protections are good (it backed up .bashrc unprompted), but "there's going to be a moment where you miss something and your agent misses something."
Defense in depth. Build trust, defer it, or remove the need for it — layered hooks are the third option.
PreToolUse hooksprompt hookssettings hierarchy: enterprise / user / project / localpatterns.yamlAstral UV & Bun scriptsAskUserQuestion/install cookbook ADWskills as distribution
REPOdisler/claude-code-damage-control — /install interactive setup, Python + TypeScript hook scripts, patterns.yaml (blocked / ask / zero-access / read-only / no-delete), the cookbook installation ADW, a mock DB for safe testing.

  1. Install

    Clone claude-code-damage-control into a throwaway repo; run /install (project-level, Python).

  2. Test all four

    Restart; try rm README.md (blocked), an ask-pattern SQL delete (type skip), ls ~/.ssh (zero-access), an append to a read-only path.

  3. Probe the prompt hook

    Try a novel destructive command not in patterns.yaml; confirm the hook catches it, then encode it as a deterministic pattern.

  4. Set the floor

    Install a trimmed global (user-level) version so every repo has a baseline.

  5. Make it yours

    Add one production-specific pattern — your cloud CLI's destroy command.

DONE WHENAll five protection types fire in a test repo, a never-seen command was caught by the prompt hook then hardened into patterns.yaml, and global hooks exist on your machine.

Great defaults, then run with it. Security you must re-think daily is security you'll skip.

9

Think in threads

yt-27 · "AGENT THREADS. How to SHIP like Boris Cherny. Ralph Wiggum in Claude Code." · jan 12, 2026 · 31:00

Thread-based engineering — every unit of work as prompt → agent tool-calls → review — gives you a measurable framework: more threads, longer threads, thicker threads, fewer human-in-the-loop checkpoints.

  • The context is Karpathy: "I've never felt this much behind as a programmer." Dan's answer: "Agentic engineering is a new skill. New skills need new frameworks to measure progress against. If you don't measure it, you will not be able to improve it."
  • A thread is "a unit of work over time driven by you and your agents," with two mandatory human nodes: the prompt/plan at the start, the review/validation at the end. The middle is the agent's string of tool calls.
  • "Pre-2023 you and I were the tool calls." Now "tool calls roughly equal impact, assuming you're prompting something useful."
  • Base thread — one prompt, one line of work, one review.
  • P thread (parallel) — Boris Cherny "runs five Claude Codes in his terminal and numbers his tabs one through five," plus 5–10 more in the web UI. "Guess who's getting more done?"
  • C thread (chained) — intentional phase checkpoints for work too big for one context window, or risky production migrations. "These checkpoints aren't because your agent messed up — that's bad agentic coding." Use AskUserQuestion or notifications (Dan: a TTS hook that speaks the summary).
  • F thread (fusion) — same prompt to multiple agents, then aggregate: best-of-n or cherry-pick. "The future of rapid prototyping will be done with fusion threads. Mark my words." Demo: 9 agents at once — 3 Claude Code, 3 Gemini, 3 Codex — each in its own sandbox, watched via mprocs.
  • B thread (big/meta) — threads containing threads; "your prompts are firing off other prompts." Sub-agents are v1; orchestrator-kicks-off-primaries is next. From your seat it's still one prompt + one review; the inside is a black box you engineered.
  • L thread (long) — "high autonomy end-to-end long duration work"; Boris's 1-day-2-hour run. Same shape as base, just hundreds or thousands of tool calls. "Great planning is great prompting."
  • Ralph Wiggum is "literally a loop over an agent" (agents + code > agents alone) — ADWs hitting the mainstream (credit: Geoff Huntley). The stop hook is the primitive: agent tries to stop → deterministic code checks a progress file or runs validation → reloop or complete.
  • Boris's other tips: always Opus 4.5; a small CLAUDE.md in the repo; no dangerously-skip-permissions (curated allowlist); and #1 — "give it a way to verify its work."
  • The four ways to improve: more threads, longer, thicker, fewer checkpoints — the whole scoreboard. Hidden 7th: the Z thread (zero-touch), review node deleted. "It isn't that we don't look at the code, it's that we know we don't have to. That's the endgame." Maximum trust; explicitly not vibe coding.
The thread taxonomy. Two human nodes, a string of tool calls between. The Z thread deletes the review node: maximum trust.
Claude Code · terminal + web + & sessionsmprocsfork-terminal + pthreadE2B sandboxesstop hook / Ralph Wiggum pluginAskUserQuestionTTS notification hookOpus 4.5
REPOpvolok/mprocs (third-party TUI, not a Dan-authored demo) — the multi-process terminal viewer he uses to watch 9 parallel agents in one screen.

  1. Install mprocs

    Define a pthread-style command (or reuse the yt-22 fork-terminal skill) launching n agents with one prompt.

  2. Run a P thread

    3 agents, same codebase-review prompt; compare answers for consensus.

  3. Run an F thread

    Same prototype prompt to Claude Code, Gemini CLI, Codex; fuse the best pieces.

  4. Stretch to an L thread

    One recurring task as a longer closed-loop prompt with a verification step — or a stop hook checking a progress file.

  5. Keep score

    A week on the four metrics: thread count, length (tool calls), thickness (nested agents), checkpoints removed.

DONE WHENYou can spawn n parallel agent threads with one command, you've fused one multi-harness prototype, and a week-over-week log shows at least one metric moved.

Mission restated, one more time: "Build living software that works for us while we sleep."

Sandboxes give agents their own computers (19) → skills make any harness programmable (20, 22) → Opus 4.5 makes delegation and long runs reliable (21) → experts make agents learn (23) → 2026 is declared the year of trust (24) → the agentic layer becomes a maturity model (25) → hooks remove the need for trust entirely (26) → threads give you the scoreboard to measure it all (27).

Scale your compute to scale your impact.

Hooks Inside Prompts: The Self-Validating Agent

yt-28 · "The Claude Code Feature Senior Engineers KEEP MISSING" · 27:29 · jan 19, 2026

Claude Code now runs hooks inside skills, subagents, and slash commands — specialized agents whose validation is deterministic and guaranteed to run. Validation buys trust; trust buys back your time.

  • "If you want your agents to accomplish loads of valuable work on your behalf, they must be able to validate their work."
  • The release everyone missed: hooks in the front matter of prompts, subagents, and skills — PreToolUse, PostToolUse, Stop.
  • The pattern: a PostToolUse hook on Read/Edit/Write runs a validator (a pandas CSV check); on failure it returns "Resolve this CSV error in <path>" plus the errors — the agent self-heals immediately.
  • "A focused agent with one purpose outperforms an unfocused agent with many purposes." A 20-line CSV-edit prompt isn't overkill — it's the point, over tens to millions of runs.
  • Validation must be hyper-focused on the purpose of that prompt — specialized checks, not generic linting.
  • You can prompt "validate your work with X" — but a hook is a guarantee. It always runs: the closed-loop prompt without prompt engineering.
  • Rule of thumb: PostToolUse validates the one file just touched (you get the path); Stop runs global validators over everything at the end.
The closed loop. The failure message re-enters the agent's context automatically — no human, no re-prompt.
REPOdisler/agentic-finance-review — a multi-agent monthly-finance pipeline (/review_finances) where every step — CSV categorize, merge accounts, normalize, generative-UI HTML — carries its own embedded validator hook; plus a parallel one-agent-per-CSV subagent demo.
hooks in front matterPostToolUse / Stopuv + pandas validatorsOpus 4.5AGP prompt templategenerative-UI reportclaude -p --settings
  • Keep validators in .claude/hooks/validators/; each writes its own log file. "You want proof that these things ran."
  • "Merge slash commands and skills — they're the same thing. It's a prompt." Every abstraction boils down to the Core Four: context, model, prompt, tools.
  • Prompts give the base; subagents add parallelization + context isolation; skills, same idea — the hook pattern extends to all three.
  • Do the agentic layer by hand: "slow down and do it by hand." Reusable prompt formats mean you and your agents never think about structure again.
  • Read the docs yourself: "Don't delegate learning to your agents… that is how you begin the self-deprecation process."
  • Bonus: claude -p --settings <json> passes an entire settings file — hooks included — into a headless primary agent.

Run the drill

  1. Validator first

    Write .claude/hooks/validators/csv_single_validator.py — print "Resolve this CSV error in <path>: <issues>" on failure.

  2. Wire the command

    Create /csv-edit with front-matter tools (Read/Write/Edit/Search) and a PostToolUse hook on Edit|Write|Read calling the validator via uv run.

  3. Prove the pass

    Run on a valid CSV; confirm the log shows a pass.

  4. Break it

    Delete a closing quote, rerun — watch the agent detect and fix it before reporting.

  5. Fan out

    Clone the front matter into a subagent; one agent per CSV file, in parallel.

DONE WHENThe agent autonomously repairs a corrupted CSV with zero extra prompting, and validator logs prove every parallel edit was checked.

"Agents plus code beats agents. That's what self-validation is. That's the closed-loop prompt."

— why Ralph-Wiggum-style techniques work

The /install Prompt Every Codebase Deserves

yt-29 · "One Prompt Every AGENTIC Codebase Should Have (For Engineering Teams)" · 22:36 · jan 26, 2026

Every codebase should ship a standardized /install (and maintenance) prompt: the new setup hook for deterministic scripts, an agentic prompt for intelligence and interactivity. Because "you can tell how great an engineering team is by the time it takes for a new engineer to run the project locally."

  • The 15-years, 50+ codebases heuristic: great teams onboard with "one link to one doc… and a few commands"; most burn 1–2 days of pair programming and Slack archaeology.
  • "Agents when combined with code beats either alone" — the whole video is one instantiation of this.
  • The new setup hook (claude --init, plus a maintenance matcher) runs before session start — installs, migrations, periodic upkeep you don't want every session. It doesn't appear in the hooks lifecycle diagram.
  • The script alone "is not that novel… raw deterministic code, it's not that valuable." Value appears when a prompt wraps it: run /prime, run the init hook, read its log, write an install-results doc, report — and resolve second- and third-order failures.
  • /install --hil branches into interactive Q&A — database fresh or keep? full or minimal? env-var guided setup? — pair-programming onboarding replaced by an agent, ~4 questions at a time.
  • The justfile as launchpad: "you, your team, and your agents don't need to remember or look up flags more than once." Recipes like just cli and just clmm encode every Claude CLI flag permutation.
◴ onboarding, most teams
  • 1–2 days of pair programming
  • Slack archaeology + stale READMEs
  • every engineer relearns the flags
  • failures fixed by whoever is around
◵ onboarding, agentic
  • "one link to one doc… a few commands"
  • setup hook installs, prompt verifies
  • justfile remembers every flag once
  • failures matched to a problem:/solution: runbook
2026 THEMEDeclared this episode: increase the trust we have in our agents.
REPOdisler/install-and-maintain — a mock full-stack app demonstrating the setup/maintenance hooks, the justfile launchpad, and the one-shot plus interactive install prompts.
setup hook (--init / maintenance)just/prime/install + /install-hiluv / npm / SQLite mock stackinstall-results logs
  • Encode recurring failures back into the prompt: a "Common issues" section of problem: / solution: pairs — "If you encounter exactly any of the following issues, follow the steps to resolve them." The prompt becomes a living runbook.
  • "When you have to update something, you don't look at the docs. You look at the script and this prompt." It's "a living document that executes."
  • Maintenance, same pattern: dependency updates, migrations, artifact cleanup, security and dead-code checks — deterministic hook first, agent validation on top.
  • This is where agent sandboxes tie in: a standardized install spins the codebase up on any new machine or sandbox with one command.
  • Mintlify independently pitched "a standard for LLM executables" — "I don't think we need a standard. It's just the idea that matters."

Run the drill

  1. Hook it

    Add a setup hook to .claude/settings.json with init and maintenance matchers pointing at scripts that install deps and log to a file.

  2. Wrap it

    Write /install: run /prime, read the setup log, resolve failures, write install_results.md, report.

  3. Make it interactive

    Add a --hil branch asking 3–4 setup questions (db mode, install depth, env vars).

  4. Launchpad

    Put just cli / just install / just maintain recipes in a justfile.

  5. Re-onboard yourself

    Delete your env and deps, then rebuild with one command.

DONE WHENA fresh clone reaches a running app via a single just command with the agent reporting success or failure — and one deliberately broken step is auto-resolved via the "Common issues" section.

The Task System: Teams, Not Todos

yt-30 · "Claude Code Task System: ANTI-HYPE Agentic Coding (Advanced)" · 28:26 · feb 2, 2026

The under-the-radar task system — task_create, task_update, task_list, task_get — lets you reliably build teams of agents. Its real power unlocks inside a template metaprompt, /plan_w_team: self-validation, team orchestration, and templating in one.

  • "More agents, more autonomy, and more compute doesn't always mean better outcomes. What we want is more organized agents that communicate toward a common goal."
  • Three components: self-validation (Stop hooks: validate_new_file — right type, right dir — and validate_file_contains — required sections), orchestration, templating.
  • Template metaprompt = "a prompt that generates another prompt in a very specific, highly vetted, consistent format." Sounds fancy; isn't. It teaches agents to build like you would.
  • Every generated task gets an owner — a named team member — plus dependency blockers, so tasks run in order and parallelize where possible.
  • "Task update is the big one" — how subagents and the primary communicate. Superior to the old to-do list + ad hoc Task calls: tasks block and depend, events ping back, no bash sleep loops.
  • Minimal viable team: builder + validator. "Have an agent build the thing and then have another agent validate the thing." 2× compute per task to buy trust.
  • Layered validation: the builder carries micro-validation (PostToolUse on Write/Edit → ruff + ty on Python); the validator is the macro check.
The minimal viable team. One agent builds, another validates; the task system carries owners, blockers, and status between them.
REPOdisler/claude-code-hooks-mastery — the hooks-education repo, updated live by an agent team (new hooks: session_end, permission_request, setup, post_tool_use_failure + README updates); contains /plan_w_team and the team agents.
task_create / update / list / getStop-hook validatorstemplate metapromptsbuilder / validator agentssub-agent TTS stop hooksOpusruff + ty (Astral)
  • Teams live in .claude/agents/team/; the plan step says "identify team members from .claude/agents/team or use general-purpose."
  • The orchestration prompt is a second input — a high-level prompt ("one builder and one validator per hook") guiding how the planner composes the team. A HOP-like pattern.
  • Self-critique: "I missed an opportunity — every validator agent could have fired claude -p to validate the log file." Tighter loops are always available.
  • The two constraints of agentic coding are planning and reviewing — this system attacks planning.
  • Anti-hype vs Moltbot/Clawbot/OpenClaw: fine if you know what's underneath; dangerous otherwise. "This entire feature set is just tools and prompts" — portable to any harness.
  • The path, recapped: base agent → better agent → more agents → custom agents → orchestrator agent.

Run the drill

  1. Team dir

    Create .claude/agents/team/builder.md (PostToolUse lint hooks) and validator.md (reports pass/fail only).

  2. Metaprompt

    Write /plan_w_team: inputs = user prompt + orchestration prompt; owned step-by-step tasks; a plan template with embedded per-task prompts.

  3. Gate the plan

    Add Stop hooks validate_new_file (spec .md in specs/) and validate_file_contains (required sections like "Team Orchestration").

  4. Real chore

    Run it on something real ("document + implement missing X"); execute the plan in a fresh agent.

  5. Watch the flow

    Builders parallelize, validators gate, blocked tasks unblock.

DONE WHENThe plan file passes both validators automatically, and the executed plan lands verifiable diffs — each build task followed by a validation task that actually checked it.

"Don't work on the application anymore. Work on the agents that build the application for you."

— yt-30

Agent Teams in tmux: You Are the Bottleneck

yt-31 · "Claude Code Multi-Agent Orchestration with Opus 4.6, Tmux and Agent Sandboxes" · 24:02 · feb 9, 2026

With Opus 4.6, the experimental agent-teams feature, tmux panes, and agent sandboxes, the model is no longer the constraint — "the true limitation is you and I." Learn orchestration and observability to scale compute into impact.

  • "It's no longer about what the models allow us to do. As of Sonnet 4.5, these models can do much more than you and I give them credit for." The constraint: tools, plus our prompt and context engineering.
  • Enable with export CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1 and run Claude inside tmux — the orchestrator opens a pane per teammate, each with its own context window, model, session ID, and name.
  • The magic trigger phrase: "build a new agent team…" — information-dense keywords that tell the agent which toolset to reach for.
  • The task list is "the centralized hub — this is where everything gets kicked off from."
  • Three tool categories: team management (team_create, team_delete), task management (task_create, task_list, task_get, task_update), communications (send_message — "the most important one of all").
  • The primary sits idle waiting for events; keep prompting it mid-run ("list sandbox directories we didn't kick off").
  • Iterative ad hoc teams: when 2 of 8 rebooted apps lost data, Dan stopped the primary from doing the work itself — "spin up a new agent team to do this work for you. Give them all the context they need."

The model is no longer the constraint. The true limitation is you and I.

— the thesis of the Opus 4.6 era
REPOdisler/agent-sandbox-skill — lets an agent create, reboot, and list E2B sandboxes and mount app codebases into them; demonstrates teams-of-teams rehosting eight one-shotted full-stack apps.
LIFECYCLEcreate team → create tasks (spawn agents) → work in parallel → shut down agents → delete team. "Delete the agents — reset the context and start over."

Run the drill

  1. Flag + tmux

    Export the agent-teams flag, start tmux, launch Claude Code inside it.

  2. Trigger the team

    "Build a new agent team — one agent per subdirectory in ./apps; each summarizes the app and how to run it."

  3. Inspect

    Watch panes spawn; verify per-agent context windows; inspect task_create / task_update calls via hooks or transcript.

  4. Poke the orchestrator

    Mid-run, ask the idle primary a question — prove it's event-driven, not busy-waiting.

  5. Teardown

    Agents shut down, panes close, team deleted — orchestrator context still small.

DONE WHENYou get a merged summary from N parallel teammates while the orchestrator stays under ~40% context — and you can name the exact team, task, and message tools that fired.
