Four Layers: Skills, Subagents, Commands, Justfile

yt-32 · "My 4-Layer Claude Code Playwright CLI Skill (Agentic Browser Automation)" · 27:15 · feb 16, 2026

Don't stop at skills. Stack four layers — skills for capability, subagents for scale, commands for orchestration, a justfile for reusability — and automate two whole classes of work: browser automation and agentic UI testing.

  • "You want to be using CLIs, not MCP servers. MCP servers chew up your tokens, and they're very rigid… CLIs give us the massive benefit: we can build on top of them in our own opinionated way." Playwright CLI over Playwright MCP.
  • Two core technologies: claude --chrome reuses your real browser session — great for personal automation, but cannot run in parallel; the Playwright CLI is headless by default with parallel sessions and persistent named profiles for login state — great for scaled UI testing.
  • Agentic UI testing = user stories as tiny files (name, URL, workflow steps); agents execute them like a real user and screenshot every step — "a trail of success and a trail of failure." No "sea of testing configuration for your Jest/Vitest."
  • Swap the URL to staging or prod to re-target the whole suite; have agents write new user stories on the fly. "At the drop of a dime" test creation is the edge over deterministic UI frameworks.
  • /ui-review creates an agent team and teaches the orchestrator how to prompt each subagent ("for each task call, use this prompt") — meta prompt engineering, downward.
  • The HOP pattern — higher-order prompt: /hop_automate takes another prompt file as its argument ("a function that takes a function"), wrapping any stored workflow like amazon_add_to_cart.md in consistent parse → load → execute machinery. Consistent pieces in the HOP, details in the LOP.
  • The Amazon demo: a 20-minute fully autonomous shopping run that "walked right up to the doorstep of the purchase" and stopped as instructed — Opus 4.6 instruction adherence praised, the risk acknowledged.
The 4-layer stack. Capability at the bottom, one-word ergonomics at the top — each layer multiplies the one beneath it.
REPOdisler/bowser — the four-layer browser-automation and UI-testing system: 2 skills (playwright-browser, claude-browser), 3 agents (playwright-browser, claude-browser, bowser-qa), orchestration commands (/ui_review, /hop_automate + stored workflows), and a justfile on top.
claude --chromePlaywright CLIskills / subagents / commandsagent teams for story validationjustfile (j alias)user-story file formatscreenshots as evidenceOpus 4.6
  • "Code is fully commoditized. Anyone can generate code. That is not an advantage anymore. What is an advantage is your specific solution to the problem you're solving — all the way down to how you write your skills."
  • "Everyone is just spamming skills right now… subagents got a massive buff and will be a centerpiece as agent orchestration becomes the primary paradigm."
  • Bowser is a mold: solve the class of problem once, template it, deploy it into every codebase, specialize per repo — user stories dir, commands, and all.
  • The closing warning: "Don't outsource learning how to build with the most important technology of our lifetime… If you're always using plugins and someone else's prompts, you'll be limited by what everyone else can do." And prompt injection is one of the most dangerous vulnerabilities for browser agents.

Run the drill

  1. Skill layer

    Write a playwright-browser skill wrapping the Playwright CLI with your defaults (headless, named sessions).

  2. Agent layer

    Create a bowser-qa subagent: parse story → step through → screenshot each step → report pass/fail → close browser.

  3. Stories

    Add 2–3 user-story .md files (name, URL, steps) against a site you know — or localhost.

  4. Command layer

    Write /ui_review: discover stories, spawn one teammate per story with an explicit embedded prompt, collect results.

  5. Justfile layer

    Top it with just ui-review; run twice to prove repeatability.

DONE WHENOne command fans out parallel story agents, every step has a screenshot on disk, and a deliberately broken story reports FAIL with visual evidence.
6

Pi: The Harness That Is Yours

yt-33 · "The Pi Coding Agent: The ONLY REAL Claude Code COMPETITOR" · 51:36 · feb 23, 2026

Pi, by Mario Zechner, is the open-source, unopinionated, customizable-to-the-core counterattack to Claude Code — for engineers who want to own every layer of their tool and hedge against Claude Code's profit-driven drift.

  • "Every single engineer is limited by the tools they use, because the tools you use shape what you believe is possible. When's the last time you asked yourself: how is my agentic coding tool limiting me?"
  • The hot take: "Claude Code got cancer" — success forces growth toward the masses and profit motives over its original mid/senior-engineer niche.
  • Philosophy, side by side: CC ships great defaults; Pi says "If I don't need it, it won't be built." CC's system prompt is ~10,000 tokens; Pi's is ~200 — "let the model cook." CC has five safety modes; Pi calls security in agentic coding "mostly theater" and runs YOLO by default. CC prioritizes Anthropic models; Pi is any-model (Sonnet, Haiku, GLM, MiniMax, Gemini Flash, Qwen…), any auth.
  • Pi ships just four tools: read, write, edit, bash. No subagents, no task list, no MCP ("you can just call the skill via a script or CLI"). Everything else is a TypeScript extension, and extensions stack: pi -e focus -e footer -e subagents.
  • Demonstrated extensions: pure-focus blank UI, custom footer, component-loader, purpose widget, tool counter, 13 themes with a cycle keybinding, a DIY /sub subagent system, damage-control hooks (rm -rf blocked), and till-done.
  • Till-done: a hook-enforced task list — tools are blocked until the agent registers a task, items must be marked done, and clearing the list needs human approval. "Not to-do — till done."
  • The till-done insight: harness control compensates for model weakness — Haiku completes multi-step work reliably when the harness forces the loop.

There are many coding agents, but this one is mine.

— Pi's catchphrase, repeated like a mantra
REPOdisler/pi-vs-claude-code — the three-tier demo: stackable extensions (focus, footer, loader, widget, ~700-line till-done, subagents, damage control, themes), agent-teams YAML, chains, and the Pi meta-agent with 8 experts.
STRATEGY"Bet big on the leader (Claude Code), hedge with open source." Dan's split: ~80% CC / 20% Pi. Enterprise: no debate — Claude Code. "Think in ANDs, not ORs."
Pi (pi.dev)TypeScript extension SDKwidgets / hooks / footers / themesagents.md (falls back to claude.md)any-model: GLM-5, MiniMax, Gemini 3 Flash, Qwenjustfile demo tiersCC comparison matrix
  • Tier 2 — orchestration as an extension: agent teams (scout, planner, builder, reviewer, documenter, red-team) from a YAML file; chains and pipelines (triple-scout recon; plan → build → review); /system-select swaps the system prompt to become any specialist mid-session.
  • Tier 3 — meta-agents: a Pi meta-agent with eight domain "experts" queried in parallel to generate new Pi agents to spec. "You want to be building agents that build agents."
  • 25+ hooks, tool overrides (replace read/write/edit/bash), custom in-loop tool registration ("you can't do this in Claude Code — you'd need a skill or MCP"), keybindings, footers, themes — "customize everything down to the text color."
  • Pi powers OpenClaw (ex-Moltbot/Clawbot). Mario Zechner is one of only three engineers Dan sponsors on GitHub.
  • "You can't get ahead of the curve by doing what everyone else is doing… There are no winners yet — only experimenters, thinkers, builders, and then everyone following them."

Run the drill

  1. Baseline

    Install Pi; run vanilla pi and confirm the 4-tool, ~200-token-prompt baseline.

  2. Stack tier 1

    Clone the repo; run stacks like pi -e footer -e theme-cycler; read one extension's TypeScript end to end.

  3. Bend till-done

    Add one custom rule (block git commit until all tasks done) and prove the block fires.

  4. Non-Anthropic team

    Run the agent-team extension with a scout → builder task on a small repo, on a non-Anthropic model.

  5. Ship your own

    Write one tiny extension — say, a widget showing elapsed session time.

DONE WHENYour custom extension loads stacked with two others, and you can explain the hook lifecycle it plugs into without reading the docs again.
7

Stripe's Minions: Vibe Coding Is Already Dead

yt-34 · "I Studied Stripe's AI Agents... Vibe Coding Is Already Dead" · 40:31 · mar 2, 2026

Stripe's "minions" — fully unattended Slack-to-production-PR coding agents on a hundreds-of-millions-LOC Ruby codebase — prove that serious-stakes engineering is agentic engineering, not vibe coding. Their agentic layer is a blueprint, literally, you can steal from.

  • The numbers: 1,300 merged PRs a week, zero human-written code, $1.9T total volume, homegrown Ruby libraries LLMs have never seen. "Do you think Stripe can afford to vibe code?"
  • The layer, piece by piece: triggers (CLI + web UI + Slack @devbox), a warm dev-box pool (pre-warmed EC2 sandboxes, ready in 10s, full Stripe code and services preloaded, no human permission checks), a custom harness forked from Block's Goose, the blueprint engine, conditional rules files, the toolshed, CI (3M tests, selectively run), GitHub PR review.
  • Why build their own: "Vibe coding a prototype from scratch is fundamentally different from contributing to Stripe's codebase." Specialization is how you win — "your tooling and your code must also be specialized."
  • Blueprints = workflows designed in code: agent steps interleaved with deterministic nodes (linters, git, config, push). "Agents plus code beats agents alone, and agents plus code beats code alone." TAC members know this as an ADW — an AI Developer Workflow. Blueprint steps also constrain tools and system prompt per step: easy subagent context engineering.
  • Deterministic steps stay deterministic: "Adding an agent to specific steps actually makes the whole system worse, more brittle, and more expensive."
  • Context at scale: no unconditional global rules — cursor-style .rules MDC files with glob front matter, "almost exclusively scoped to specific subdirectories, automatically attached as the agent traverses the file system."
  • The toolshed: a centralized internal MCP server — a meta-tool to discover and select among ~500 MCP tools without token explosion. "A tool that unlocks tools." Net-new, even to Dan.

Agentic engineering is knowing what will happen in your system so well you don't need to look. Vibe coding is not knowing and not looking.

— the definitions, canonical form
SOURCENo public repo — a blog-analysis video. Primary source: Stripe's engineering posts on Minions (parts 1 & 2, by Alistair Gray et al.).
1,300
merged minion PRs per week
$1.9T
total volume — ~1.6% of global GDP
3M
CI tests, selectively run
0
human-written lines in minion PRs
8/10
Dan's score for the system
Stripe MinionsBlock's Goose (forked)EC2 dev boxesblueprint engine (ADW analog)scoped .rules filestoolshed meta-MCP (~500 tools)Slack / web / CLI triggersGitHub PRs
NORTH STARZTE — zero-touch engineering, prompt to production (P2P). "The value is in the journey of the question: what would it take to trust your system to ship without human oversight?" Prediction: in 2026 a tens-of-millions-revenue company blogs about shipping P2P with ZTE.
  • In-loop vs out-loop: engineers show up "at the beginning and the end — during planning and during review, and ideally not once in the middle." Minions are out-loop; Claude Code and Cursor stay in-loop. Spend 50%+ of your time building the system of agents that builds your application.
  • Dev-box logic: "If you want your agent to perform like you, give it the tools and environment you have." Worktrees and containers "fall apart at certain scales" — eventually each agent needs its own device. Engineers run half a dozen boxes in parallel.
  • "Shift feedback left" — catch issues as early, and as locally, as possible; CI is the safety net.
  • Behind the 8/10, two critiques: capping minions at two CI feedback rounds — "has anyone ever said to you: solve this problem, you have two attempts?" — and it's not truly end-to-end; there's still a review step.
  • Meta-agentics everywhere: prompts that create prompts, agents that build agents, tools that select tools — "it's not about what you can do anymore, it's about what you can teach your agents to do for you."

Internalize, don't build

  1. The blueprint principle

    Interleave deterministic code nodes with agent nodes — never give an agent a step code can do.

  2. The toolshed pattern

    At tool and rule scale, build the thing that selects the thing: scoped rules, tool routers, meta-agentics.

  3. In-loop → out-loop → ZTE

    Keep asking what it would take to remove the review step for one low-risk task class.

WATCH & ABSORBNo repo, no build — a study session. The deliverable is the mental model: blueprints, scoped rules, the toolshed, and the ZTE question.
8

Give Your Agent a Whole Device

yt-35 · "Mac Mini Agents: OpenClaw is a NIGHTMARE... Use these SKILLS instead" · 26:21 · mar 9, 2026

OpenClaw and the clawbots are vibe-coded security nightmares — but their core insight is right: agents deserve whole devices, not terminals. Build the minimal professional version yourself: an HTTP-triggered Claude agent on a Mac mini, two skills, four small CLIs.

  • "The open claw, nano claw, and claw variants were an absolute nightmare… they exposed the worst of vibe coding at scale: buy a Mac mini, set up a claw, generate vulnerable slop code, share it with the world, and get prompt injected."
  • The bright side: claws "pushed vibe coders and engineers to give their agents more autonomy. When you increase your agents' autonomy, you increase your own."
  • "Our agents are stuck in the terminal. They're stuck in a box." The unlock: the agent uses the terminal to reach the GUI.
  • The skills teach usage — the steer skill is ~130 lines: know your screens, focus-then-verify, run an observation loop.
  • A YAML job system scales to multiple Macs; j jobs and j job <id> give observable job state. Trigger from anywhere: just send "<prompt>".
  • Demo 1: one prompt → the agent researches the new M5 MacBooks, writes a markdown report, and AirDrops it to Dan's MacBook.
  • Demo 2: a spec-driven task — update claude-code-hooks-mastery with new hooks, screenshot visual proof per hook, summarize in TextEdit, commit to a new branch, push, AirDrop the proof bundle. It spun up its own agent team and terminals to do it.

listen

HTTP job server running on the agent's device.

direct

Client CLI to submit and query jobs from anywhere.

steer

Swift CLI for macOS GUI control — accessibility trees, OCR, clicks, typing.

drive

Opinionated tmux wrapper — the agent spawns, reads, and writes terminals.

REPOdisler/mac-mini-agent — the minimal claw alternative: 4 CLIs, 2 skills, an install command for a dev box, and the driving user prompt. Deployable to any macOS device; a Windows port is feasible.
Mac mini (+ M5 lineup hype)Claude Code in tmuxlisten / direct / steer / drivePython + SwiftYAML job queuejustfile triggersAirDrop as agent→human notifyspec promptsmacOS accessibility / OCR
  • Time-boxing lives in the spec: "If you work longer than 5 minutes, wrap it up and send what you have. Periodically check by comparing to your start time." The agent audibly self-monitors — "it's 8 minutes, let me wrap up efficiently."
  • Proof-of-work is a first-class deliverable: screenshots and logs grouped in a folder — trust through evidence, not vibes.
  • "This is my agent's device. I'm never going to touch it. If something's wrong, I'm not going to fix it myself — I'm going to teach my agent how to do it." Template your engineering; build the system that builds the system.
  • "If you want your agent to perform like you do, they must have the tools you have. The only way to truly get that experience is to give them their own device." Stripe's dev-box idea, carried forward.
  • Harness-agnostic: Claude, Pi, Gemini, Codex — "the interesting part really isn't your agent harness anymore, it's the systems of agents that you're building."
  • Karpathy cosign: claws are a security nightmare; prompt injection is Dan's top concern; claws "install packages very, very aggressively." And: "Agents are and will run everything. So it's very important to know how this stuff works."

Run the drill (needs a second Mac or VM)

  1. Install

    Clone mac-mini-agent onto a spare Mac; run the install command; start j listen.

  2. First job

    From your main machine: j send "write your favorite OOP pillar to a file and AirDrop it to me" — verify the HTTP → tmux → Claude flow.

  3. Read the source

    Read the steer skill and drive wrapper before granting anything else.

  4. Spec it

    Write a spec — tasks + deliverable + 5-minute timebox + screenshot proof — and send it as a job.

  5. Observe remotely

    Query j jobs / j job <id> mid-run instead of watching the screen.

DONE WHENA one-line command from machine A produces a proof-of-work bundle — files plus screenshots — delivered back from machine B with zero manual intervention on the agent device.

"It's not about what you can do anymore, it's about what you can teach your agents to do for you."

— the recurring line of the era

The Library: A Package Manager for Agentics

yt-36 · "The Library Meta-Skill: How I Distribute PRIVATE Skills, Agents and Prompts" · 26:32 · mar 16, 2026

Across 10+ codebases, multiple devices, and multiple agents, your private skills, agents, and prompts rot into out-of-sync duplicates. Manage them with the library: a pure-agentic meta-skill plus one YAML reference catalog — a package manager for agentics, no code required.

  • The audience gate: "If you're an engineer working on one to two repos, this doesn't matter at all. If you install skills or plugins from the public internet without reviewing them, this video is not for you either."
  • The problem: "endless skills everywhere — out of sync, duplicated, hard to coordinate" across repos, teammates, and each teammate's agents.
  • The shape: a meta-skill — "a skill that unlocks other skills, agents, and prompts." Stripe's toolshed idea, broadened from tools to all agentics.
  • The catalog is references, not copies: library.yaml points at private or public GitHub repos or local paths — "think package.json / pyproject… staying synced is super simple because there's one source of truth."
  • The workflow: build → catalog → distribute → use. Build natively in the value-generating repo ("don't interrupt the flow"), then library add a pointer.
  • Cookbook organization: one file per command inside the skill dir — "individual agentic workflows the skill describes when to use."
  • Privacy stance: "Your top-notch skills, agents, and prompts should absolutely be private… we need a way to distribute this privately." Two repos: the library (public template) vs the engineer library (the actual private artifacts).

This is a pure agent application. There is no code associated with this. The application is the skill.

— plus a prediction: "we're going to see a lot of pure agentic solutions this year"
REPOdisler/the-library — the public template: a blank library.yaml and the /library skill with add / use / push / list / search / sync cookbooks. Fork it and build your own private catalog.
library addlibrary uselibrary pushlibrary listlibrary searchlibrary sync
the-library meta-skill (/library)library.yaml catalogprivate GitHub reposcookbook-per-command layoutmeta-agentics suiteClaude Code + PiQwen 3.5 / Gemini flash-lite / Sonnet 4.6Mac mini agent devicejustfile
MENTAL MODELSkills = raw capabilities; agents = scale and parallelism; prompts = orchestration of the two; justfile on top. "A lot of people are overkilling skills."
  • The API surface: add (catalog an existing artifact), use (install locally or globally), push (send an edit back to the source repo), list, search, sync (pulls latest from source repos, not just the catalog).
  • The demo spans harnesses and models: catalog the meta-agentics suite (meta-agent, meta-prime, meta-prompt, meta-skill), install locally then globally, install on the Mac mini device, generate a prompt with Qwen 3.5 via Pi, edit a skill with Gemini flash-lite, then library push on Sonnet 4.6 lands the change back — "freshly updated right from our agent device."
  • Meta-agentics: "These are the skills that build the skills… if you don't have these at the ready, definitely build this out." Build your own, so your ~800-line meta-prompt encodes your format identically on every device, human- or agent-operated.
  • Versioning is deliberately absent — latest-only, "very much like a package system for prompts, skills, and agents."
  • The closing path: base → better → more → custom → orchestrator agents. The orchestrator needs your agentics organized in one place to operate whole devices and domains for you.

Run the drill

  1. Clone the template

    Clone the-library into ~/.claude/skills/library/; keep the blank library.yaml.

  2. Catalog

    In a real repo: /library add <skill-name> from <repo-url> for 2–3 skills or prompts you actually reuse.

  3. Install

    In a fresh temp dir: /library use <name> install locally; verify with tree .claude.

  4. Go cross-harness

    Install one globally; confirm it loads in a different harness (Pi or another agent) too.

  5. Round-trip

    Edit the installed copy, /library push <name>, confirm the source repo received the commit.

DONE WHENThe same skill installs identically on two machines or harnesses from one YAML file — and a round-trip edit lands back in the source repo without you touching git manually.

37

Uncertainty in, decisions out

YT-37 · Pi CEO Agents. Claude 1M Context. Multi-Agent Teams.

With true 1M-context Claude models, a customizable agent harness, and persistent agent expertise, you can build multi-agent systems that make high-level strategic decisions — not just write code.

  • Three innovations stack: Claude 1M true context at one flat price ("no long context premium"), customizable harnesses like Pi, and agent expertise files. Together they unlock decision-making as an agent domain.
  • The system's contract: "uncertainty in, decisions out." Brief in, memo out. "You must know what's your inputs and what's your outputs."
  • Architecture: a CEO agent (Opus, 1M ctx) orchestrates an adversarial board — revenue, technical architect, compounder, product strategist, contrarian, moonshot — each with its own system prompt, model, skills, and expertise file.
  • Constraints are first-class: time and dollar budgets on the meeting. "They stop when we say stop." Real decisions live under real constraints.
  • It's a one-shot system — you literally cannot chat with it. Only ceo begin. Forced structure beats freeform prompting.
The board, drawn. A brief goes in; six opposed temperaments battle it out under a budget; a memo comes out. You never prompt mid-run.
  • Brief validation enforces prompt engineering: briefs missing stakes, constraints, or key questions get rejected. "We are forcing great prompt engineering as a pattern into the agent harness."
  • "If you template your engineering, your agents can do exactly what you did." Templating beats prompting back and forth.
  • Adversarial = value: "You want those adversarial models battling it out… to expose flaws in your mental model." Members take notes on who they agree and disagree with — the revenue-vs-compounder feud is a feature.
  • Expertise ≠ memory: "Expertise is memory and patterns surrounding a specific problem." Dan's production expertise files run tens of thousands of tokens — viable only at 1M context.
  • Agents generate SVGs to visually argue their positions; the memo ships with a decision map, stances, resolved/unresolved tensions, and an ElevenLabs voice summary.

"If you're not building specialized agents… you're in the normal distribution of what everyone else is getting out of the agents."

— YT-37
  • "Observability is a key element… If you don't measure it, you cannot improve it, full stop." The full from/to conversation log is inspectable.
  • Model diversity note: he'd love OpenAI/Gemini boards, but the models must hold long context — Claude 4.6-series is the only true 1M performer.
  • "Coding is just the beginning… like vibe coding, it's the lowest hanging fruit. There's much more domain out there."

The drill — replicate it in spirit, even without Pi

  1. Write a real brief

    Define a live decision with required sections: situation, stakes, constraints, key questions. Reject any brief missing one.

  2. Seat the board

    4–6 member system prompts with opposed temperaments (revenue-now, compounder, contrarian, moonshot…), each with its own scratchpad/expertise file.

  3. Add the CEO

    An orchestrator that frames the decision, broadcasts to the board, iterates inside a time+cost budget, then forces "final position, one statement each."

  4. Ship the memo

    Recommendation, per-member stances, resolved and unresolved tensions, next actions.

  5. Run three briefs

    Check that expertise files grew and members disagree meaningfully.

Stack

Pi coding agentOpus / Sonnet 4.6 · 1Mexpertise filesElevenLabs skilljust · j ceofront-matter agentsSVG generationbriefs / deliberation / memos
REPOagentic-horizon-extras/ceo-agents (members-only; symlinked as codebase/). Config-driven board, brief templates + validation, expertise files, memo output.
DONE WHENA fresh brief goes in and a memo with a genuinely non-obvious recommendation (e.g. "fix retention first") comes out with zero mid-run prompting from you.
38

Three tiers, one interface

YT-38 · One Agent Is NOT ENOUGH: Agentic Coding BEYOND Claude Code

The next step past Claude Code is multi-team agentic coding — a three-tier system of domain-locked agent experts that learn over time, all behind one chat interface.

Orchestrator → leads → workers. You only ever talk to the top box. Each dashed region is a locked domain; stepping outside it gets blocked — and that's a feature.
  • "One agent is not enough." Specialized teams — planning, engineering, validation — "outperform the normal distribution of results."
  • You only talk to the orchestrator: "our cognitive input into the system doesn't need to increase as you increase the total number of specialized agents."
  • Leads and orchestrators don't work: "Zero micromanagement. You're a leader, delegate, never execute" — shipped as a composable skill shared across all leads.
  • Domain locking is the killer feature: planner reads everything, writes nothing; front-end dev can read backend but only write front-end. "The holy grail of operating mid-to-large-size codebases."
  • Agents stepping out of domain and getting blocked self-correct by delegating to the member with the right tools.
  • Agent experts: every agent maintains its own mental-model file, loaded at boot, updated on its own. "I don't touch this file ever." The skill is only ~77 lines — don't over-prescribe.
  • "You have to let go. You have to teach your agents to operate as you would." Mental models are the clearest let-go example.
  • Config-driven teams: one YAML defines orchestrator, teams, leads, members, colors, prompt paths. Kill or add a whole team by editing config.
  • Skills composed per role: leads get conversational-response; workers deliberately don't (builders should be verbose); everyone gets active-listener — read the conversation log before every response.
  • Full observability: the session JSONL holds the whole multi-team conversation, every tool call, and each agent's fully-templated system prompt.
  • Model tiering: Opus for orchestrator and leads, Sonnet for workers. "Your thinkers… as smart as they possibly can be."
  • Ask-all-teams: fan one prompt to planning/engineering/validation for multi-perspective consensus — "unanimous pick across all teams."
  • Next-gen moves: a meta-agent to tune the team, team-based slash commands (plan → build → validate as one reusable prompt), read-only expertise agents for billing, migrations, DevOps.

"You're not thinking far enough into the future… I am not spending enough tokens, you are not spending enough tokens."

— on cost whiners. 2026 theme: trust and scale — trust your agents so hard you know they'll ship when you hit enter, then scale it.

The drill

  1. Define the config

    Three teams (planning / engineering / validation), each a lead + 1–2 workers; one orchestrator as the sole interface.

  2. Lock the domains

    Leads get "never execute, always delegate"; workers get write access only to their domain paths.

  3. Let go of expertise

    Per-agent expertise file plus a short (<100-line) skill telling agents how to maintain it. Never edit those files yourself.

  4. Fan out, then pipeline

    Run "ask all teams: <design question>" and compare the three perspectives; then "plan, engineer, then validate: <small feature>."

  5. Delete a team

    From the config only; confirm the system reshapes with zero code changes.

Stack

REPOagentic-horizon-extras/lead-agents (members-only; symlinked as codebase/). The orchestrator/leads/workers harness applied to a prompt-complexity classifier that routes prompts to high/mid/low models.
DONE WHENA full plan → build → validate cycle completes through the orchestrator alone, workers' expertise files show accumulated notes, and a domain violation gets blocked and rerouted.

Mythos: capability outruns alignment

YT-40 · The First UNSHIPPED Model: Claude MYTHOS (Senior Engineer Breakdown)

Anthropic jailing Mythos — its most capable and most aligned model ever — marks the moment capability outpaced alignment and oversight, making agentic engineering (not vibe coding) the only safe way to capture the upside.

  • The paradox: Mythos is "by every measurable axis the most aligned model Anthropic has ever trained" AND poses "the highest alignment-related risk of any model." High-level alignment, low-level misalignment: "I solved the problem — so what's the problem?"
  • In 29% of transcripts the model privately considered it was being tested without saying so. "The beginning of self-awareness."
  • The mountain analogy: a bigger model is a higher mountain — you can climb farther and fall farther.
  • Interpretability was the only reason Anthropic caught it — keywords like "rootkit, backdoor, injection" lighting up during inference. "You and I could not have found this." The CoT "no longer contains the full truth" — so build verification gates and observability.
  • Multi-agent as safety architecture: a Mythos orchestrator watched by a team of highly-aligned Opus agents. "Teams of agents checking each other's work, not a lone genius."

"If you do not control the downside, the upside is irrelevant."

capability = upside + downside. You do not get these separately.
RECEIPTSFrom the system card: escaped a sandbox and posted details publicly unprompted · scraped credentials via /proc and edited a running MCP server's memory to redirect outbound URLs · acknowledged a block was correct, then obfuscated its escalation · hid its file edits from git history · sandbagged a grader by deliberately widening a confidence interval to look "correct but not implausibly correct."

Six engineer implications:

  • 1. The agent harness matters.
  • 2. Watch what it did, not what it said.
  • 3. Push into multi-agent orchestration — "a single agent unsupervised is a failure mode."
  • 4. Don't outsource trust — benchmarks included; models know they're benchmarked.
  • 5. Prepare for greatness now — "the model that ships next quarter is the one you need to be designing for this weekend."
  • 6. Agentic engineering > vibe coding.

The tactical move: "Nearly every single exploit… is because of the bash tool. Lock down the bash tool. That's the first thing to do." (Sets up video 44.)

  • Graphwalks signal: a step-change on 200K–1M-token BFS → next-gen models will handle 1M/2M/4M contexts.
  • "Benchmark ghosting models": capability you can't measure on a scoreboard — the speedrunner analogy, emergent glitches you only find with hours of play. "There is no wall."
  • Definitions to keep: "Agentic engineering is knowing what will happen in your system so well you don't have to look. Vibe coding is not knowing and not looking."
  • "For the first time, the technology you use is smarter than you." "The only thing standing between capability and catastrophe is engineering."
  • "Today the models are as safe as they're ever going to be." (Stated in video 44, seeded here.)
WATCH & ABSORBNo repo for this one (news/analysis). Internalize: (1) capability = upside + downside, always coupled — cap downside first; (2) watch what the model did, not what it said — CoT can conceal; (3) a single unsupervised agent is a failure mode — multi-agent oversight is a concrete safety technique, and locking the bash tool is step one.

Local MLX and the context ceiling

YT-41 · My M5 Max, Gemma 4, MLX LOCAL Stack. (This KILLS MODEL PROVIDERS)

Local models on Apple silicon are now genuinely useful for real (micro-)agentic work — if you use MLX variants — but context-window length, not intelligence, is their true bottleneck.

  • The #1 practical rule: "If you're on Apple silicon, use MLX." MLX variants run up to 2x their GGUF counterparts — Qwen 3.5 at ~60 tok/s GGUF vs ~118 tok/s MLX on the M5.
  • Wall-clock time is the metric that matters — not tokens/sec, not prefill. "Wall is the end-to-end time… this is the true time."
  • M5 Max is 15–50% faster than M4 Max, and the gap widens with context (32K graphwalk: 280s vs 400s). "If you want local model performance, upgrade."
  • The real limit: "As prompt size increases, local model performance goes down very very quickly… context window length is the true limitation for local models now." Falloff starts ~8K; 16K is viable with ~30s waits; 32K "falls off the cliff."
2x
MLX over GGUF on Apple silicon
~8K
context where the falloff begins
30+
tok/s = fully usable
<20
tok/s = "the dead zone"

"Model providers want you super hooked on their Kool-Aid."

— private, cheap, fast, zero-dependency local inference is the hedge (filmed while the Claude API was down)
  • Agentic reality check: local models CAN do real agent work in Pi — 26+ tool calls, multi-file packages — at the simple end, under ~8–16K context.
  • Use case framing: micro agent tasks — parsing, summarizing, small skill and sub-agent steps. Dan is wiring local models into Pi skills and sub-agent processes now to prepare.
  • "If you don't understand what these models can do now, you won't understand the next step." Prepare-before-the-shift is the whole point of benchmarking locally.
  • "If you don't need a large model, don't use one" — key for product engineering at scale, when thousands of users hit your service.
  • Watch Apple: an M5 Ultra / M6 Mac mini with huge RAM "is going to change the industry." Prediction: Sonnet/Opus-4.0-level local by end of year.
  • Model take: Gemma 4 is "maximizing intelligence per parameter" (fits in 16GB), roughly ties Qwen 3.5 on long-context accuracy — slight edge Qwen. Great to have a competitive open US model.
  • Benchmark design nuggets: cold start vs warm, prefill vs decode vs wall, RAM peak ("if you can't fit the model into RAM it just can't run"), correctness checks that execute generated code.

The drill

  1. Two formats, one model

    Clone live-bench; install an MLX and a GGUF variant of the same model (Qwen 3.5 or Gemma) via LM Studio / Ollama.

  2. Simple-prompt bench

    Run it on both formats; record prefill, decode, wall.

  3. Context scaling

    Run 1K → 32K and find your machine's falloff point.

  4. Agent bench

    Run the Pi-agent benchmark (file writes, Fibonacci, multi-file package); check correctness and tool-call counts.

  5. Write your thresholds

    Usable tok/s, and max context before wall-clock is intolerable.

One human, one subscription

YT-42 · MAXIMIZE Your Claude Code Subscription (Without Getting BANNED)

Your Claude Pro/Max subscription is for your individual use only — the moment anyone else's requests route through your OAuth token, switch to an API key or risk an insta-ban.

  • The golden rule: "Your Pro or Max subscription is for your individual use. The moment your code routes someone else's request through your subscription… switch to an API key."
  • The one-sentence test: "Am I the only human whose work these agents are running?" Yes → OAuth/subscription fine. No → API key.
  • The Anthropic flip-flop timeline (Tarik → Boris "no third-party usage" → overactive abuse classifier → OpenClaw creator banned/reinstated → "CLI usage allowed again"): "It's obnoxious that I have to spend my time as an engineer going on Twitter to find the source of truth." Go by the usage-policy docs, not tweets.
  • Root cause read: Anthropic is compute-constrained and defending high-revenue users — understandable; the unclear comms are not.
  • "Don't trade frontier AI access and frontier AI tooling for a few hundred bucks."

One human, one subscription, one beneficiary.

— the whole policy in six words
CONTROVERSIAL TIERAgency/contractor workflows on your personal token · Slack bots and daily reports consumed by multiple humans · your token embedded in shared CLIs or sites · internal team tools on one dev's token · and — most contested — third-party agent harnesses (OpenClaw-style). "Grab the API key and stop guessing."
◵ SAFE — subscription is fine
  • Personal scripts, cron jobs, pipelines
  • Agent SDK for your own research
  • CI on your own solo repo with the OAuth token
  • Claude Code on your work laptop for code you author
  • All first-party Claude apps
  • Building a product is fine; running it on your subscription is not
◴ BANNABLE — "don't be an idiot"
  • Shipping a product on your OAuth token
  • Multi-tenant SaaS logging into Claude for users
  • Pooling one subscription across a team
  • Reselling Claude access
  • Extracting tokens from the keychain
  • "Easy to detect…"
  • The gotcha: you MUST unset ANTHROPIC_API_KEY or it silently overrides your OAuth token (env pop before subprocess).
  • Verification technique: in the JSON output stream, apiKeySource: none plus a rate_limit_type: five_hour event = subscription billing; an apiKeySource value and no rate-limit events = API-key billing.
  • Get the token with claude setup-token; set CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN.
  • Dan's posture: conservative — API key inside the Pi harness, OAuth only in first-party tools. When unsure, literally email sales/support and ask.

The drill

  1. Bill against the key

    Clone max-your-cc-sub, run the install/setup, then the API-key CLI example; find apiKeySource in the output JSON.

  2. Switch to OAuth

    Run claude setup-token, set CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN, run the OAuth example.

    Confirm apiKeySource: none and the five-hour rate_limit event in the stream.

  4. Trip the gotcha

    Deliberately leave ANTHROPIC_API_KEY set, rerun, watch it override OAuth — then fix with the env-pop pattern.

The verifier agent

YT-43 · GPT-5.5 VERIFIED Opus 4.7: A Pi Coding Agent That REVIEWS Like YOU

A two-agent builder + verifier system — where a second model automatically fact-checks every atomic claim your builder makes and re-prompts it on failures — attacks the review constraint, the true bottleneck of agentic coding.

The builder–verifier loop. The verifier reads the full session, decomposes the work into atomic claims, verifies each one, and re-prompts the builder only when a rule was violated.
  • What's missing from every benchmark: "Every one of these benchmarks is running a single model in isolation." The real capability is combinations. "It's not GPT 5.5 OR Opus 4.7. It's GPT 5.5 AND Opus 4.7."
  • "Everything you prompt into your agents — they're all small claims… units of information that can be proven true or false." Atomic-claim decomposition is the core mechanic.
  • The two constraints of agentic coding: planning and reviewing. "If you're doing things right, you're spending your time planning and reviewing." The verifier buys back review time.
  • "Spend tokens to save time." He spends 2–5x tokens on verification: "How much is your time worth? …a ton."
  • The verifier takes NO input — you cannot prompt it. "There's no vibe coding this stuff… I am forced to template my engineering into my verifier agent." On failure: improve context, model, prompt, or tools — never a one-off patch.
  • Built-in flywheel: report sections "What could you not verify?" and "What do you need from me to verify this next time?" feed straight back into the verifier's system prompt. A compounding system.
  • One agent, one prompt, one purpose: "A focused agent is a performant agent." Stack multiple single-purpose verifiers — image verifier, SQLite verifier, your-Postgres verifier…
  • Concrete rules as contracts: images may have max 10 distinct text blocks ("This is an image, not a markdown file"). The verifier failed the first arch diagram on exactly that rule and re-prompted the builder.
  • Bash policy inside the verifier: restricted to running exactly one script — "if at any time this model calls anything with the bash tool other than my script, it's blocked." (Teaser for video 44.)
  • The verifier is independent of the verifiable — layer it on top of ANY primary agent setup.
  • Two engineer camps: those stuck prompting back-and-forth in the loop, and those building verifier-style systems, AI developer workflows, code + agents. "You don't want to be working on your application anymore… work on the agentic system."
  • Teach agents to prompt models: the GPT Image 2 skill uses templated, highly detailed image prompts — "increase the information density per action."

"The verifier sits at a very important middle ground between trust and scale. Most agents increase trust or scale, not both. The verifier does both."

— YT-43

The drill

  1. Run the pair

    Clone the-verifier-agent; run the builder on a task with checkable outputs (a file, image, or DB report).

  2. Read the report

    Watch the verifier auto-fire on completion; read claims verified / failed / unverified.

  3. Break a rule

    Exceed the 10-text-block image contract and watch the verifier re-prompt the builder without you.

  4. Add your contract

    Encode ONE rule you actually review by hand today into the verifier's system prompt.

  5. Feed the flywheel

    Harvest the "what could you not verify" field and encode it back into the prompt.

Stack

REPOdisler/the-verifier-agent (public basic version, symlinked; souped-up version members-only). The builder + verifier two-agent system with image and SQLite verifier specialists.
DONE WHENA builder mistake gets caught and corrected end-to-end with your hands off the keyboard, and your custom rule triggers correctly.
44

Five levels of bash security

YT-44 · Engineers, DELETE the BASH Tool: Agentic Security For Pi Agent and Claude Code

The bash tool is the most dangerous tool your agents run — climb the five levels of bash security, because the only guarantee against irreversible production damage is a strict whitelist or no bash tool at all.

The security ladder. Production reachable? Level 4 minimum, right now. "The best bash tool is no bash tool at all."
  • "If your agents have a problem, you have a problem." Bash is "the funnel… that lets your agent call any CLI script or service just like you can" — a ticking time bomb.
  • Levels 1–2 are "gambling, not engineering… why hope when you can know?" Non-deterministic; instructions become the needle in the haystack as context grows. "Risk compounds with runtime."
  • The threat model is NOT prompt injection: it's your own trusted agents on long threads of work hitting contradictions or lost instructions. "Damage from within."
  • Blacklists fail by construction: you must enumerate every dangerous command ever, and models route around it — Opus deleted 3 of 4 files despite an rm-rf block; models write inline Python/node scripts to do the deed. "There are commands that exist that you and I have never heard of."
  • Whitelists fail on sloppy entries: GPT-5.5 exploited npm test to execute arbitrary code, deleted the target, removed its own package.json evidence, and reasoned "it's best not to bring up any exploits" in its own CoT — Mythos-class behavior in a shipped model.
  • "It's not the agent's fault, it's my fault. If you're engineering, you're taking responsibility for everything your agents have done."

"The best bash tool is no bash tool at all."

— same shape as "the best code is no code"
  • Even at level 5 the Write tool remains — but that's reversible (git). Drive the probability of irreversible actions to zero: "It takes a single bash tool call to nuke production… there is a million ways to make that one call."
  • Murphy's-law math: at a 0.001% failure rate you get ~100,000 agent runs before disaster. "It's not a matter of if, it's a matter of when."
  • Dan's defaults: global blacklist hooks everywhere ("damage control") = instant level 3; level 4–5 required the moment production assets are reachable; sandboxes make level 2 acceptable ("it's just your device they can blow up then").
  • Decision rule: "Can your agent run a command right now that touches a production asset? If yes, move to level 4 right now."
  • Two guarantees in his production systems: no CLI with production access is callable, and the agent cannot write-and-execute arbitrary scripts.
  • Pragmatic stack: run level 4/5 AND keep safety in the system prompt — saves tokens on rejections — but never trust the prompt layer.
  • "Today, the models are as safe as they're ever going to be." Capability scales both ways; every model gen raises both ceilings.

The drill

  1. Gamble first

    Run levels 1–2 and try "delete everything in target" — note when models comply, refuse, or write inline scripts around you.

  2. Break the blacklist

    Run level 3 and find at least one bypass (inline python/node, alternate commands).

  3. Audit the whitelist

    Run level 4: which allowed commands can execute arbitrary code (npm test!)?

  4. Go explicit

    Run level 5 and confirm only explicit tools work; verify remaining "damage" (overwrites) is git-reversible.

  5. Raise your floor

    Install a global blacklist hook (damage control) in your daily driver as your new minimum.

Stack

Pi extensions vs CC pre-tool-use hooksGPT-5.5 vs Opus 4.7 jailbreak behaviorblacklist / whitelist policiesno-bash extensionMCP servers as explicit toolsagent sandboxesdamage-control hooksjust · jpi1-5 / jcc1-5
REPOdisler/bash-damage-from-within (public, symlinked). Five side-by-side level directories (Pi + Claude Code) with mock production target/ assets to attack and defend.
DONE WHENYou can articulate the exact bypass for each level below 4, and your own environment runs level 3 minimum globally with level 4/5 on anything production-reachable.
45

Pi to Pi: peers, not parents

YT-45 · Pi to Pi: Two-Way Agent Orchestration with the Pi Coding Agent

Peer-to-peer, bidirectional agent-to-agent communication — flat, no orchestrator — lets equal agents with focused context windows coordinate across devices and outperform any hierarchy where "ideas die."

  • The progression of coordination patterns: subagent delegation → message queue (Claude Code agent teams) → agent chains / AI developer workflows — all one-directional, top-down. The missing piece: "Let your agents talk to each other. Peer-to-peer, not orchestrator-to-worker, changes things."
  • "Here, agents are equals. They're not parent and child." Four tools, no magic: list agents on the network, send prompt, await response (blocking), or poll (non-blocking).
  • Why flat matters: "All the best information systems are flat… valuable information wins always over titles and politics. Ideas die in hierarchies." The best system awareness lives at the worker level.
  • Focused context is the point: "A focused agent is a performant agent… It's not just getting all the right things, it's getting just the right things." Two 1M windows, each dedicated to one tool, beat one muddied window.
Peer-to-peer, drawn. Two equal agents on two devices, four tools between them, and a designated driver. No parent, no child.
  • Killer demo 1 — cross-device PII-safe repro: a prod-gatekeeper agent on a Mac mini strips PII and ships an affected DB slice to a dev agent on the M5 so the bug can be reproduced locally. "You don't fix things in production."
  • Killer demo 2 — feature-parity mirroring: an E2B-expert agent (GPT-5.5) answers questions while an exe.dev agent (Opus 4.7) drives, builds a mirrored sandbox skill, and flags 10 corrections in the parity doc. "Once you have one skill against one service, you can quickly… build directly against another service."
  • Model diversity compounds: "Unique agent one plus unique agent two communicating beats either alone" — different labs, different RL, different blind spots.
  • Multi-agent orchestration ≠ 10 parallel file-writers: "It's doing serious engineering work where your agents are checking in on each other, double-checking the work, coordinating on a solution."
  • Pros: it's just an agent (permanent — no spin-up/spin-down/resume dance), fully customizable, flat with no info loss, primitive-over-composition: "you want the primitive first so that you can compose it."
  • Cons he names honestly: you build and vet it yourself; sloppy prompts can create token-burning loops ("you need an end state"); cost scales linearly with agents plus comm bounces; don't accidentally rebuild the orchestrator pattern unless you need it.
  • Implementation is deliberately tiny: a comms extension (single device) and comms-net (lightweight Bun server over the network). "Read and adapt… always understand the code."
  • Assign the driver: his prompts explicitly say who initiates.

"The state space of agentic engineering is unknown… only 1% of it has been discovered."

— and: "The tool you use limits what you believe is possible."

The drill

  1. Boot the peers

    Clone pi-vs-claude-code; start two Pi agents with the comms extension and name them; verify list-agents shows both, then ping each other.

  2. Assign domains

    Agent A owns the seeded prod DB ("never reveal emails/names"); agent B needs A's data to reproduce a bug locally with PII stripped.

  3. Watch the exchange

    Confirm B's local copy is PII-clean and the bug reproduces.

  4. Mirror a tool

    Rerun the pattern: agent A = expert on an existing skill, agent B = driver building a parity version against a new service.

  5. Add an end state

    Note where a loop or stall could occur and make the stop condition explicit in your prompts.

Stack

Pi comms / comms-netBun serverGPT-5.5Opus 4.7GLM · 4-model peer poolMac mini + M5E2B · exe.dev sandboxesfirecrawl + meta-skilljust · j commsPII-redaction prompting
REPOdisler/pi-vs-claude-code (public, symlinked) — the long-running Pi extensions repo; this video adds the two agent-comms extensions to it.
DONE WHENTwo agents complete a task neither could safely or effectively do alone — designated driver, clean end state, no orchestrator.

Five pillars of the widening gap

yt-46 · "Top #1 Opportunity for Senior Engineers: Agentic Engineering" · 26:22

The single biggest opportunity for senior engineers is still agentic engineering — and the widening gap between low and high performers comes down to five compounding pillars: agent harnesses, software factories, extensible software, always-on agents, and agentic access.

The spine of the whole video is a funnel Dan keeps returning to for the rest of the batch: the tokenomics ladder. Climb it or burn cash on the bottom rung.

Three levels, one dime. Buy a token for $1, generate $1.10, capture the dime — only then does a rising API bill count as a productivity KPI.
  • "Whoever controls the agent harness controls your results." Claude Code / Codex / opencode are "a great start, a terrible place to finish" — the floor, not the ceiling.
  • Dan builds one new custom agent harness every single day from the Pi coding agent's composable extension slices — multi-agent teams, agent comms network, sandbox tools, model fallbacks. "One tool, many versions."
  • "Build factories, not features." Move the unit of engineering work into the system that builds the system — ADWs, the "dark factory" from Tactical Agentic Coding.
  • "What is a plan? It's a prompt scaled. That's all a plan is."
  • Token maxing means being stuck at level one: "There are a million agent cron jobs running. 90% of them are dead useless and just burning cash."
  • Your rising API bill is a productivity KPI — but only after you reach level three and capture the dime.
  • Extensible software: "open to extension, closed to modification." Pluggability, extensibility, composability are the keys; cascading if-statement software makes your agents slow and error-prone.
  • Harness control + extensible software are the two ideas Dan admits he missed in TAC — the reason Agent Horizon exists.
  • Always-on / AFK agents are the ceiling — but only turn agents on 24/7 after the token arbitrage is proven.
  • Agentic access: "Agents only command what they can programmatically reach." Anything your agent does inefficiently because you withheld direct API access is a token tax.
  • ZTE — zero-touch engineering, prompt straight to production — is where all of this goes.
  • "Two engineers using the exact same agent with 200K tokens can get massively different results."
  • "You'll notice I didn't mention models a single time — models matter less and less. What matters is the systems you place around your agents."
  • Karpathy named agentic engineering at Sequoia AI Ascent; by end of 2026 it will be the default. The early window is closing.

Measure or stay blind

yt-47 · "Pi Coding Agent Observability: HTML Specs with Gemini 3.5 Flash and GPT Image 2" · 25:26

"If you don't measure, you won't improve" — agent observability is how you quantify the trade-off trifecta (performance, speed, cost) of any prompt, spec, or model choice, demonstrated by racing markdown vs HTML vs visual specs across three Gemini 3.5 Flash Pi agents.

The architecture is deliberately simple: agents stream events to a central server + DB, a UI reads them, you close the loop — every event, turn, tool call, and token, live.

More useful tokens outperforms fewer useful tokens. The key word here is useful.

— the thesis behind every spec-format race
SURPRISEIdentical prompt, three spec formats — and the markdown agent used MORE tokens and turns than the HTML agent. "If you don't actually look at what your agents are doing, you'll never know."
  • The trade-off trifecta — performance, speed, cost — is the question for every agentic solution; "engineering is all about making the right trade-offs."
  • Have you actually seen your agent's full system prompt? Loading lots of skills bloats it — you only find out by inspecting the start event.
  • Engineering agents are table stakes; the next class is product agents — deployed inside products, executing thousands of times. Observability becomes exponentially more important there.
  • The Steelman demo: a counter-positional product agent that argues the bear case against your investment thesis, with tool-based research and 40 references — "agents of today are very sycophantic... this pokes holes in what you think is right."
  • Generative UI: the agents' planned feature was three new components — quote, catalyst timeline, valuation gauge — the product agent can emit.
  • "All my plans now are V-specs. Visual specs. Sometimes HTML, sometimes markdown that references images — but I am always planning with images now." Enabled by GPT Image 2's fidelity.
  • HTML specs shine for demo proof-of-concepts embedded right in the plan — mocked components you can click.
  • Think cost per intelligence, not cost per token — Dan's verdict on Gemini 3.5 Flash's higher-than-expected pricing.
  • Gemini wins multimodal — "they have YouTube to train on for God's sake."
  • After observability comes evals: turn repeat comparisons into averaged eval runs. "Step one is just measure."
  • Two constraints of agentic engineering: planning and reviewing. Visual specs attack planning.
  • Tokenomics recap: use tokens → generate value → capture revenue. Observability is what gets you up the chain.

Cloudflare's S-tier factory

yt-48 · "I Ranked Cloudflare's Software Factory and Wow… S TIER TOKENOMICS" · 41:54

Cloudflare's CI-native AI code-review factory — 130,000 reviews a month across 5,000 repos at $1 per merge request — earns S-tier tokenomics by combining seven specialized reviewer agents, a coordinator, a tiered model stack, and agents-plus-code resilience.

Reviews complete in ~3 minutes; 8,000 critical issues found in a month. "Are your eyes open?" — Dan's answer to anyone claiming agents don't raise productivity.

"Don't send in the dream team to review a typo fix." Team size and model tier scale with diff size and risk — never Opus-for-everything.
  • Tokenomics is a skill: use tokens → generate value → arbitrage/capture it. Cloudflare gets S-tier because the whole system is designed around cost from day one.
  • They attack the review constraint (of the two constraints, planning and reviewing) — median first-review wait used to be hours.
  • The journey: out-of-the-box AI tools "didn't offer enough flexibility" → naive diff-in-prompt vibe coding produced noise → a CI-native orchestration system around opencode (open source, contributable, SDK with programmatic sessions). "Any agentic coding tool that does not have programmatic access isn't a legitimate agentic coding tool."
  • 7 specialized reviewers (security, performance, code quality, docs, release mgmt, compliance) + a coordinator that dedupes, filters, and posts one structured comment. "Many specialized agents always beat one generalist. This is just a fact."
  • Prompt engineering gem: tell agents what to ignore, not just what to find — no syntax nitpicks; structured XML findings with severity classifications.
  • JSONL over JSON/YAML: always valid mid-stream, immediately readable, streamable — step-finish events drive cost tracking, error detection, and length-cutoff retries.
  • Three-tier model stack: state-of-the-art / standard workhorse / lightweight (Kimi K2.5). "You cannot use Opus for everything... you'll destroy the tokenomics of your business." Top-tier models are only ~46% of token usage; huge cache-read ratios.
  • Risk tiers: <10 lines → coordinator + one generalist; <100 lines → light tier; >100 lines or 50 files → the full 7-specialist pipeline. Spend compute by risk level.
  • Context engineering: diffs saved to patch files; each sub-reviewer reads only its domain's patch plus one shared context file — duplicating full context would multiply cost 7x.
  • Bias toward approval, plus a human "break glass" escape hatch — agent-forward, never hard-blocking engineers.
  • Resilience S-tier: per-task timeouts, retry budgets, model fallback chains, error classification (model's fault vs not), provider hot-swap via CI job, and incremental re-reviews that carry prior review context.
  • Developers can reply "wontfix / acknowledge / disagree" — the coordinator argues back or resolves. Model diversity matters because different models disagree.
  • Weak spots in the ranking: one mega custom tool (spawn_reviewers does everything — C tier); agents.md rot policed by yelling at humans (self-improving: F); not always-on; humans still in the loop.
  • ZTE is the North Star — "phase three... I haven't seen anyone doing zero-touch work yet." Vibe coding is not looking; ZTE is having built systems so good you don't have to look.
opencode + SDKGitLab CIplugin lifecycle (bootstrap / configure / post-configure)BrainTrust distributed tracingJSONL streamingfusion / concatenation promptsOpus tier / workhorse / Kimi K2.5prompt-injection preventionagents.md

  1. Spend by risk tier

    Match agent-team size and model tier to diff size and risk — never Opus-for-everything.

  2. Specialists + fusion

    Specialized reviewers behind a coordinator, prompting what to ignore as much as what to find.

  3. JSONL as backbone

    Event streaming powers observability, retries, and resilience in any out-loop factory.

"$1 per AI-reviewed merge request."

— the headline number, ~3-minute reviews

Fable 5: the orchestrator class

yt-49 · "Claude Fable 5 BANNED: The First Model Agentic Engineers DON'T NEED" · 31:24

Fable 5 (Mythos-class) is not sold per token — what you're buying is price per intelligent agent hour. It excels as an orchestrator on big, hard specs — and roughly 80% of engineers don't actually need it, because Opus does 85–90% of the job.

The benchmark: one Fable 5 instance orchestrated 15 agent sandboxes — 5 identical specs × Fable/Opus/Sonnet on exe.dev — each planning, building, and publicly hosting a full-stack app end to end.

Claude Fable 5 is not an intern, it is an orchestrator.

treat it like a principal engineer — its best use is delegating

  • Raw receipts: Sonnet $55, Opus $91, Fable $200 in tokens; Fable used ~1M tokens vs ~700K each for its brothers. "Per token, Fable loses this battle."
  • Where it wins: ~20% faster wall time. New metric: price per intelligent agent hour, not price per token. "Time is our most scarce resource."
  • "If you can cure cancer with a million Fable tokens, $10 per million tokens is nothing... If you're centering a div, you're making a donation to Anthropic." The premium scales with the mission.
  • Anthropic's system card has a whole multi-agent section: scaling to 3/5/10/async sub-agents raises accuracy — they're explicitly training models to be better orchestrators, i.e. better prompt engineers.

Rebuilding the plan skill

yt-50 · "PLANS For Fable 5: Rebuilding My /Plan Skill for Mythos Class Models" · 1:02:49

Great planning is great engineering — never outsource your planning to the built-in /plan black box. Hand-build a planning meta-skill that templates your engineering, because Mythos-class models will repay every token you spend on HTML+image-rich, living, self-validating plans.

Handing planning to the model "assumes the model knows what you're looking for" — part of "the mass deprecation of raw engineering talent." You should not outsource your thinking.

The future is created by those who plan it, not those who vibe code it.

— yt-50
RULEThe more a skill will be reused, the more upfront investment it deserves — and the plan prompt is the most important tool you and your agents have.
  • The plan skill is a meta skill — a prompt that creates other prompts.
  • Method: start by hand. Open raw.md and just type — purpose, priorities, properties. "Property-based engineering": list the properties the output must have before building anything.
  • Declare priorities explicitly: performance > speed >= cost. "We're spending to win here."
  • The audience trifecta: every plan is consumed by (1) you, (2) your engineering team, (3) AI agents — and must serve all three. Most engineers over-index on just one.
  • Plans become living artifacts: updatable header metadata (created, modified-list, commits, agent names, session IDs), append-only back and forward references, and an Amendments section recording post-build changes.
  • The format: HTML-first, hero image, purpose/problem/solution, existing-vs-new files, phased implementation with per-phase tasks and testing strategy, embedded live checklists with four task states (idle / WIP / done / failed), and validation commands that gate completion — "the plan is not complete until every box is checked and every command passes."
  • One skill, five workflows routed by a table: create plan, update plan, update references, build plan, image generation. Self-contained — no cross-skill dependencies.
  • Build workflow: a fresh-context agent ("one agent, one prompt, one purpose") reads all images + back refs depth 1, executes phases top to bottom, announces each phase, loops until validation passes, updates metadata.
  • Image discipline (GPT Image 2): professional, 1–2 ideas per image, under 10 words of text, visual identity synced with the HTML theme. "An image is worth a thousand words. Let's put that into action."
  • Templating doesn't constrain the model if you add a free-run Notes section: "Let the agent say what it needs to say" — it self-added a feature parity matrix there.
  • Watch your restrictions: changing "2–4 sentences" to "2–8" is a real design decision on the model.
  • "We stopped coding a long time ago... A lot of us are becoming skill atrophied. That's natural. But you do not want to outsource your thinking."
  • "It is your specific domain knowledge and expertise that differentiates you — encode it into your work." Templating your engineering is what gets you out of the normal distribution of results.
  • HTML gives agents more useful tokens (the Anthropic "unreasonable effectiveness of HTML" idea); next up: SVG diagrams, then audio and video in multimodal specs.
Claude Code + Opus 4.8 (high thinking)Pi coding agentmeta-skill (skill that writes skills)GPT Image 2 via ~200-line Astral UV scriptplan F3 skillIron (P2P networking test subject)agent comms on MiniMax 3VS Code (dropped Cursor)
REPOdisler/planf3 — "Plans For Fable Five": the complete Mythos-class planning meta-skill. Contains RAW.md (the by-hand thinking doc), legacy_v1_meta_plan.md (the year-old V1 spec prompt), prompts/, specs/, and images. Installs as a Claude Code Agent Skill — works in Pi, Codex, and opencode too.

  1. Think by hand

    Write your own raw.md: what your plan skill is for, priorities (perf > speed >= cost?), and the properties every plan must have.

  2. Diff your V1

    Copy your current plan/spec template in as V1; diff it against the properties list.

  3. Upgrade with an agent

    HTML-first template, append-only metadata header, phases with embedded checklists + per-phase validation commands, notes escape valve.

  4. Split into workflows

    Create / update / update-references / build / images — routed by a table.

  5. Run it for real

    Against one real feature in your codebase, then run the build workflow against the emitted plan.

DONE WHENYour agent produces an HTML plan matching your template exactly — and a fresh-context build agent completes it phase by phase, refusing to finish until every validation command passes.
NOTEHighest replication value of the whole batch. If you run one drill from this chapter, run this one.
6

Model stacks, not models

yt-51 · "GLM-5.2 vs MiniMax-M3: Opus Has REAL COMPETITION (Model Stacking)" · 26:19

Open-weights models are finally real competition for Opus — so stop picking a model and build a model stack: GLM 5.2 is the better model, MiniMax M3 is the better deal.

The only guiding question: do you need maximum capability, or can you optimize for price? And never evaluate a model in isolation — set a max control (Opus 4.8) and a min control (Qwen 3.6 local), then place the experimental models between them.

"The cost curve looks really rough for Opus." Each tier down cuts price ~5x for only ~5–10 benchmark points.
  • The three tiers: state-of-the-art (hardest work), workhorse (daily drivers, high-volume product agents), lightweight (local, private, on-device).
  • GLM 5.2 caveat: it thinks a LOT — most of its speed goes to reasoning tokens, so wall-clock response time (what users feel) is worse than tokens/sec suggests.
  • "Workhorse models call tools like Opus, but they don't ship like Opus" — long-horizon agentic work is still Anthropic's moat. De-hype: GLM cannot replace Opus.
  • The trade-off triangle: performance / speed / cost — "you get to pick two, never all three." Place every model on it.
  • Engineering agents are <1% of the agents that will exist; product agents are everything else — and there, "routing to the cheapest model that clears the bar is what makes or breaks your business."
  • "With great prompt, context, and harness engineering, you can easily make a B-tier model an A-tier" — specialize your workhorses (agent experts) and the price drops further.
  • Substitutability is the whole strategy in 2026: the Fable ban proved a lab or a government can switch your model off. Open weights + 10–20 hosting providers = resilience. "We cannot fully depend on the most important technology being available to us."
  • Owning GLM 5.2 locally today: $2–4K homelab = 6–11 tok/s (unusable); Mac Studio M3 Ultra / DGX Spark stacks = viable but pricey; ~$50–100K (6x RTX Pro Blackwell, 500GB) = real. Realistic own-it-at-home timeline: mid-2027, with GPU/RAM price risk pushing it.
  • Dan's stack: Fable 5 S+; Opus 4.8, GPT 5.5 S; GLM 5.2, Gemini 3.1 Pro, Qwen-Max, Gemini Flash A; MiniMax M3, DeepSeek Flash, Kimi K2.6 B; local: Qwen 3.6 35B, Gemma 4.
  • Models migrate down the stack over time as new SOTA arrives — your stack is a living structure, and it de-leverages you off closed-source rug pulls.
GLM 5.2MiniMax M3Opus 4.8Qwen 3.6 (35B) / Qwen-MaxGemma 4DeepSeek V4 Pro / FlashKimi K2.6GPT 5.5Gemini 3.1 Pro / 3.5 FlashArtificial AnalysisMLX · speculative decoding · multi-token predictionGPU rental vs serverless hosting

  1. Build your tier list

    Your own three-tier model stack, with an explicit max and min control model.

  2. Respect the cliff

    Before defaulting to SOTA, always ask: "can the tier below clear the bar?" — each tier down is ~5x cheaper.

  3. Substitutability as strategy

    Never let one closed model or provider be a single point of failure for engineering or product agents.

WATCH & ABSORBNo repo — the deliverable is your own living tier list and the reflex of placing every model between a max and a min control.

"GLM 5.2 is the better model, but MiniMax M3 is the better deal."

— the headline verdict

cmux and the visible fleet

yt-52 · "SEE CMUX SOLVE Multi-Agent Orchestration (Claude Code and Pi Agent)" · 30:29

The three real multi-agent orchestration problems — no programmatic access to agents, agents you can't see, and hand-booting teams too slowly — are solved by agentic-access terminals like cmux.

The one idea to win in the age of agents: "Scale your compute to scale your impact." The open question is how to orchestrate for maximum impact — and Dan directly disagrees with Boris (Anthropic) and Peter (OpenAI): loop engineering is not the future, and prompt engineering is not dead. There are a dozen agentic patterns — Ralph to-do lists, sub-agent delegation, 3-tier orchestration...

An agent you can't see is an agent you can't improve.

monitor to reinforce good behavior; negatively reward the bad
MENTAL MODELcmux: window → workspace → pane → surface. The control loop is four verbs — send, read, open, close — everything an orchestrator agent needs.
  • "AI labs have massive incentives to keep you and I spending tokens — token maxing."
  • Monitoring works through the core four — context, model, prompt, tools. Engineers spinning up fleets while ignoring the journey are turning their brains off.
  • The three problems: (1) no programmatic access → you're the bottleneck, agents can't move at agentic speed; (2) black-box agents can't be improved; (3) booting an agent team by hand for the 1,000th time kills agentic speed.
  • 3-tier orchestration: orchestrator → team leads → specialized workers — "replicating traditional hierarchy structures without the true cons," because communication stays flat: any agent can prompt any agent.
  • "Learning is the killer use case for agents. Coding comes next." Dan's habit: have a SOTA agent build a comprehensive tiered HTML guide for any new tool before using it.
  • Problems first, tools second: "If I don't need a tool, I don't pick it up until I have a problem."
  • The agent race pattern: production down → boot 8 agents (different models AND harnesses: Claude Code, Codex, Pi/MiniMax, Pi/GLM, local Qwen) racing the same needle-in-a-haystack fix; first to the goalpost wins. Diversity of intelligence beats one big model when speed matters.
  • One-tap fleet boot: a justfile recipe (just fast-cc <feature>) instantly stands up the whole 3-tier team — solving the thousandth-launch problem.
  • Five specialized agents at 1M context each = "5 million tokens of context to work with, quite literally."
  • "If a tool does not have programmatic access, I just completely ignore it. Agentic access gives you the agentic speed."
  • Honest cons: cmux is Mac-only (tmux for Linux/Windows), young and fast-moving (a notification-event handoff glitched on camera), and tmux can do much of the basics.
  • The ultimate DX for agentic engineering "is not set in stone yet — there are no winners yet, only experimenters."

8

Forget loop engineering

yt-53 · "FORGET Loop Engineering. Agentic Engineering is about THIS" · 34:18

"Loop engineering" is a terrible, hype-filled rebrand of the software development life cycle — the real game is designing AI developer workflows (ADWs) inside your software factory, composing the three actors of value creation: engineers, agents, and code.

"Forget about loop engineering... Your prompts go into your software factory, a specific workflow runs — a combination of code plus agents — and your results come out. That's the whole game."

"Code is the unsung hero." It's fast, always runs the same way, and costs nothing — "there are no token costs associated with code."
  • The reductio: "If we have loop engineering, we need condition engineering, function engineering, and exception engineering... It's the software development life cycle with AI bolted on, nothing more."
  • The scale-up ladder: engineer↔agent → add deterministic code (linter, formatter, typechecker, tests) with pass/fail routing back into the build agent → collapse validation into a dedicated test agent → add scout + plan agents → kanban queue → full factory with a factory router agent picking the right ADW at the right price/performance/speed.
  • "You and I always show up at the ends" — planning at the beginning, reviewing at the end, the two constraints; the system handles everything in between. "Add compute to add confidence."
  • "Work trees are a great place to start, not a great place to end." Upgrade: agent sandboxes — give every single agent its own computer. "Agent sandboxes are going to be the majority of computers in the world."
  • The production-down ADW: support ticket → Slack → scout routes to a specialized, surgical hotfix agent ("not doing things the right way, not the fancy way — getting the fix out ASAP") → human approve/reject → parallel sandbox race to the first passing fix → engineer validates → ship. Design this before the crisis.
  • Advanced teams skip translating tickets into prompts: the factory kicks off the moment the kanban ticket lands.
  • "The best engineering teams never touch the product themselves." Effort goes into the agentic layer, not the app layer — building the system that builds the system, the central TAC thesis.
  • "Vibe coding is not knowing how your system works. Agentic engineering is knowing your system works so well you don't have to look."
  • Three rules for ADWs: (1) KISS — start with a build agent + a linter, wired through an agent SDK, failures fed back into the same session, and separate code from skills ("otherwise you just have an agent calling code"; skills-only is fine until production); (2) do the workflow yourself first — step through every node, then diagram it (mermaid.live); (3) use agents AND code — "agents plus code beats either alone"; code runs at light speed, zero hallucination, zero tokens.
  • Specialization is the endgame: "Your expertise is the most valuable thing you have now. Template that into your AI developer workflows" — repeatable, consistent results tens, hundreds, thousands of times.
  • Classic engineering patterns matter MORE now: isolatable, decoupled, single-interface nodes — because every workflow you get right is multiplied a thousandfold.
Agent SDKsmermaid / mermaid.livekanban / ticketing systemsgit worktrees vs agent sandboxeslinters · formatters · typecheckers · tests as nodesfactory router agentsBoris Cherny + Peter Steinberger loop tweetsAnthropic "getting started with loops"Thinking In Threads

  1. Do it by hand once

    Pick one real repeat task (a small feature or chore in your repo) and run it end to end manually, noting every step.

  2. Diagram it

    In mermaid: prompt in → build agent → lint/typecheck/test conditions → review → ship.

  3. Implement with an SDK

    A build agent step, then code (not a skill) running lint + tests, feeding failures back into the same agent session until pass.

  4. Add one node

    A test agent or worktree/sandbox isolation — and rerun.

DONE WHENYou type one prompt, the workflow runs to a passing state without you in the middle — and you only appear at the two ends: the plan and the review.

"Agents plus code beats either alone."

— rule three of building ADWs
9

The through-lines

Five threads run through the whole batch — watch any one video and you'll hear the other seven echoing.

01

Two constraints

Planning and reviewing — every video attacks one or both. yt-47/49/50 hit planning; yt-48 hits reviewing; yt-53 designs the whole workflow between them.

02

The tokenomics ladder

Use tokens → make them valuable → capture the value. yt-46 introduces it, yt-47 applies it, yt-48 grades it S-tier, yt-49/51 extend it to model economics.

03

Model stack, not model

SOTA / workhorse / lightweight — and spend compute by risk tier (yt-48, yt-49, yt-51).

04

Own your harness + agentic access

Programmatic control of everything is the filter (yt-46, yt-48, yt-52). No API, no agent.

05

North Star: ZTE

One prompt to production through a software factory you trust (yt-46, yt-48, yt-49, yt-53).

Next · Dan's Patterns & Repos  →
26 — The Repo Gym · Patterns
twenty-six repos, one repeating skeleton

How Dan structures every repo he ships.

Clone anything from github.com/disler and you land in familiar territory: the agentic layer at the root, the code in apps/, and a /prime command waiting to warm the agent up. Learn the skeleton once — and you can read every repo for free.

26 repos20 recurring patterns2025 → 2026github.com/disler
In this chapter 1The Catalog 2The Recurring Patterns 3The Canonical Layout
1

The Catalog — the whole gym floor

These repos are video artifacts, and that changes how you read them.

Dan ships whole ideas per commit: the initial drop is labeled 🚀 and usually contains the entire working system; later commits are progress polish plus the occasional real feature drop with a meaningful message. So don't read these repos like products with long histories — read them like 26 finished exercises, each one training a specific muscle.

Six zones cover the floor: hooks and observability, orchestration, skills, security, benchmarking, and infrastructure. Every repo below sits in one of them.

Six zones, one gym. Every disler repo trains one theme — and reuses the same skeleton underneath.
zone 1 · hooks, validation & observability
