2

The Recurring Patterns

Twenty patterns repeat across the catalog — ten of them so consistently they amount to a house architecture.

The most telling one is pattern №1: a /prime context-priming command — read the README, run git ls-files, scan the key directories, report understanding — exists in virtually every repo, with variants like prime_cc, prime_obox, and prime_tts.

Dan never lets an agent start cold.

— pattern №1, /prime — present across all 26 repos
The canonical anatomy. Docs feed context left into .claude/; specs feed plans right into apps/. Every repo below is a variation of this drawing.
01

/prime context priming

Read README, git ls-files, key dirs, report understanding — in virtually every repo, with per-domain variants. The agent never starts cold.

02

uv single-file Python scripts

PEP 723 inline deps: every hook, validator, and tool is standalone uv-runnable; whole apps ship as one file (sfs_agentic_drop_zone.py).

03

The copied hooks stack

pre/post_tool_use, stop, notification, subagent_stop, user_prompt_submit + llm/tts utils with fallback chains, logging every payload to logs/. A portable artifact carried repo to repo.

04

send_event.py observability bus

Hooks POST summarized events (--source-app, --event-type) to a local server; SQLite + WebSocket/SSE + a Vue dashboard. Rebuilt for Pi too.

05

apps/ monorepo layout

Every runnable codebase lives at apps/<name> with its own pyproject.toml or package.json; the repo root is the agentic layer.

06

ai_docs/ curated documentation

Scraped, condensed third-party docs checked into the repo for /load_ai_docs — often maintained by a docs-scraper sub-agent.

07

specs/ plan-first directory

Plans as markdown (later HTML) that drive generation — versioned specs v1 → v4 in infinite-loop. Great planning is great engineering.

08

justfile + agentic /install

just recipes for humans, agents, and CI; an /install slash command does toolchain checks and setup interactively.

09

HOP/LOP prompt orchestration

One higher-order prompt fans out to N parameterized lower-order prompts and aggregates results — nano-agent's perf evals, /infinite, /ui-review, /review-finances.

10

Skills that ship their own tooling

SKILL.md + cookbook/ + prompts/ + an embedded CLI or scripts dir; progressive disclosure so the agent only reads what it needs.

…and the other ten, in brief

11 · Meta-agents & meta-skills — things that generate the things: meta-agent.md writes agents, planf3 writes plans, the-library manages skills.

12 · Model-tier variants — the same prompt at haiku / sonnet / opus; the performance-speed-cost trifecta as a file-naming discipline.

13 · The safety ladder — bypassPermissions only inside sandboxes or watched zones; otherwise blacklist → whitelist → no-bash custom tools.

14 · Deterministic validation — agents validate their work with scripts, not vibes: validator hooks, committed eval results, QA reports in ai_review/.

15 · Cross-agent portability — CLAUDE.md + AGENTS.md + GEMINI.md triplets; Dan builds for the category, not one vendor.

16 · TTS + personalization — completion announcements with your ENGINEER_NAME; fallback chain ElevenLabs → OpenAI → pyttsx3.

17 · Output styles + status lines — versioned status lines (v1 → v9) and portable style sets as an observability UI.

18 · README as video companion — one-line hook, YouTube link in the first five lines, hero image, Quick Start, "Master AI Coding" footer.

19 · Team agent patterns — builder + validator team dirs, plan_w_team commands, lead/worker 3-tier cmux teams.

20 · Emoji/terse commits — 🚀 for the initial drop, progress for iteration, meaningful messages reserved for real feature drops.

3

The Canonical Layout

any-disler-repo/ .claude/ commands/ # prime.md · install.md · hop_*.md — prompts as programs agents/ # sub-agents · meta-agent.md · team/ builder+validator hooks/ # pre_tool_use.py … uv single-file scripts + utils/llm, utils/tts skills/ # SKILL.md + cookbook/ + embedded tooling settings.json # hook wiring + permission allowlists ai_docs/ # curated third-party docs — /load_ai_docs specs/ # plans drive generation (markdown, later HTML) apps/ # every runnable codebase — own pyproject/package.json logs/ # every hook payload, logged as JSON images/ # README hero + architecture diagram justfile # the launch pad — humans, agents, CI CLAUDE.md # + AGENTS.md + GEMINI.md — cross-agent portability README.md # the video companion

This tree is why the patterns travel so well.

Because the root is always the agentic layer and the code always lives under apps/, whole subsystems copy cleanly between repos. The hooks stack, the TTS utils, the meta-agent, the observability bus — each is a portable artifact that appears near-identical in hooks-mastery, observability, nano-agent, big-3, and agent-experts.

When you build your own repos this way, you get the same effect: every new project starts at 80%, because the agentic layer comes with you.

NEWCOMER NOTE Exploring any disler repo? Run /prime first, then read .claude/commands/ before the code — the prompts are the architecture; the apps are just what they operate.
Next · Rebuild Paths, Commit by Commit  →
27 — The Repo Gym · Rebuilds
commit archaeology as curriculum

Rebuild what he built, in the order he built it.

Eight repos, decomposed into milestone rebuild paths — follow the real commit history where it exists, decompose the architecture where a single 🚀 commit holds the whole system, and finish every rebuild against a checkable definition of done.

8 rebuilds40+ milestonesreal commit messagesfoundations → capstone
In this chapter 1drop-zones 2hooks-mastery 3infinite-loop 4beyond-mcp 5bowser 6nano-agent 7observability 8finance-review

These eight weren't picked for popularity.

The selection criteria: density of transferable patterns, real commit history to follow, and how much of Dan's stack the rebuild forces you to internalize. Where a repo's history is one 🚀 commit, the path decomposes the system's internal structure instead — you rebuild the architecture, not the diff.

"The arc: trigger an agent → control an agent → parallelize agents → choose tool interfaces → compose layers → build and measure the agent itself → observe fleets → trust autonomous pipelines. That is, in order, how Dan thinks."

— the eight-rebuild learning arc
Climb in order. Each rung assumes the muscles built on the rung below — drop-zones takes an afternoon; finance-review assumes everything.
1

agentic-drop-zones

Minimal agentic infrastructure — one file, one YAML, huge leverage.

This is the best first rebuild: it teaches triggers, prompt templates, streaming output, and the autonomy trade-off without any web stack. The history is flat — the init commit contains everything and seven progress commits are README polish — so you rebuild by decomposing the single file itself.

agentic-drop-zones · 8 commits, 2025-08
init          # 2025-08-28 — the entire system in one commit
progress ×7   # README + architecture polish

# drops.yaml — one zone, declared not coded:
#   name · file_patterns · reusable_prompt · zone_dirs
#   events · agent · model · mcp_server_file

  1. M1 — Watcher skeleton

    Build sfs_agentic_drop_zone.py with watchdog observers and a rich console; detect created files matching patterns. You learn single-file uv apps ("sfs" naming) and event-driven triggers.

  2. M2 — Declarative zones

    Move every zone into drops.yaml — name, file_patterns, reusable_prompt, zone_dirs, events, agent, model, mcp_server_file. Config-not-code extensibility.

  3. M3 — Prompt templating + agent dispatch

    Load .claude/commands/<x>.md, substitute FILE_PATH, shell out to claude -p with bypassPermissions and stream JSON to styled panels. Prompts become reusable templates; full autonomy gets an explicit risk frame.

  4. M4 — Multi-agent + MCP

    Add the gemini_cli branch (yolo --sandbox) and per-zone .mcp.json. Agent-agnostic dispatch, per-zone tool scoping.

  5. M5 — Real zones

    Ship echo.md, create_image.md / edit_image.md, finance_categorizer.md, morning_debrief.md, more_training_data.md with example input files. One pattern, many workflows — the zone catalog is the product.

DONE WHEN Dropping echo.txt streams a response within seconds; an image-prompt file produces a generated image in the zone; and a malformed file fails loudly, not silently.
2

claude-code-hooks-mastery

The foundation layer every other repo copies from.

The hooks stack, TTS/LLM utils, sub-agents, meta-agent, output styles, and status lines all originate or mature here — and it has the cleanest real history in the catalog: each commit is a named capability. Rebuild it commit by commit and you retrace the exact order Dan layered deterministic control onto a nondeterministic agent.

claude-code-hooks-mastery · git log, oldest first
🚀                               # 2025-07-02 core hooks stack
UserPromptSubmit                 # 2025-07-18
subagents                        # 2025-07-27
add PreCompact and SessionStart  # 2025-07-29
ccmodels                         # 2025-08-03
genui                            # 2025-08-17
Update Hooks                     # 2026-02-01 teams + new hooks

  1. M1 — Core hooks + flow control (🚀)

    Wire pre_tool_use, post_tool_use, stop, notification, subagent_stop as uv single-file scripts in settings.json; block rm -rf and .env reads in PreToolUse; log every payload to logs/; add utils/tts (elevenlabs → openai → pyttsx3) and utils/llm. You learn hook payloads, the exit-code contract (0 allow / 2 block / JSON), and uv inline deps.

  2. M2 — Prompt-level control (UserPromptSubmit)

    Add user_prompt_submit.py for validation and context injection — intercepting before the model even sees the prompt.

  3. M3 — Sub-agents + the meta-agent (subagents)

    Build .claude/agents/ with hello-world, TTS completion-summary, research agents, crypto analyzers — and meta-agent.md, which generates new agent files from a description. Agents that build agents.

  4. M4 — Lifecycle completeness (add PreCompact and SessionStart)

    Cover session boundaries; inject dev context at SessionStart. Map the full 13-event lifecycle.

  5. M5 — Model-tier engineering (ccmodels)

    Restructure crypto agents into haiku / sonnet / opus variants — the performance-speed-cost trifecta as a file-naming discipline.

  6. M6 — Output styles + status lines + local LLMs (genui)

    Eight output styles including genui and tts-summary; status_line v1 → v9 pulling session data; utils/llm/ollama.py for local-model fallbacks.

  7. M7 — Teams + new hooks (Update Hooks)

    Add permission_request, subagent_start, post_tool_use_failure, session_end, setup hooks; team builder/validator agents with plan, build, and plan_w_team commands; a tts_queue. Builder/validator orchestration plus failure-path hooks.

DONE WHEN All 13 hooks log their JSON payloads; a dangerous rm -rf is blocked with exit 2; /plan_w_team runs builder then validator; the meta-agent generates a working new agent file; and TTS announces completion with your name.
3

infinite-agentic-loop

The purest lesson in spec-driven parallel orchestration — the whole system is two markdown files.

Its 18-commit history is a visible iteration loop on a spec (v1 → v4): you literally watch Dan learn what makes agent-facing specs work. Nearly every message says progress — the information is in what each commit adds.

infinite-agentic-loop · what each "progress" adds
progress  # .claude/commands/infinite.md + ai_docs/
progress  # specs/invent_new_ui.md → src/ui_innovation_1..10
progress  # invent_new_ui_v2.md → src_enhanced/
progress  # invent_new_ui_v3.md → themed_hybrids/ (then: broader)
progress  # prime.md + settings.json permissions + legacy/
progress  # README + src/ui_hybrid_10..18 — the scale run
progress  # 2025-06-10: v4 → src_group/ui_hybrid_N/ file sets

  1. M1 — The command

    Write the orchestrator prompt: parse $ARGUMENTS (spec, out_dir, count), analyze the spec, reconnoiter the output directory, deploy sub-agents. Slash commands as programs.

  2. M2 — First spec + first outputs

    specs/invent_new_ui.md, single and small-batch runs. Uniqueness comes from directory reconnaissance — agents never duplicate prior output.

  3. M3 — Spec iteration

    v2 and v3: tighten naming patterns, design dimensions, quality bars. The spec is the product — output quality tracks spec quality, not prompt cleverness.

  4. M4 — Repo hygiene + permissions

    prime.md, settings.json allowlists for Write/Edit/Bash, move old outputs to legacy/. Permissioning for autonomous loops.

  5. M5 — Scale run

    20-iteration batched waves; infinite mode until context exhaustion. Wave management and progressive sophistication under a finite context budget.

  6. M6 — Multi-file generations

    v4 upgrades the spec so each agent emits a file set — index.html, script.js, styles.css. Structuring specs for compound artifacts.

DONE WHEN /infinite specs/your_spec.md out 5 yields five genuinely distinct, spec-compliant artifacts with correct sequential naming — and infinite mode runs at least two waves with rising sophistication.
4

beyond-mcp

The clearest architectural-judgment lesson in the catalog.

The same domain — Kalshi prediction-market data — built four ways forces you to feel the context-cost, discoverability, and reusability trade-offs everyone argues about abstractly. The numbered apps are a built-in rebuild order, and the thesis is blunt: MCP's cost is instant context loss.

apps/ 1_mcp_server/ # FastMCP + .mcp.json — schemas eat context at start 2_cli/ # typer CLI + /prime_kalshi_cli_tools on demand 3_file_system_scripts/ # one uv script per operation 4_skill/ # SKILL.md — auto-discovered, context-cheap

  1. M1 — Domain client

    Shared Kalshi API access: status, events, markets.

  2. M2 — apps/1_mcp_server

    FastMCP server + .mcp.json; measure the context the tool schemas consume at session start. MCP mechanics and their instant context loss.

  3. M3 — apps/2_cli

    A typer/click CLI plus a /prime_kalshi_cli_tools command that teaches the agent the CLI on demand. Prime-on-demand beats always-loaded.

  4. M4 — apps/3_file_system_scripts

    One uv script per operation with its own /prime command. Scripts are the zero-infrastructure tool surface agents can read and edit.

  5. M5 — apps/4_skill

    SKILL.md wrapping the capability with a trigger description and progressive disclosure.

  6. M6 — The bake-off

    Run the same prompt set against all four; compare tokens at startup, tokens per task, latency, failure modes. Write the trade-off table yourself.

DONE WHEN You can answer "MCP, CLI, scripts, or skill?" for a new toolset in one sentence — backed by your own measured token numbers.
5

bowser

The best single artifact for Dan's composition philosophy.

Skill → Subagent → Command → justfile, each layer independently testable, each wrapping the one below. It's small — 30 files, 3 commits — but the architecture generalizes to any capability, not just browsers. Two skills give you the headed/headless duality: claude-bowser (Chrome MCP, observable) and playwright-bowser (playwright-cli, fast and silent).

Four doors into one capability. Skill for direct use, subagent for isolation, command for orchestration, justfile for one-liners.

  1. M1 — Layer 1, skills

    Write the claude-bowser and playwright-bowser SKILL.md files; test directly with just test-playwright-skill. Includes a just skill that teaches the agent the task runner itself.

  2. M2 — Layer 2, subagents

    playwright-bowser-agent, claude-bowser-agent, bowser-qa-agent wrap the skills for isolated parallel sessions with a structured report contract. Subagent = capability + isolation + report.

  3. M3 — Layer 3, commands

    /bowser:hop-automate runs saved workflows; /ui-review discovers YAML user stories in specs/, fans out QA agents, and collects reports into ai_review/. HOP orchestration over subagents.

  4. M4 — Layer 4, justfile

    One-liners per layer (just hop …, just ui-review) and the README's four-layer table — reusability for you, your team, and other agents.

DONE WHEN You can enter at every layer — the skill directly, one agent, a full /ui-review fan-out, and a just one-liner — and parallel QA produces per-story reports in ai_review/.
6

nano-agent

The only repo where you build the agent itself.

Loop, tools, provider abstraction — then expose it through MCP and evaluate models scientifically. Rebuilding it demystifies what Claude Code is under the hood: a tool-call loop, a provider interface, and a surface to invoke it from. The initial 🚀 contains the system; you decompose it into seven milestones.

nano-agent · the eval machinery
nano-cli run "<prompt>" --model gpt-5-mini --provider openai

# HOP: .claude/commands/perf/hop_evaluate_nano_agents.md
# LOP: lop_eval_1__dummy_test.md … 5__complex_engineering_test.md
# per-model wrappers in .claude/agents/nano-agent-*.md
# graded results committed as eval_results_*.md

  1. M1 — Agent core

    Typed file-system tools plus an OpenAI-SDK agent loop in apps/nano_agent_mcp_server. The tool-call loop all agents share.

  2. M2 — Provider abstraction

    Route gpt-5*, claude-*, and gpt-oss:* (Ollama) through one interface. OpenAI-compat as lingua franca; local models first-class.

  3. M3 — CLI

    nano-cli run with --model and --provider, plus test-tools — test the agent without any MCP client.

  4. M4 — MCP server

    Expose prompt_nano_agent via MCP with .mcp.json.sample and uv tool install. Agents-as-MCP-tools; delegation from Claude Code.

  5. M5 — Per-model sub-agent wrappers

    Nine .claude/agents/nano-agent-*.md files, all calling the same MCP tool with a pinned model. Fan-out parallelism via sub-agents.

  6. M6 — LOP evals

    Five graded eval prompts, from dummy test to complex engineering test, each with expected outputs and rubrics. Evaluations written as prompts.

  7. M7 — HOP + results

    hop_evaluate_nano_agents.md runs all agents in parallel on a LOP and grades performance / speed / cost; commit the eval_results. The trifecta — and cheap models often win.

DONE WHEN One HOP invocation runs ≥3 models in parallel on the same task and produces a graded comparison table with token counts, wall-clock, and cost — including a $0.00 local-model row.
7

multi-agent-observability

Takes the hooks stack and turns it into a product.

Python hooks → Bun/SQLite server → WebSocket → Vue client: the single best full-stack exercise in the catalog, and the direct ancestor of pi-agent-observability. Its 16 commits track two eras — visualization polish in 2025, then the human-in-the-loop and team era.

…hooks-multi-agent-observability · git log, oldest first
🚀                                    # 2025-07-10 pipeline end-to-end
progress ×6                           # 2025-07-13 visualization polish
add UserPromptSubmit                  # 2025-07-18
QOL Improvements                      # 2025-10-10
HIL + Haiku 4.5 Enhancements + Swim Lanes   # 2025-10-19
Add multi-agent observability system with
  hooks, agents, and skills…          # 2025-10-26
update for task/team tools & new hooks      # 2026-02-08

  1. M1 — Event pipeline end-to-end (🚀)

    Hooks + send_event.py POSTing with --source-app and --event-type; a Bun server with SQLite insert + WebSocket broadcast; a minimal Vue client with live event rows; apps/demo-cc-agent to generate traffic. Hooks as telemetry; source_app:session_id as agent identity.

  2. M2 — Visualization polish (progress ×6)

    Filtering, emoji per event type, pulse charts, README architecture diagrams. Making event streams legible.

  3. M3 — Prompt observability (add UserPromptSubmit)

    A new hook plus client rendering of prompts, and a /start command to boot the system — extending a live schema across all four layers.

  4. M4 — Session lifecycle + SDK docs (QOL Improvements)

    session_start, session_end, pre_compact hooks; swap in the agent-SDK ai_docs. Keeping an agentic repo current as the platform evolves.

  5. M5 — HIL + swim lanes (HIL + Haiku 4.5 Enhancements + Swim Lanes)

    Human-in-the-loop example hooks, Haiku-4.5 summarization inside send_event.py, and a swim-lane view comparing agents. Pause agents for human decisions; summarize with a cheap model at the edge.

  6. M6 — Agentic-layer maturity

    Meta-agent, scout-report-suggest agents, worktree commands and skills, output styles, client theming — the repo eating its own dogfood, agents maintaining the observability repo.

  7. M7 — Task/team era (update for task/team tools & new hooks)

    Team builder/validator agents, plan_w_team, permission_request and post_tool_use_failure events flowing to the dashboard. Observing teams, not just sessions.

DONE WHEN Two projects with different --source-app values stream simultaneously into one dashboard; events arrive summarized; swim lanes show a team run; and killing the server never breaks the agent — hooks fail silent.
8

agentic-finance-review

The capstone of the trust theme: specialized self-validating agents.

Hooks live inside the agent definitions — not in global settings.json — and every stage is checked by a deterministic Python validator. It composes nearly everything above (commands + subagents + hooks + orchestration) into an autonomous pipeline over bank CSVs with a checkable output. The formula, verbatim: Focused Agent + Specialized Validation = Trusted Automation.

Validation rides with the agent. Each stage agent embeds its own hook; each hook runs a deterministic script.

  1. M1 — Mock data + one validated stage

    mock-input-data/raw_*.csv and normalize-csv-agent.md with an embedded PostToolUse hook running csv-single-validator.py. Agent-scoped hooks — validation as part of the agent, not the harness.

  2. M2 — Stage agents

    categorize-csv-agent, merge-accounts-agent, csv-edit-agent — each with matching validators. One agent = one job = one validator.

  3. M3 — Visualization stages

    graph-agent generating eight plots with graph-validator.py; generative-ui-agent producing index.html dashboards with html-validator.py; Stop-hook validation (ruff / ty) on /build. Validating generated UI and code deterministically.

  4. M4 — Orchestrator

    /review-finances chains normalize → categorize → merge → accumulate → graph → dashboard over ROOT_OPERATIONS_DIR, with monthly and year-to-date outputs. Autonomy earned through verification.

DONE WHEN Dropping a new month of raw CSVs and running one command produces normalized and merged CSVs, eight graphs, and monthly + yearly dashboards — every stage's validator green, and any failure loudly halting the pipeline.
AFTER THE EIGHT Three honorable mentions: the-verifier-agent if you use Pi (verification moved into a dedicated observer agent); the security pair damage-control + bash-damage-from-within (build the guardrails, then attack them with the 5-level prompt catalog); and agent-experts — rebuild one codebase expert for a repo you own once #2 and #8 are done.
Next · The 12-Week Program  →
28 — The Training Program
every workout in this book, sequenced

Twelve weeks to think like Dan.

Eighteen course workouts, the best repo rebuilds, and the sharpest video drills — arranged as a progressive overload plan. One rep every two to three days, always on a real codebase.

4 phases18 workouts6 repo rebuildsgraduation criteria
In this chapter 1The Rules 2Phase 1 · Foundations 3Phase 2 · Systems 4Phase 3 · Custom Agents 5Phase 4 · Orchestration 6Graduation
1

The Rules of the Dojo

Six rules, no exceptions.

Train on a real codebase. Not a toy — yours. Every workout below names the chapter that explains it; re-read the chapter, then do the rep. Keep a training log in your repo (a running markdown file counts) and track Dan's four KPIs weekly: Size ↑, Attempts ↓, Streak ↑, Presence ↓.

Spend tokens to save time. Optimizing your API bill instead of your hours is, in Dan's words, a failure of imagination. And every morning, the meta-question: "Am I working on the agentic layer, or the application layer?"

Four phases, rising load. Foundations → systems → custom agents → orchestration and trust.
2

Phase 1 · Weeks 1–3 — Foundations

  1. Week 1 · Feel the difference (ch02, ch03)

    TAC-1 workout: run an AI coding prompt and an agentic prompt side by side, then run an agent programmatically. TAC-2 workout: make one real codebase legible to agents — stdout, types, and a /prime command — and verify with a one-shot fix.

  2. Week 2 · Master your context window (ch10)

    AH-1 workout: cut your Claude Code boot context by more than 50%, replace always-on memory files with priming, and delegate one job to a background context window. Reduce & Delegate until it's reflex.

  3. Week 3 · First pipeline, first rebuild (ch04, ch27)

    TAC-3 workout: build a chore→plan→implement pipeline from templates and ship one chore with exactly two prompts. Repo gym: rebuild agentic-drop-zones — one file-watcher plus a drops config is your first taste of agentic infrastructure.

PHASE 1 DONE-WHENYour codebase primes in one command, your context boot is half its old size, and one chore shipped with two prompts and zero manual edits.
3

Phase 2 · Weeks 4–6 — Systems

  1. Week 4 · The prompt ladder and going AFK (ch11, ch05)

    AH-2 workout: build a seven-level prompt ladder in one repo — one prompt per level, all sharing the same section format. TAC-4 workout: ship one GitHub issue to PR with zero keyboard time between prompt and review.

  2. Week 5 · Close the loops, see everything (ch06, ch27)

    TAC-5 workout: convert your manual verification ritual into closed loops an agent runs, including one browser-level e2e test. Repo gym: rebuild the hooks-mastery meta-agent, then the multi-agent observability stack — hooks → server → SQLite → live dashboard.

  3. Week 6 · Focus and armor (ch07, ch22)

    TAC-6 workout: add review and document agents to your pipeline, with image proof and conditional docs. Then install damage control on every production repo and run the bash-security drill — attack your own guardrails.

PHASE 2 DONE-WHENAn issue became a PR while you were away, your agents validate their own work in a loop you can watch on a dashboard, and destructive commands bounce off your hooks.
4

Phase 3 · Weeks 7–9 — Custom Agents & Experts

  1. Week 7 · Parallelize, then drop the R (ch08, ch22)

    TAC-7 workout: run two ADWs in parallel via git worktrees, then take one trivial problem class to zero-touch with auto-merge. Video drill: the zero-to-skill build — author one real skill from a blank directory, cookbook and all.

  2. Week 8 · Build agents, ditch the middleman (ch12, ch27)

    AH-3 workout: build three custom agents of increasing capability on the Claude Agent SDK — a Pong agent, a tool-wielding Echo agent, and a read-only codebase QA agent with a security hook. Repo gym: rebuild beyond-mcp and measure the same toolset four ways (MCP vs CLI vs scripts vs skill).

  3. Week 9 · Experts and your own layer (ch14, ch09)

    AH-5 workout: stand up one complete agent expert — Act, Learn, Reuse — for a real domain in your codebase, e.g. your database layer. TAC-8 workout: bootstrap a minimum viable agentic layer on your codebase and prove it with one gateway run and one chained ADW.

PHASE 3 DONE-WHENA custom agent you wrote answers questions about your codebase with tools you chose, an expertise file is maintaining itself, and your repo has a living agentic layer directory.
5

Phase 4 · Weeks 10–12 — Orchestration & Trust

  1. Week 10 · One interface, many agents (ch13, ch16)

    AH-4 workout: build a minimum viable O-Agent — a custom agent whose only tools are create/command/status/delete over background instances, with a file-based observability trail. MA-1 workout: build the one-shot board meeting — brief in, parallel opinionated debate, memo out, under a time and budget constraint.

  2. Week 11 · Teams that ship (ch17, ch18)

    MA-2 workout: stand up a three-tier orchestrator→leads→workers team over a real small codebase, with domain locking and per-agent mental models, driven through a single chat entry point. MA-3 workout: turn the team into an N-variant generator producing brand-consistent UI pages in parallel, with validation and till-done tracking.

  3. Week 12 · The verifier, then the grade (ch19, ch15)

    MA-4 workout: run and extend the builder+verifier system — every builder turn decomposed into atomic claims and proven, including one domain verifier you write yourself. AH-6 workout: grade your codebase against the class/grade rubric and climb exactly one grade.

6

Graduation

YOU GRADUATE WHEN Three changes shipped through ADWs with zero keyboard time between prompt and review · one problem class runs at zero-touch · a verifier or reviewer agent gates your builds · an expertise file compounds without your edits · and your four KPIs have moved for three consecutive weeks.
THEN Start over at week 1 — on the next codebase. The program is a loop, not a line. That's the point.
The loop. Train, measure the KPIs, systematize what worked, extend trust — and go again, one level up.

It's not about what you can do anymore. It's about what you can teach your agents to do for you.

— IndyDevDan
Next · The Send-off  →
— The Send-off
Where this leaves you

The tools will change. The principles won't.

You now hold everything Dan teaches. None of it matters until you run the workouts — trade your keyboard time for the agentic layer, one rep at a time.

RECAP 01

Master the Core Four

Context, model, prompt, tools. Every feature, framework, and hype cycle reduces to these.

RECAP 02

Template your engineering

The plan is the prompt. Encode how you build into committable, improvable assets — then fix systems, never issues.

RECAP 03

Build the system that builds the system

Spend 50%+ of your time on the agentic layer, close every loop, and target zero-touch engineering.

We weren't just AI coding anymore. We started orchestrating intelligence.

— IndyDevDan, Tactical Agentic Coding
